CHILDREN BETWEEN ONE AND FIVE.

Sir,—lt will be remembered that a conference was recently held concerning children between the ages of one and five years. A committee was appointed, and it has since reported that it thinks the first step should be to inquire into local conditions. This committee drafted a number of questions, which were approved by the Guild Executive, and they will shortly be issued to representative people. Further details will be given at the annual meeting next Tuesday. Meantime I should like to say that the Guild will be glad to have the co-operation of any people in the town, who can throw light upon present conditions, or who can make any prac- tical suggestions for the welfare of little chil- dren under school age. _

I shall be pleased to forward a set of questions to anyone who can help by sending in replies. The questions may also be considered by groups of people who are specially interested in the child-life of the town. WILFRID ROWLAND. Winmarleigh Chambers.

===== 0002601_19140221_art0015.txt | included=true | included =====
SANKEY BRIDGES ADULT SCHOOL.

Sir,—l have read the reply to my letter by the President of the above School, and as I read it I came to the conclusion that he had mis- taken the meaning of my letter. I did not criticise the Adult School Movement whatever, as I am fully aware of the usefulness of that movement, both in this town and others that I have resided in, in various parts of York- shire.

_ I simply took exception to and criticised the statement made by one of their members. Hav- ing attended several of their Sunday morning meetings in Yorkshire as a speaker .on the tem- perance question, I am fully aware of the full privilege given to all who are present' to discuss . .

thoroughly the subject brought before their notice, and I only, wish that other societies and denominatidns wefuld show the same activity in this work of discussion on all important topics of the present day. In respect to the challenge I made, it could be tested fairly enough by plac- ing two men equal in all respects to each other in health and strength at the commencement of the task (they could be medically examined before they began the task and as soon as they

had finished), -and in respect to the statement I criticised let me ask this question : Is there any experienced doctor who will advocate strong drink for building up the sinews and muscles of the body, or do they advocate it as a stimulant only The answer to this question should prove whether ale will keep a man fit for work, when as the person who made the statement said that tea, coffee and cocoa had been tried but failed. In regard to the invitation 'he publicly gives to me, through your columns, I accept it will- ingly, for if I can be of service to anyone, either their members or members of any other place, then my services are at their disposal, and as my address is at the bottom of this letter, their secretary can send me an open date, as I am prepared at any time and all times to defend the cause of total abstinence. " J. W. BRADT 12, Gandy's-terrace, Warrington.

J. W. BRADLEY.

===== 0002601_19140221_art0016.txt | included=true | included =====
FREEDOM FELLOWSHIP DISCUSSION.

Sir,—l note that the Chairman of the Freedom Fellowship denies the charge entered in my pro- test against omitted discussion at their meet- ing. An unanticipated bald denial gave me pause with surprise. Please allow me to state details. The Chairman, in his introduction of Mr. Bowman, promised time for questions and discussion. At the close of the lecture the Chairman announced his willingness to accept questions, to which Mr. Bowman would reply en bloc. The Chairman hints that my knowledge of public meetings should have taught me the ease with which question time gives way to discus- sion time. My experience of public meetings and their procedure has taught me that ques- tions and discussion are two distinct items, and I have yet to learn the ease with which invited questions give way to uninvited discussion. To the contrary, I have noted with what ease and alacrity -chairmen have ruled w.ould-be con- founders of the two out of order, subsequently closing question time to introduce discussion. The Chairman in this instance did not invite discussion, although I, perhaps others, waited patiently for the announcement. I did not ex- pect presidential indulgence, nor do I crave it, but a formal introduction of discussion will give opponents security to proceed, without thought of impending remonstrance.

I thank the Chairman for his cordial invita- tion. Let me assure him that ray protest was not inspired by either bias or partiality, and that it never occnyred to me to take a census of the opinion of the audience before making my protest.

, _ GILBERT MANION. 49, Folly-lane, Warrington.

===== 0002601_19140221_art0017.txt | included=true | included =====
PAYMENT FOR SERVICES.

Sir,—Will you find Me a little space to pro- test against the action of our Local Insurance Committee in their extravagant request to the Commissioners for the payment of 7s. per meet- ing? If we cannot have voluntary service in an affair of this kind (which has yet to be proved) then surely we can expect, and ought to get, sufficient enthusiasm among our public-spirited men to do this work without, at any rate, mak- ing money out of it. I see one delegate mentions those members who have to lose Work to attend. Well, no one will complain 'about legitimate expenses being paid; but, sir, it seems to me a vastly different thing when a sum like 7s. for every member attending is proposed. "We are yet a long way off the consummation of all the good things promised under the Insurance scheme, and one has to confess that if everybody is actuated by the same motives as those by which our local representatives seem to be, they will about arrive with the millenium. G. SHAW. 11, St. Barnabas'-place.

===== 0002601_19140221_art0018.txt | included=true | included =====
"THE SHANE OF ENGLAND."

Sir,—We write from Antwerp, where we have come for the special purpose of making personal inquiries as to the facts relating to the Con- tinental traffic in old English horses. We admit, frankly and thankfully, that great improvements in the class of horses exported, the means of transit, and the supervision and inspection of the animals have undoubtedly taken place, and yet we are fully convinced that nothing short of the absolute prohibition of the export of old decrepit or infirm horses alive can satisfy the public conscience or be consistent With the dictates of hUmanity. . We- are satisfied that under the best condi- tions practically attainable such animals must suffer greatly during' the sea voyage in rough and cold weather, and after 'arrival in this and other Belgian ports the supervision ceases when the poor creatures have left the city precincts , for the interior, and we know of our own know- ledge that the method of handling and killing such animals is revolting in the extreme. -1

In order to avoid inflicting upon your rpaders a long letter, may we invite those who are in- terested in this subject to apply to the Hon. Secretary of the Horses and Drivers Aid Com- mittee, 83a, Chester-square, S.W., for a leaflet containing the most recent reports of its volun- tary inspectorate respecting what is known here by the natives as "la honte de l'Angleterre"— " the shame of England." PERCY CAREW ESSEX.

.. ----- CHARLES REINHARDT, M.D. Queen's Hotel, Antwerp, February 9, 1914.

KITCHEN ECONOMY.

The approach of Lent reminds us of Shrove Tuesday, or, as it .is more familiarly known—

especially amongst the youngsters—Pancake Tuesday, and the thrifty housewife finds her- self regretting that this feast was not arranged for a time when eggs were not so dear. For, with the increased cost of living, it needs all her care to make ends meet, and yet provide those little luxuries in the home which give zest to life—more particularly to the younger mem- bers of the family. Happily, dear eggs need not deter the housewife from giving her family their full share of the Shrove Tuesday delicacy. With Bird's Egg Substitute (Powder) she can make the most deliciously light and rich pan- cakes without having recourse to eggs. But the use of Bird's Egg Substitute is not confined to pancakes. It will take the place of eggs in cakes, Yorkshire pudding, &c., and, being abso- lutely pure, ensures perfect results. Recipes are given with each packet, or our readers may obtain a copy of "Pastry and sweets," which contains a large number of excellent recipes, by sending a postcard to Alfred Bird and Sons, Ltd., Birmingham.

===== 0002601_19140221_art0020.txt | included=true | included =====
PANCAKE RECIPE.

Half a pound of flour, one teaspoonful of Borwick's Baking Powder, and a little salt; mix well in a dry state, add one egg and three- quarters of a pint of milk. Fry quickly over a brisk fire and serve immediately. Pancakes made according to these directions will turn out beautifully light and appetising.

IN THE COURTS.

RAILWAYMAN'S THEFTS. Series Spread Oyer Three Years.

At the Warrington Borough Court on Tues- day, Thomas Kennedy, a railway employe, of 85, Oldham-street, was charged on remand with, during the - last 12 Mouths, stealing from the London and North-Western Railway. Company a lady's raincoat, lady's nightdress, lady's shawl, a silk blouse, a pair of corsets, table centre, a costume length, and a carriage window strap, value in all ..t4 Se. 6d., the property of the railway company. When the case was originally before the magistrates it was stated that following an un- successful application by prisoner's wife, she gave information which led to the charges of stealing_ being brought against her husband.

- 0 Mr. Eady, solicitor, Euston, prosecuted. 1 Prisoner had been employed by the London and North-Westerniiwlaesss anacgdteipassenger uaaoccupiedr da. E. a positionsiol wn passenger aty heC dao gams uy or goodsnrif yhai nsspdi porter slt and 9Oh3e. re- ceiving 30s. or a little more a week. On Mon- day, February 9th, in consequence of informa- tion which was. given to the police, inquiries were made; and the property which formed the subject of the charge was recovered from a num- ber of houses in the town. The articles had been given to people by the prisoner or his wife. In consequence of those facts prisoner was arrested,- and on, being cautioned he at first denied all knowledge of the goods. but he after- wards admitted having stolen the property dur- ing the past three years, and although there was only, one charge during the past twelve months their Worships would appreciate the seriousness of the charge, tind also the difficulty the com- pany had with these cases, for prisoner was a man in a position of trust. Certain of the articles were in his charge during his journeys to Wigan and other places up and down the line.

Detective-Sergeant Reddington detailed the recovery of the property. Wheis prisoner was formally charged he said it was perfectly true. He was sorry he had done it _ _ _

The prisoner asked for another chance, be- cause of his wife and family. He was sent tie prison for six months, with hard labour.

FOOTBALL COUPONS.

A Peculiar Charge Alleged.

An interesting football coupon case came before the Mayor (in the chair) and other mag- istrates at the Borough Court on Wednesday, when Richard P. Walmsley, carrying on a dentist business at Arpley-street, Warrington, and Walter Eastham, tanner, 1, Robson-street, Warrington, were charged with attempting to obtain ..V.3 lls, by false Pretences from Walter Page, 323, Liverpool-road, Warrington. Mr. T. S. Steel prosecuted and said that Page carried on a football coupon competition. Per- sons were invited to forecast on printed forms the results of football matches, and to enclose a postal order to cover the amount, of money they wished to speculate. The coupons and postal order had to be received by the prosecutor before the matches were played, and the en- velope had to bear a certain delivery postmark. It was alleged that in order to get over the Postal difficulty, a letter addressed in pencil was delivered at defendants' house, and having ob- tained the results of certain matches the defend- ants re-addressed the envelope and enclosed coupons and postal order to the prosecutor. The coupons, it was alleged, were marked with some of the results of that day's matches. The letter was then taken to the residence of the prosecutor and deposited in the letter-box in order to make him believe it had been delivered by the postman on his round. As it would be impossible to complete the case that day, 4r. Steel. applied for a Week's adjournment. .

_ Evidence of arrest was given by Supt. Wil- liamson.

_ Walmsley said the charge was simply ridicu- lous, and he had had nothing to do with it. He aPPlie,d for bail: Eastham alleged that the police had some ani- mosity against him, as he had had six months nearly three years ago. The magistrates adjourned the' case for a week, bail being allowed, Walmsley in two sure- ties of £5O each and himself in £100; Eastham two sureties of £25 each and himself in £5O.

BOROUGH POLICE COURT.

MONDAY. Before the Mayor (in the chair), Aldermen Smethurst, Tinnion, James Evans and Bolton, Councillor Wilkinson, and Messrs. W. Roberts and G. Pilling. Apprentice. Boy's Adventure.—,llugh Peter Furness, baker and oonfectioner, 17, White - street, was charged with assaulting Leonard Burns and stealing from his person a bag con- taining ..t2O in cash, the property of John Bow- den.—The Chief Constable said that on Saturday morning a 14-year-old boy named Leonard Burns, an apprentice to Mr. John Bowden, of 105, Longford-street, had been to Parr's Bank, and had in his possession a bag containing .tlB in silver and £2 in copper. As he was in a passage at the rear of Orford-lane he was attacked from behind and received blows about the head and felt someone tugging at the bag. The boy held on to the bag and subsequently he received four further blows, and prisoner was then alleged to have taken the bag and ran away with it. The boy gave chase and a number of people rushed after the prisoner. He was caught, and whilst he was being held by two men one of them heard a thud, and on looking to the ground saw a piece of iron, which had a piece of paper tied round one end. It was suggested that it was with that iron that the boy was struck. The boy saw the prisoner deliberately drop the iron. The man was handed over to P.C. Green, and when at the Police Station, in answer to the charge, prisoner said that the boy Pushed against him and injured his hand. He only took the bag away to frighten the boy. He (the Chief Constable) suggested that prisoner should be mentally examined, and for that purpose the case should be remanded for a week, so that an observation could be made in Manchester.—Mr. T. S. Steel, who appeared to defend, said that prisoner had been ill for some time. He agreed to the police application.—The magistrates concurred, and the accused was remanded for a week.

• Gaming on Fair Ground.—John Littlewood, travelling showman, giving an address at 89, , Bewsey-street, was charged with gaming on t4e Fair Ground on Saturday night.—The,evidenCe of Detective-Sergeants Reddington and Holland showed that on Saturday night prisoner had a stand on the Fair Ground, where he had a revolving board with numbered divisions. Eight tickets, with numbers corresponding with those OR the board, were sold for a penny each, and the winner was given a prize of sixpence, leav- ing a profit of 2d. for the prisoner.. The winner was ascertained b the throwing of a dart at the board whilst it was revolving, and it strik- ing one of the numbered divisions. Prisoner said he was on the Pair Ground the previous Saturday, and the Chief Constable said he hoped the Bench would take that into account. There was no end of money coming to bail him out. . This practice of robbing .young people should be stopped.—Prisoner was fined .R. 5 and costs, and the instruments were ordered to be forfeited.

Family Trouble.—margaret Brady, 83, Liver- pool-road, was summoned for assaulting May Jefferson, 5, Wellfield-street.—During the evi- dence for the prosecution defendant had a faint- ing tit and had to be removed from Court.—The evidence showed that complainant went to the house of her aunt, Mrs. Brady, with regard to certain talk that had been going on about her and her sweetheart. Words passed between the parties and Mrs. Brady was alleged to have seriously assaulted complainant by striking her.—Mr. A. Browne, who prosecuted, asked that the alleged " talk " should be stopped, and Mr. T. S. Steel, who defended, admitted a tech- nical assault.—The Bench dismissed the case on defendant apologising, paying the costs and promising not to repeat the statements. TUESDAY. 411 P

_ Before Messrs. 11. Roberts (in the chair), C. Jackson Holmes, T. Locker and Colonel Edelsten.

Drunk in Charge of a Horse and Cart.— Thomas Arnold, of 9.1, Greenall's-avenue, was fined 20s. and costs, or a month, for being drunk in charge of a horse and cart. Prisoner had

been involved in a collision between his cart and a tramcar in which a window was broken. He pleaded guilty. A Footpath Ohstruction.—John Thomas Bub lock, 13, Church-street, was summoned for ob- structing the footpath near his premises by two hampers of cabbage and one hamper of potatoes. The evidence showed that the hampers were On the footpath for an. unreasonable time. It was promised that the offence would not be repeated and the case was dismissed on payment of costs THURSDAY. Before Mr. J. Rymer Young (in the chair), _

Mr- W. Roberts and Dr. Naden.

For a Pigeon Cot;.—Elias Robinson, 14, Dal- ton-hank, was charged with stealing a, quantity of roof felting and iron sheeting, the property Rylands Bros.—Mr. Steel appeared for the defendant and pleaded not guilty.—The Y Chief Constable said the prisoner, had been em- ployed b a local firm of carting contractors, and was responsible for the stables, and was going to use the goods for a pigeon cote. The felting was found in a manhole by Mr. Wads,' worth,-the manager, and the iron was buried in the ground. Evidence having been given by several witnesses, Mr. Steel said his client stoutly denied the charge, and suggested that probably someone who had a spite against the man had put the goods in the cate.—A fine of .t 5 ss. was imposed, with an alternative of two months' imprisonment.

_ Picked the Handbag.—Ellen Faulkner and Elizabeth Smith, hailing from Manchester, were charged with stealing a purse containing 3s. 9d. from the person. of Lucy Woolley, Appleton, near Widnes, and also with loitering in the Market-place with intent to commit a: felony.-- Miss Woolley was looking at a stall in the Market, when she missed her purse from a hand- bag. A scene was created, and P.C. Turner came up. Whilst he was getting information from Miss Woolley, a woman in the crowd shouted "She has put the purse back in the bag," and it was found that the purse was there, and that Faulkner was standing near Miss Woolley.—Faulkner had a very bad record, hav- ing served two terms of five years each and several short periods. Smith had also a record. —Both women were sent to prison for three months.

Help for the Poor-box.—Josiah Aspinall, 6, Leigh-street, West Houghton, Bolton, was sum= moned for having driven a motor car at a speed dangerous to the public at Bridge Foot.—P.C. Corbett said he put his hand up for defendant to stop, but he came on, and he (the officer) in stepping out of the way was caught by the inudguard.—Air. T. S. Steel represented the de- fendant, and asked for a lenient view to be taken of the case.--Defendant was ordered to pay the costs and .4 is. into the poor-box.

POULTRY PEN.

Hints and Interesting Facts and Chat for Amateurs. 1.1014•14444.14.1014.14

"EXAMINER" SPECIAL. The hatching season is commencing. Some 7314 Y think this period too early, yet just as the 14tiblY bird gets the first worm so does the early wlnlblel.ests lay the first eggs, and that at a period ",eggs are becoming scarce and prices going l'arlY chickens for table purposes also corn- goon prices, and so the early birds are PMfitable. „,,14117 amateurs, of course, have not the ac- irlleodatiou for early setting; if they have it d 4°W about the time to begin. First select a rY' Bhelterei nesting place; keep as fa i away in. Possible from a draught, and as soon as a sh°t*lY hen is available get ready. If a hen cnrs a disinclination to leave the nest and 8,.110(s when handled, it is a sure indication that " i 8 in the mood for hatching.

SELECTING THE EGGS. Great care should be displayed in selecting the eggs. If these have to be purchased be :;Ire You deal with a respectable firm, otherwise the results may be exceedingly poor. There are dealers and dealers, and one has to be guarded whence come the eggs for setting par- 12°8es. Those who can select the eggs either from amongst their own stock or from that of friends will possibly have the best results. A F"lte guide is to choose only eggs of normal size aed shape. , Ilis-shaped or extreruly thin-shelled eggs Should be avoided, and now I speak more narti- cularly of fowls, although the same hint applies to ducks and other types of birds. It is a mis- 'like to think that large eggs give the better results, most expelts are ,agreed that such accrues from those of medium-sized, well-formed eggs. The fresher they are the better, but they Can be kept a week without damaging the fertility.. Longer keeping leads to uncertain results.

THE FERTILITY QUESTION. 10 tell to a certainty whether an egg is fertile. unfertile is an impossibility. There are tZetruments advertised for this purpose, but never justify the things claimed for them. I„sBlthess is the first thing desired. Some .111, believe that not only can they tell the oft.tiiittY, but can fix the sex through the shiail:ee eoelxol3—pointed eggs are claimed to ,proc o i n l'elian,!4; rounded eggs .pullets. 1 lacP lejlieCe on this theory. At this time mhens,o Dointe,,hare just started to lay, present re 71; ", eggs than later. copcs '8 an unexplained freak of nature. More 1.-o.erejt, emerge from early settings than from R7,,er °nes, ;me titer freak of nature, and this has rise -tri-The pointed egg theory. Another i,urett-.18 that the position of the air cell at the °atler end of the egg indicates the sex. This. 18 another of the popular .delusions. .Much "epends on what one might call luck, but if a breed has been selected it is almost certain '8) to gs Per cent, of the eggs will be fertile.

THE SITTING HEN.

The lien which is to do the hatching is worthy Of a little more attention than is usually be- :,tawell• Before placing her into the nest give the latter a good dusting with insect powder Which will keep the sitter free from vermin Pests. The eggs should be placed one or two at a time in the nest, just in front of the hen, Su that she can see them. Then she will tuck them carefully beneath her with her beak. She should not be enticed to leave the eggs, ten in Illunber being considered a fair setting, during the first 24 hours. If she does not come off the second day to feed she must be gently lifted out of the nest, and great care must be taken not to disturb the eggs. Ten to twenty minutes, according to the weather, is long enough for her to get a good Meal and a stretch. If she does not return of her own accord she should be induced to do 90. The best food for a sitting lien is maize, barley or wheat, and be sure there is always a supply Of fresh water. Some dry ashes or sand, mixed With some insect powder, should be kept near the nest, so that the hen can have a dust bath before returning to the nest after her meal. The Period of incubation lasts about 21 days and should never exceed that time.

DUCKS AND GEESE. 1.;r1;e above directions apply chiefly to fowls. oftll49a are, however, largely hatched by the aid as the duck is a notoriously poor sitter. A large hen will cover six goose eggs, but no more should be allowed her. The incubation period is between 28 and 30 days. Every few days the eggs should be sprinkled with water, which has had the chill just taken off it. Sitting usually commences at the end of February. Turkey hens are also good sitters and they have been known to hatch 14 eggs. It it wisest to let the hen sit upon her own eggs, wlifch she commences to lay in February. The period of hatching extends to 29 or 30 days. EGG-LAYING COMPETITIONS.

The third month's report on the competition at Sedlescombe shows a still. further improve: ment in the output of eggs. The total for the four weeks commencing sth December and \ end- ing Ist January is 3,423 eggs, as against 2,262 for the previous month; this gives an average of 13 eggs and over per pen for the 28 days. Such an average from so large a flock as 2.56 birds in the depth of winter, including a time of wet and muggy days as well as days of frost and snow, must make many a poultry keeper, who gets an egg or two now and again from his birds, Very envious, but it shows what can be done by , careful breeding, good housing, and scientific feeding. The average per bird in the small house and run section alone is even better, for this works out at 15 eggs per bird for the 28 days. The highest individual score is that by a White Wyandotte pullet, which laid 27 eggs in 28 days. The leading pens to date in the two sections are Section I. Buff Books, with a score of 202 eggs (value n us. Rd.), and Section 11. White Wyandottes, 212 eggs (value .E 1 lie. na.). The leading feature in the second month's report oft the Harper-Adams competition, in which fifty pens of six birds each are competing, is the excellent average number of eggs laid by the birds. For the period of 28 days from the 23rd Novemher to 20th December 3,379 eggs have been laid by the 300 birds, giving an average of 11.26 eggs per pullet. Of the 300 birds, hew- ever, N had not laid during the month, so the average of those which had commenced to lay was 16.56 eggs per, pullet. No fewer than 86 birds laid twenty or more eggs during the 28 days. Pen No. 18, White Wyandottes, still hold the leading position with a total to date of 243 eggs (value ..t1 18s. 31d.), of these 99 only. were laid during the second month, two of the birds being broody.

THE "COMING FLOWER." A leading nurseryman, on being asked his opinion on the coming flower, unhesitatingly averred that the Antirrhinum, or Snapdragon, will be the most popular flower in a few years' of course, referred to plants time. The question, raised annually. Wonderful improvements have been made by the hybridists in recent years, which has placed Antirrhinums in the front rank as border and bedding plants. There is a', vast difference between the dull, intermediate colours of the older forms and the pure, bright varieties now available. At the Royal Horti- cultUral Show held a couple of weeks ago, a firm staged a number of blooms of a winter- flowering Antirrhinum, named Nelrose, the first of a new race from America. The flowers were of a charming shade of creamy pink, and in every way as good as those grown in the sum- mer; while the deep green foliage was sugges- tive of the Wallflower. By striking cuttings in July, growing them on steadily, bringing them into the greenhouse later with a gentle heat, it is induced to flower profusely during the winter.

SOME HARDY CLIMBERS. A free-growing and rapid evergreen climber; bearing racemes of purple flowers, is Bridgesia spicata, or, as Wits in some parts called, Ercille spicata. It is self-clinging, and will grow in any ordinary soil. Another excellent climber is the Dutchman's Pipe (Aristolochia Siplio), which has striking foliage. It is very hardy, and is suitable for the sunless side of an arch or pergola. The Fiery Thorn (Crattegus pyracantha Lelandi) is most conspicuous in winter, having a profusion of brilliant scarlet berries. In the spring it is covered with small creamy-white blossoms. This does well in any soil, and is suited for a north aspect. The Jasmines, Ivy, Ampelopsis varieties, and the various Clematis are well-known without - special reference.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, FEBRUARY 21, 1914.

FARM AND GARDEN.

SWINE FEVER TREATMENT.

Mr. Runciman, answering questions in the House of Commons as to the action taken and intended to be taken by the Board of Agricul- ture to control swine fever, regarding thecheck- ing of which slow progress had been made, said swine fever had not been more than a minor contributory cause of the scarcity of pigs and the increased price of bacon. There had been a reduction of nearly a million and threequarter hundredweight in the amount of pig meat im- ported in 1912 as compared with 1908, a fact which he quoted to support his contention, lie was not satisfied with the state of things and would readily adopt new measures if they were likely to prove effective. The Second Interim Report of the Departmen- tal Committee on Swine Fever, published last week, deals with the •results of investigations made in Holland, Hungary and the United States. "No satisfactory evidence," says the report, "was obtained to show that effective im- munisation by artificial methods can safely be employed except in conjunction with isolation and restriction on movement,"—a finding which does not hold out much hope of assistance from serum treatment.

During the week ended February 7 there were 61 outbreaks of swine fever, involving the slaughter of 669 animals. Two of the outbreaks were in Scotland, one in Wales, and the re- mainder in 22 English counties.

AN EARLY SEEDING SEASON.

Handsome Return from Potatoes.

The planting of corn and other spring crops is not general, but a beginning has been made in several districts, chiefly in the southern and eastern counties, and in most localities every- thing is in readiness for an early start. The weather last week was less settled, but field operations suffered little interruption, farmers being able to proceed with whatever kind of work they deemed most appropriate to the time of year.

The modern tendency is to sow spring crops, particularly barley and other cereals, earlier than was the custom a generation ago, but few enter with enthusiasm into the work before the middle of February. The dangers of undue haste are not overlooked, although all farmers are not equally apprehensive of their serious- ness. The risks, however, are not all on the side of precipitate action; delay may be equally prolific of disappointment and loss. The ex- periences of recent years emphasise the wisdom of seizing every opportunity February offers for the drilling of corn and the planting of beans, peas, and early potatoes., The growing of potatoes for the summer mar- kets is extending. The business is speculative, but the possibility of failure is balanced by the prospects of handsome returns, and the way in which the suitable localities are singled out for the purpose is high testimony to the intelligence and readiness of farmers in making the most of their opportunities. Planting has been in pro- gress in the south for some time past, and last week a start was made in the Lothians as well as in Ayrshire and County Dublin.

===== 0002601_19140221_art0038.txt | included=true | included =====
CHESHIRE FARMERS AND THE LAND INQUIRY.

The Minimum Wage and Rural Houses. A general meeting of the Nantwich Farmers' Club on Saturday discussed resolutions drafted by the committee for further consideration by the Central Chamber of Agriculture in Lon- don, declaring approval of the payment of the minimum wage to able-bodied agricultural labourers and the support of a scheme of rural housing based on economic rent. These resolu- tions were drafted by a committee of past presi- dents of the club after consideration of the Land Inquiry Committee's report. Mr. C. E. Young (Sheppenhall), in moving the adoption of the resolutions, observed that many farmers in Cheshire already paid a living wage to their labourers, and there was nothing to fear in the adoption by them of the principle of the minimum wage. Mr. S. Jackson (Wistaston) proposed that the approval of the club of the minimum wage be deleted from the minutes. The Land Inquiry Report was a purely party report, instigated by a certain political party. (Hear, hear.) Farmers could pay their labourers without in- terference by legislation or officialism—probably by "gentlemen from Cambridge," who would be the judges as to whether they were paying their men a sufficient wage. (Laughter.) Mr. W. E. Birchall said no one outside a lunatic asylum would suggest that the Cam-' bridge professor would be brought in to con- stitute a wages board. ("Oh." and laughter.) Mr. W. 11. Hobson (Blakerthall) said the Land Inquiry Committee had only published what had suited their purpose. ("No.") Mr. Young, replying, said farmers who could not afford to pay a living wage to their labourers had better "shut up shop " to-morrow. (Hear, hear, and laughter.) The members voted, and by 12 votes to 10 the amendment of Mr. Jackson was carried. A number of members did not vote. The amend- ment declared : "We do not disagree in princi- ple to the proposal to fix wages by some sort of wage tribunal. It must, however, be laid down that any proposals which may be made shall provide that lower wages than the '.minimum may be paid to the partially efficient labourer."

"SYNDICALISM."

The New Movement Described to Adult School Members.

A NEBULOUS REVOLUTION.

"Syndicalism ' was,the subject of a .talk at the Sankey Bridges Men's Adult School on Sunday morning by Mr. David Ridyard. After a brief summary of the Trade Union movement in England and France, the speaker pointed out that the Syndicalist movement itself was prac- tically the same as out- native Trade Union movement. The word "Syndicat" was the French equivalent of Trade Union. In France ' the modern movement had been greatly influ- enced .by the Intellectuals, many of whom are steeped in the Marxian theory of political economy. At a Congress held in Amiens in 1906 the Syndicates were committed to a revolution- ary policy known to-day as Revolutionary Syndicalism. This movement is spreading in England, Italy and America, but not to any alarming extent.

In brief, the theory was this: That society, which depends ultimately upon the proceeds of industry, should be based upon industries only; that government, as at present understood, should cease, and all power be in the hands of the workers in whatever capacity they work. In order to bring this about, Syndicalists hold- ing the view that the political machinery is in the hands of the capitalist class see no use in Labour representation or political action at all, but attach all importance to direct action. Direct action is opposed to representative gov- ernment. The workman is to influence those in authority, not through Parliament, but through the power of association amongst trade unions. All efforts must be devoted to fusing together into one great federation all workers in the country. Federation in each country would be linked up, and industry would then be able to control production, distribution, and exchange.

===== 0002601_19140221_art0042.txt | included=true | included =====
A General Strike.

In the ease of disputes in any trade arising under the present regime a general strike would be called and capital paralysed. Another weapon to be used is "Sabotage," which has been defined as "wilful destruction, or render- ing useless of instruments, &c., with a view to stopping or hampering industry or commerce." According to the Syndicalists it is not necessary for the whole or even the majority of the work- ers to be converted to this doctrine in order to bring about this change in society, a minority conscious of the end aimed at being considered strong enough tq carry the majority with them. ~The best that can be said for such a theory is that it is too nebulous altogether. Even granting that the Revolution was a success, which is doubtful, the constructive policy is unsatisfactory. Without asking for a cut-and- dried plan under the new order, it is not suffi- cient to tell us that the production, dic., will be carried out by sections. Who is to control these sections? Because even assuming a high stand- ard of intelligence amongst workers, some form of government will be essential. This brings us back to political action again. which Syndi- calists oppose. Given a government, nominally under the control of the workers, the danger of officialism is ever present, and will have to be guarded against. Capitalism is, like every other system, a growth. It began in a small way and has grown into something stupendous. What the system which succeeds it will be like we cannot yet say. It is likely that the transition from this system to the next will be like the dawn of day, it is not possible to say when one ends and the other begins. In the meantime workers must take advantage of both political and industrial machinery, doing their best to work out their salvation with the means at hand.

===== 0002601_19140221_art0043.txt | included=true | included =====
BEWSEY MEN'S.

Mr. W. Jackson, a member of the school, gave an interesting account of his impressions of Professor G. Currie Martin's recent lectures on the " Great Religions of the World." As several members of the school had not had the pleasure of attending the lectures, Mr. Jackson's resume was opportune. He said the lectures proved an interesting study in the evolution of Reli- gious Thought. The main idea seemed to demonstrate. the /act of a natural tendency in the human species to seek out some object to reverence and worship, and that throughout the hiStory of the human race, however it may have degenerated at times, religion, or as it may be termed reverence and worship, was paid to a Divine Creator. It behmred us to ap- proach the great non-Christian religions with sympathy and understanding, as they played a great part in ennobling the Eastern peoples, and the higher elements of these religions have been included in Christianity. The speaker said the Professor was careful to point out that the essence of Christianity did not arise out of the birth of Christ, but out of what He taught and did, the life He lived, the sufferings He endured, and what He really was.

===== 0002601_19140221_art0044.txt | included=true | included =====
ACADEMY-STREET MEN'S.

On Sunday morning a lecturette on the "history of the Press" was given by Mr. Tones. He prefaced his remarks by dealing with the original introduction of the newspaper about MK Compared with the printing of the modern daily, the first issue of news to the public was exceedingly sparse. The men of that day had not the facilities for gathering news, the re- porter was non-existent, the telephone and the telegraph were unknown, and news was scanty. As the days passed the ingenuity of man found out a better method of obtaining news. Isaac Pitman brought out his wonderful shorthand system, which was to revolutionise the news- paper. ht the early days of its inception, the people wondered how the speeches of great men could be printed in the papers in so short a time after delivery. The lecturer made refer- ence to an event which happened in the early days of a leading daily paper. The staff were anxious to obtain fuller reports of Parliamen- tary speeches, but as reporters were unknown at this time, the difficulty was great. They were fortunate, however, in securing the services of a gentleman who assisted them greatly. It is said of him that he could sit during the long hours of debate, make a few notes of the most important speeches, and then transcribe them to paper almost word for word as they were uttered. This proved of immense financial benefit to this journal. The speaker then mentioned many of the papers that were pub- lished, but whose existence was short. The advent of the halfpenny daily paper had proved a great boon to the public, for it had been the means of bringing the latest news within the reach of all. The lecturer then described the methods of the production of a daily paper, each minute detail being explained.

===== 0002601_19140221_art0045.txt | included=true | included =====
STOCKTON HEATH MEN'S.

"Art and Religion" was the subject of the lecturette given by Mr. H. 0. Bennett on Sun- day morning. Egyptian religion expressed in buildings and paintings, principally on tombs and mummy cases, was chiefly dwelt upon. No foreign influence seemed apparent in the art. It was almost impossible for us to realise the genius of the Pyramid builders. Each god had a definite set of attributes and colours. The Sphynx, always a puzzle, typified the combina- tion of the human and animal, the physical and the intellectual. Many of their symbols and , designs were still popular with us as ornaments. Our museums, such as Liverpool, were rich in examples of their religious and historic picture writing. Mention was also made of the Lotus ' —emblems of eternal life and other 'symbolism. Christian art was also touched upon. The Bible, lesson on " The scholars' training in loyalty" was well introduced by Mr. R. Lam- pard. A helpful discussion followed.

Polileness is like an air-cushion—there may be nothing solid in it, but it eases the jolts wonderfully.

===== 0002601_19140221_art0046.txt | included=true | included =====
STRANGE CHARACTERS I HAVE NET. '

It was a very hot afternoon in midsummer. I had been tramping since early morning, and at last I was drawing near C.,- Part of the tramp I had enjoyed, for it had been along grass-grown, hawthorn-bordered lanes, and the air had been fragrant with the sweet scent of new-mown bay, but I had to leave the lanes and take to the road, and the road was very dusty and hard. And as I walked along I saw a man sitting by the roadside and I crossed over to ask him how much of the road still lay before Me. He was hatless, with bronzed, handsome bearded face, upon which dissipation had setin- delibly its seal. He was clad in well-cut, well-fitting clothes, but upon them nature had been trying her dyes, making them in part green, in part a rusty brown. He an. swered in a cultured voice. "Why do you walk in the heat of the day? You ought then to lie down by the hedgeside to get some rest and sleep. The night is the best time for walking if you are on the road long."

===== 0002601_19140221_art0047.txt | included=true | included =====
A TRAGIC DOWNFALL.

I sat down beside him in the cooler shade of the hedge, and soon we were deep in con- versation. He was a fascinating talker, well-rcad, with & wide knowledge of the world. And as I sat there listening and marvelling that one so obviously cultured, so obviously a gentleman should be a tramp, to illustrate some point he was trying to make be pulled out of his pocket a well-worn leather-bound volume and proceeded to read a passage from Homer, in the original Greek! For a couple of hours we sat there talking, and then together we went on to C- and to the lodging house at which he had made his home. _ _ -

I sow much of him during the short time I stayed there. He didn't use the common room that I and the rest of the lodgers used. He had a private room, and often I was in it; to keep him company and to enjoy his company during his sober moments; to read his books, when Ile was not sober or when he was sleeping off a debauch. He had but a handful of books, but they were all great books and all were at my service; save one. His Homer never left his person. And one day quite by accident I learned the reason why. IV°, were sitting in front of the fire in his room. I was reading, he sitting staring straight before him, lost in thought, his Homer open upon his knee, looking into the past or into the dread future? I know not what pictures were hanging in the cham- bers of his imagery. But from where I sat I quite involuntarily saw the flyleaf of the open book upon his knee, and on it was written an honoured name, with underneath the single word "Balliol," and a date. .

_ There was a world of tragedy in that simple inscription.

A MEMORABLE SCENE.

He never mentioned his past. The landlord told me that he had been living for some years there, though in summer time he often went on long tramps along the cliffs or over the countryside. And from the landlord I heard a story of a lady who, not long after he went to stay there, drove up in a. carriage, asked for my friend by the name be had assumed, and was shown into his room: to leave an hour later, her drawn veil failing to hide her tear swol- len face Since then his only communication with the outside world was a registered letter that Came every Saturday, containing a bank- note, but no writing of any kind. When I went to his room to bid him good- bye the letter had just been delivered. Ile was sitting at the table as I entered, his head resting on his hands, the letter on the table beside him. He looked up as I entered, and the look of misery in his eyes I shall never forget. And so I ventured to ask him if even now he couldn't break with his past. I shall never forget that last scene. Had I read it in book or magazine / would have dismissed it as theatrical, improbable. His features were twitching as he leaned over me and quoted in a tense voice a passage from one of his favourites—the old Persian Omar Khayyam. - - -

"The moving Finger writes, and having writ, Moves on. Not all your piety nor wit Can lure it back to cancel half a line, Nor all your tears wash out a word of it." "Good God," he said, getting up and pac. jag the floor in his agitation. " What must be written of the past—and of the future ?" " When Circe holds out the cup, I have no saving herb in my palm:" and he went out, to be brought back hours later—b.easti3, drunk. I never saw him again, nor heard of him till I saw in the papers, as probably did most of my readers, the announcement of the death in a common lodging house, of the heir to a baronetcy. Many other strange characters I have met, thoug:h not one interested me as did this man.

===== 0002601_19140221_art0048.txt | included=true | included =====
THE SUPER-TRAMP.

mmembar mecticig in one dosshouse--a foreign-looking tramp with an artificial leg and a pedlar s license—both valuable assets to a member of the begging profession! He was a quiet man, and not much notice was taken of him until we woke up one morning and found our dosshouse famous. lie had tramped and begged in the States, Canada and the Old Country—and is now hailed as England's greatest nature-pcet--W. Davies, the Super-Tramp. On another occasion I spent the night on one of the seats on the Embankment— London's open-air Sanatorium for down-and- eaters. It was bitterly cold, my clothes -were wet, and every bone and muscle in my body ached horribly. And quite suddenly I left the Embankment far behind. I was sitting in the garden at home, enjoy- ing the perfume and the beauty of the sweet- smelling flowers, and watching the uncertain steps of a tiny little toddler as she tried to make her way from the house along the gar- den to "uncle." And she reached me and .ambid tta into my lap; then a policeman:l

strong grip shook me as be saia rougnty, "Now then! this isn't a padding-can!" " The fellow was unnecessarily harsh," said a refined voice from near by, and look- ing up I saw another derelict had taken d. seat on the same form while I slept. He was an old man, with silvery hair and a beard and a most refined—almost beautiful face, When I got to know the old man better I heard his story. He had been an artist, had been twice biting "(Oa the line," and just when, after years of hard work, fame seemed - to be holding out to him the wreath of laurel, disease struck him down, and in the end left him with palsied hands. And so his artist days were over; and he struggled on as well as he could, until his small savings were exhausted, and he sank lower and lower until he was glad to accept the eighteen pence a day for the carrying of sandwich . boarc/K Then through einaining strength he lost that also, and be had to drift, like many another deirelict on to the Embank- ment.

I saw him not long ago happy and com- fortable in a little cottage in the country; for one who interested himself in the case wrote a letter to the newspapers, and elicited such a generous response as made the old man's future secure.

===== 0002601_19140221_art0049.txt | included=true | included =====
IN A COMMON LODGING HOUSE.

In one London lodging-house I met a form- er K.C. or rather Q.C., though there was no- thing strange about his downfall; unbridled license had brought to him a fit punishment. In another dosshouse I met two University men, one an M.A. and formerly assistant master at one of the great public schools. Ho was a ripe scholar, and when talking to me always (ascribed his downfull to ill-health; really he was a drunken verminous wreck.

The other w-as-n-o-IY-poeriieTlie .confessed to having "had the honour of being kicked out of Trinity in my third year." lie had sought tor go.a at Klonayse, tor orchids in Central America; had twice been on Texas ranches, twice in Canada, and in nearly every state in the Union. He is now occupying a position under Government— picking Oakum. I am writing this in a com- mon lodging house. As I sit I can see in the room five other men all of whom could be de- scribed as strange characters I have met. With but one excrption all can speak at least two languages. One is the son of a former Lord Mayor of D—. He had a brilliant career at school and college. Now, cat off by his own people, he is content to sponge on men little better off than himself. , Another was for twenty-three years in the employ of one of the greatest of the cotton firms. He speaks almost as fluently French, German and Spanish, as English. Also a little Russian. He for years travelled the continent for his firm. Drink caused his downfall, but he is ti-ring hard to regain his feet, and next week his friends are sending him to Canada, to give him a chance of be- ginning life over again in a new country. 'Bitting playing chess with him is a man who for twenty years lived in Spain as 3 fruit exporter. His income was a very large one, but be lived tip to it, and two successive bad seasons caused his business to 'smash and him to bolt.

He landed in England with £250. Last night he had to borrow sixpence to pay for his bed. So I might go on writing of many strange characters I have met, but when in a doss- house I meet a man who has fallen through sheer misfortune, and not through a contri- buting fault of his own, that character will be strange to Inc.

CHESHIRE COUNTY SURVEYOR.

The Late Mr. Bull is Succeeded by Mr. W. Holland.

At the last meeting of the Cheshire County Council Col. Legh submitted for adoption the recommendation of the Main Roads Committee that Mr. W. Holland. the assistant county sur- veyor, be appointed to succeed his late chief, Mr. Harry Bull.

Mr. Coard S. Pain seconded, and said Mr. Holland, from his experience and from the able way he had negotiated matters with the Road Board, well deserved the position. The com- mittee's recommendation was unanimous.

The Chairman also paid a tribute to the late Mr. Bull. They were fortunate, he added, to have someone to take his place they all knew so well, and who had done such an enormous amount of good work for the county. He had saved the county .a great deal of money, his in- fluence with the Road Board having enabled them to get amounts that possibly they would not have received through anybody else. The recommendation was carried by a unani- mous vote.

Mr. Holland was called into the chamber, and the Chairman, addressing him, said : I have very much pleasure in telling you that the County Council have appointed you our new surveyor. Deeply as we regret the loss of Mr. Bull, we do feel we have in you an excellent man to take his place. We hope you will live for many years to do the same good work you have done hereto- fore. I congratulate you on your appointment, and I am quite sure we have done the right thing in appointing you. (Applause.) Mr. Holland : May I return my sincere thanks to the Council. I gratefully appreciate this mark of confidence, and I can assure you I will do my best to uphold the dignity of the Coun- cil, and will do my best for you. (Applause.) The salary is X. 550. An assistant county sur- veyor is to be appointed at a commencing salary of .1.350.

SOME COINS AND THEIR NAMES.

What does the word " dollar " come from? It appears to come from the German word thal, which means a valley; and there is an inter- esting little story connected with this. There is a little silviir mining city or district in north- ern Bohemia called Joachimsthal or Joachim's Valley. Once upon a time the reigning duke of that region ordered the silver makers of that district to coin a silver piece, which was ac- cordingly made and called "Joachinithaler." The word "Joachim" was soon dropped, which only left the word " Dialer." This piece of money passed into general use in Germany and Denmark. Then the spelling of it changed to " daler," we borrowed the title (hut not the thing), and from us it was taken over by the people and Government of the 'United States. The florin was first coined in Florence; but it is not certain whether it derives its name from that city or from the Italian word tlorene, which ,means flower. lam inclined to believe that the latter is the most probable, as the coin was stamped with the fleur-de-lis. The Frenchmen have coins which commemor- ate their great kings: there is the " napoleon " and the " louis," equal to the English " crown " —our heaviest and biggest coin. " Franc " means literally the name of the country where it is chiefly used. " Livre." which is the old name for franc, and " lira" are equal to our sovereign; they are the French and Italian and English MUMS for the same thing.

ABOUT id. IN THE Z OF AN INCREASE.

Education in Warrington is to cost during the ensuing year about another penny in the £. This was the statement made at the Education Committee meeting on Monday, and the Coun- cil is to be asked to levy accordingly. A sum of £58,508 is required for the educational work. Receipts are estimated at £31,559, leav- ing £26,949 to be met by the rates. The actual increase is £2,150, which works out at 10. in the £, but through the re-assessing of the borough the assessable value has been increased, and about Id. rate additional will be required. Of the extra £2,150 required, a sum of £1,400 is needed to meet increases in teachers' salaries, but the Committee deem this money well spent. The rating of schools has been in- creased by £3OO. The cost of education per head on the populd- tion at last census is 16s. 41d., and of that sum is. 21d. has to be provided out of the local rates, the difference being made up from Government grants. The budget debate at the meeting of the War- rington Education Committee on Monday even- ing was marked by a few lively episodes, in which Councillor Pemberton played a Con- spicuous part, but the recommendation for a penny increase was carried by an overwhelm- ing majority. Mr. A. J. JOLLEY, chairman of the Finance Sub-committee, intimated that the total ex- penditure would be £58,508. The receipts would be £31,559, leaving a deficiency of £26,949, and to meet that they would ask for .1:26,040 from the borough rate, compared with .1•24,000 year. lie urged the need of hurrying on the erection of the additional schools, otherwise they would have the grants reduced. There had been a tendency for the Board of Education to increase the burden of the local authority, and they had repeatedly protested against that without result. The tendency was still to put the burden on the weaker backs of the rate- payers, instead of upon the broader national taxation. and at present there were no signs of relief for the taxpayers.

The Teachers' Salaries. The increase of .t 1,400 in teachers' salaries might cause some searching of heart, but the in- crease was due purely to the operation of the scale arranged by the Town Council, more espe- cially in regard to the remuneration of some of the older male assistants, the Council feeling that the maximum of some of these should be increased—quite a reasonable and proper thing to do. (Hear, hear.) They had to provide .000 more in respect to increase in rates, but that was like taking the money out of one pocket and putting it into an- other, as it went back to the borough fund. There was 0400 increase in the sinking fund, £2OO for scholarships, and .t. 150 for fuel, &c. There was a new item of £l4O for University Scholarships, which would be welcomed. (Ap- plause.) The net result was a deficiency of £26,949. and the committee recommended that the esti- mates should be approved, and the local author- ity requested to provide £26,000 from the bor- ough fund. lie moved accordingly.

The Actual Increase. The actual increased cost was estimated at £2,150, and the actual increased deficiency at .1.2,084, the last of which fell upon the local rates. That did not seem quite fair. (Coun- (illor PEMBERTON hear, hear.) The portion borne by the rates was equal to more than 11d., but it was not suggested that they should actually ask for that amount. In view of the alterations taking place in the assessing, each penny rate yielded considerably more than it did a few years ago, and allowing for the in- crease in the assessable value, the actual in- crease was not quite 1 1-10 d. in the X.. Each penny yielded £33 more than it did a year ago, and consequently, instead of having to call for lid_ they called for id., and remem- bering that the £2OO increase in rates upon the schools went hack to the borough fund, the actual increase in the rate was id. in the

Not Minimising. He did not want to appear to minimise that it was an additional burden. (Councillor PEM- BERTON : Oh, no!) lie put the facts before them without minimising or exaggerating. The average ratepayer might ask the very pertinent question, what he got from the additional ex- penditure. (Councillor PEMBERTON : Nothing!) He would not hesitate to say they got 20s. in the X- (Councillor PEMBERTON : No, no!) That was a matter of opinion, but he had no hesita- tion in saying that from every branch of the educational work they got the best results, as they got faithful and loyal service from the teachers. (Applause.) The cost of education in Warrington was esti- mated at 16s. 4-Id. per bead of the population at the last census, and of that is. 21d. fell on the local rates. When they compared the expendi- ture in Warrington with other towns on the poundage rate, the burden in Warrington seemed heavier, because Warrington was a low- assessed and low-rented town—compared with Lancashire, it was the lowest assessed town, with the exception of Oldham and Wigan. THE TURMOIL ENDS, The turmoil then came to an end, and Coun- cillor PEMBERTON said when the Education Authority commenced, the Children in attend- ance were 12,802; the attendance to-day being 12,431—actually 300 less, and yet they were faced with all that extra expenditure. DO you call that, he queried, economical ad- ministration? Do you call this committee economists? Do you say you are conducting . this Education Committee on economical lines. It is perfectly untrue, and if you appealed to the ratepayers you would all be chucked out. They would not tolerate you for five minutes. It makes my blood boil to sit listening here to the fairy tales of the Finance Sub-committee. (Laughter.)

Rates Compared. This year they were taking .£948 out of the balance, and last year £865 was taken, and now they were spending money that could he saved. The rate was equal to Is. lOW., yet for nearly all the borough committees the rate was only Is. 71d. When they got all that for Is. 71d.. it WaS lime somebody said something about our education rate of Is. 101 d., hut nobody said a word about it. Did they call that economical administration? If he had his road he would not pass the minute, and he would take strong measures with estimates like that. There was not the slight- est attempt to save, and be had to strongly pro- test against an education rate of Is. 101 d.

BRIDGES OF FRIENDSHIP.

Mr. S. S. Townroe Speaks from Non conformist Pulpit.

"SELF-STYLED BETTER CLASSES''

From our own Correspondent.

Mr. B. S. Townroe is still going to and fro in the Middleton . Parliamentary Division and walking up and down in it as prospective Unionist candidate. Last Saturday he opened a Wesleyan sale of work in the Todmorden dis- trict, and on the following day he gave the weekly address at the P.S.A. in a Baptist Chapel there.

Announcing as his subject "Bridges of Friend- ship," he dealt with the question of labour unrest. Observing that the country to-day is full of strife, suspicion, and uproar, he said : " Groups of men, trusting in organised wealth, or in clamour and agitation, are pursuing worldly ends by worldly methods, utilising our half-barbaric system of politics, and engaging in fierce industrial disputes and rivalry. But deep down below these elements of conflict and unrest there are forces which will have far more power to mould the 'nation's future than any of the more superficial and sensational factors which Mt the columns of the newspapers. The steady, quiet work of churches and chapels, of Cavendish Associations and of Workers' Educa- tional Associations, is providing that change of spirit without which we cannot hope to decrease misery and poverty, or prevent that waste of intellectual and spiritual capacity which is so shameful a blot on our 20th century civilisation. For religious and social work in truth provide to-day bridges of friendship over which one class can pass to another class, and become More united in close sympathy and mutual under- standing. When we have crossed any of these bridges, how different is our outlook, how much broader are our sympathies, how less narrow are our prejudices." Mr. Townroe went on to illustrate this by means of an allegory, show- ing how a youth who, encrusted with the pre- judices of a public school and inheriting the middle-class outlook on life, had his eyes opened and his life changed by first-hand study of the under-world (bridge one), experiences among bluejackets at Chatham (bridge two), and bow a third bridge crossed by the now earnest ex- plorer led him to the kingdom of industry, " among people little understood by the self- styled ' better classes,' and often misrepresented by those whose ' push ' and clever tongues have earned for themselves the name of Labour leaders."

THE THOUGHTS OF THE WORKERS. It was slow reaching the thoughts of the " workers," but in time he found among them lives as heroic as any recorded in the pages of history, having a wide and deep influence on their town and companions in the mills. So he learnt something of the relationship between capital and labour, and came to perceive how that puzzling problem was not only economic and relative to wages and length of hours, but usychological, "for it dealt with the human 9oul, which needs spiritual and human influ- ence, as well as bread and butter and beer." fie saw that the .reorganisation of a good feel- ing between master and man was as much needed in business as the organisation of the production; buying and selling of manufactured articles; and further, that the future of indus- trial unrest lay as much in the hands of em- ployers as of politicians. In the old days rich and poor lived together in close intimacy; but changed conditions had led to the separation of employer and employed, causing between the classes a gulf which could only be spanned by inch bridges as were now suggested. Those who crossed must not go over with any idea of patronage. On each side, spiritual and intellec- tual faculties could he strengthened and lives enriched by a broadening of social sympathies. There were national and municipal objects for which they could , combine, in comradeship. If those who had great opportunities did not do their best to understand with fuller and in- timate knowledge the world in which their lot was cast, the present unrest would increase, and it would probably increase with more serious consequences, the world being a grim one in which thousands go hungry and homeless. But if the whole nation accepted the message which had been given to it in recent years by many- of its leaders, that the duty of each was, to approach in the spirit of friendli- ness nearer to his neighbours, whether they were richer or poorer, then good would come out of the present industrial unrest, and a firm and strong bridge would be built across the widening gulf of class hatred and class preju- dice.

===== 0002601_19140221_art0063.txt | included=true | included =====
ST. HELENS CHILD BURNED TO DEATH.

An inquest was held at St. Helcns on Evelyn Seddon, four years of age, of Springfield-road; Thatto Heath, who died in the hospital on Thursday from the effects of•burns. The mother stated that she was washing, and the boiler fire in the back yard was lit. In her absence the child had evidently played with the fire and thus ignited her _clothing. The boiler fire door was broken in half, and had been for some time.

In answer to the Coroner, witness said she reported the matter to the agent, lrut nothing had been done in the way of repairs. The Foreman of the Jury said there was no law to compel a landlord to put the boiler door right. The Coroner: Oh, yes, there is! You are wrong! Before the Housing and Town Plan- ning Act there was no power to compel the landlord to do it, but now there is that power. The agent ought not to receive rent for a house where the boiler door is half broken off. It was his place to keep it right. They could not expect a poor woman like Mrs. Seddon to pay for a new boiler door. The agent had been told, and the result of not putting On a new door was that this ebild suffered great agony and lost its life. lie was the owner of pro- perty himself, and if the state of this boiler fireplace had been reported to him he would have been quite uneasy until it was put right. The jury returned a verdict of "Accidental death."

Education Committee Approve of the New Proposals.

A STEP TO THE 'VARSITY.

The Warrington Education Committee on Monday night unanimously approved of the establishment of two annual University Scholarships of ,t7O each, and thus the educa- tional ladder has been provided with the two topmost rungs and made complete. Canon MORLEY STEVENSON, in moving the pro- position, gave full details of the scheme, which, he said, was the outcome of very patient and careful consideration. The scholarships would only be given to the right people, and these would have to prove that their merits and cir- cumstances justified the giving of the scholar- ships. Councillor WILKINSON seconded the adoption of the scheme.

Alderman BENNETT thought that the age of candidates should be extended from 19 years to 25 years. Mr. JARMAN thought that student teachers should not be excluded from the scholarships. Councillor llonaTs (the chairman): They have provision made for them already.

Mr. JARMAN : Provision of a sort. Councillor ROBERTS: Yes, a very good sort.

GIRLS WHO MARRY.

Dr. JOSEPH (the Deputy Mayor) congratulated the Committee on the scheme. One clause stated that parents or guardians of pupils com- peting must have resided in Warrington for two years. There was nothing to prevent parents who lived elsewhere to come into Warrington to qualify. I think, he ' said, more preference might be given to the boys, as frequently a girl has a great deal of money spent upon her, and then she goes and marries, and it is all lost. (Laugh- ter.)

Mr. J. UPSON : I would like to point out, with reference to the peridd of living in the borough, that the boys and girls in the borough have only scholarships to the extent of £5 available, whereas if they live in Padgate, Penketh, or Winwick they have scholarships of £6O, and if they live in Cheshire they have £6O, and I have not heard of any eager exodus to Warrington from Penketh, Padgate, or Cheshire for the scholarships. (Laughter.) .Dr. JOSEPH : Perhaps they do not know about it. (Laughter.)

LANCASHIRE'S DEARER EDUCATION.

Increases in the cost of both secondary and elementary education are foreshadowed in esti- mates issued by the Lancashire County Council. For elementary education alone the estimated increase, compared with the preceding year, is put at A:20,310. The actual estimate for the year ending March 31st, 1915, is .t. 572,260 as against A:551,950 for that ending next month. Teachers' salaries account for £14,000 of the increase, while £3,000 more is required for the Medical inspection of school children. On the income side the receipts from sources other than that of rates amount to £275,326. as against .C 276,198 in the preceding year. Compared with the preceding year, the esti- mates for higher education show an increase from .t 149,175 to £152,475. Last year the Local Government Board authorised the County Council to raise a rate not exceeding 2W. in the X for higher education only. The County Council passed a resolution asking the Board to make an order sanctioning a rate for higher education not exceeding 3d. in the C. and if OP increased expenditure provided for in the est!: mates is sanctioned by the County Council will be necessary to make an application thiA year to the Local Government Board for .P4'wer' to levy a rate for higher education na4 exceed- ing 3W. in the X.

GREATER CHANGES TO COME.

Councillor ROBERTS said his wife had always been a helpmeet to him. Continuing, he said that municipal politics had for a long period occupied a considerable part of his spare time, and his own feeling about it was that while great changes had come and greater changes would come, it was more essential now than ever that representatives should work together for the COMIIIOII welfare. (Hear, hear.) He belonged to the people. He was the son of a working man, and so far as prospects years ago were concerned it seemed that his lot in life would be largely associated with the class and position of his father. Circumstances had turned out differently, but his eympathies with those from whom he sprung had not lessened. With regard to the future, he hoped, as ,he had lived in harness, to die in harness—(hear, hear) —but that could only be by their good-will and pleasure. The educational side of municipal matters strongly appealed to him, because he fully recog- nised the important part education must play in regard to the interests of the country in the future.

===== 0002601_19140221_art0080.txt | included=true | included =====
An Educational Advance.

In Warrington they were taking an additional step in their educational arrangements. Up to the present they had not had an opportunity of an outlet from the elementary and secondary schools for those children who had the brains, whose circumstances would not allow them to go further, and for their abilities to be displayed or cultivated. He would submit a scheme at the Education Committee and afterwards to the Council, to make it possible for the cleverest Lads and girls to go from the elementary school to the secondary school, and then to the uni- versity. (Hear, hear.) He was a democrat, and believed that the average quality of brain was as good in the lowest ranks of society as in the highest. He hoped those who were successful would carry into their new spheres of life the sympathies; associations and knowledge of the class to which they belonged. (Hear, hear.)

Revolution Not Necessary. Other countries had achieved a certain amount of liberty for the masses by revolution- ary Methods. It did not matter what question wa,9 brought forward, so long as it involved ex- penditure i of money t was difficult to persuade people that money carefully and thoughtfully expended was bound to Produce its own fruition. A LIBERAL MEMBER. A comprehensive vote of thanks was passed on the proposition of COUnCiDOE ROBERTS, who spoke in eulogistic terms of the excellent work done by 3,1 r. TO COMBAT PLANT DISEASES.

Sir Sydney Olivier, Permanent Secretary to the - Board xof Agriculture, spenking at a National Farmers' Union dinner at Maidstone, on Saturday,. outlined the policy the Board have decided upon With regard to diseases of plants d pests. The system of haying merely county inspectors, lie explained, was not satisfacbory, and it was proposed to 'strengthen the Board's inspectorate and deal with plant disease's in the same way as they dealt with diseases of animals. Without additional expense to the counties, probably 20 or 30 additional inspectors would be appointed in the course of the next year to cope with and stamp out outbreaks of plant disease wherever they might occur.

A WIDNES SENSATION.

_ The body of George Thomas was found hang- ing by a handkerchief to a cistern of a lavatory in Victoria. Park, Widnes, on Wednesday. lie was 33 years of age, and was the son of Mr.' George Thomas, postmaster of Kingsley, near Frodsham. A week or two ago he went to stay with Mrs. Read, of Birchfield-road, for the bene- fit of his health. Some time back he was • slightly injured in a railway accident in Staf- fordshire, and from then his di,sposition had been very nervous.

I Record Gathering in Orford

Ward.

MRS. CROSFIELD S THRUSTS.

Tories Do Not how a Liberal

When Thep See One.

Liberal women of Orford Ward created a record for ward meetings en Monday evening, when a most enjoyable tea, meeting and con- cert were held in the Brick-street Schools. A large number sat down to tea, which was excel- lently served and thoroughly enjoyed. The subsequent proceedings were presided over by Mrs. Crosfield, who was given a hearty recep- tion. The concert was an excellent one, and the items supplied by the Misses Halliwell, Master Halliwell and Master Dodds were fully appre- ciated, as well as the songs rendered by the Misses Vera and Milly Jones, Miss E. Wilkin- son, Miss Elsie Hewitt Mrs. Sanders, and Mr. E. Walker. The, accompanists were Miss Wardle and. Miss Sherburn. Mr. Crosfield was given a typical welcome and delivered a pointed address. Miss' Truda Crosfield dealt 'with the Woman Suffrage question. The prize given by Mrs. Wilkinson for the neatest baby's pinafore not to cost more than Is. was presented to Mrs. Hill, and Mrs. Leach received Mrs. Halliwell's prize for the neatest baby's 'petticoat costing less than Is. _ _

Mrs. Osborne and Mrs. Dutton were on the platform, along with members of the Ward Committee, which is constituted of the follow- ing: Mesdames Halliwell (captain), Bunch, Lindley, Pendlebury, T. Locker, Pemberton, Sanders, Miss Short and Miss Booth. The helpers included Mesdames John Wilkinson, Cunnington, Massey, Coyne, Critchley, Little- wood, Hewitt, Roberts and Frayar, and the Misses J. E. and M. J. Wilkinson. •

INNOCENTS IN IRELAND. Mrs. Crosfield and "The Trip."

Mrs. CROSFIEth said that although she had been abroad, it had not prevented her following Warrington news with great interest, and, at times, with great amusement. (Laughter.) There was no doubt that the Tories were doing all they could to' make Warrington look ridicu- lous; and it was a good thing there was a large element of Liberalism in the town, or she did 'not know what would happen to "poor' old Warrington." (Laughter.) The beginning of the whole trouble was the election of Mr. Harold Smith. They might tell her, Warrington was to blame for that, but then, at that time, War- rington did not know hi rri as well as it does now. (Hear, hear.) There has been a remark- able exploit, said Mrs. Crosfield, of which we have all heard, thanks to "The Examiner"— the expedition of the twelve to Ireland. (Laughter.) Six of them were Tories, and I am sure they returned no . better than they went. (Hear, hear.) But what -puzzles me are the other six, who ,were alleged to be Liberals. Wp will call them "the six: innocents." What were these innocents? Have they been heard of as prominent liberals? (" No.") Then why were they selected to represent the Liberals of Warrington? It -is very mysterious indeed. (Laughter.) • One of them—the Tariff Reformer—showed a remark- able thirst for information when 'com- bined With a free trip. (Laughter.) ,The fact that he is a Tariff Reformer proves that he is not a Liberal, because one essential part of Liberalism is Free Trade. (Hear, hear.) Either "the six innocents," who were supposed to represent Liberals in Warrington, were very weak-kneed and not strongly constituted Liberals, and that is why they were selected; or the Tories don't know a Liberal when they see one. (Hear, hear, and applause.) When these "six innocents" heard of ,the trip they offered their services; and the Tory-party, not knowing a Liberal when they saw one, selected' them. Then A was the Tory party who were 6the innocents to have been taken in. (Laughter.) It is a piece of absurdity and Cheap Jack trumpery to invite men to Ireland who have not got the political and economical knowledge, to judge a question of this kind.. (Hear, hear.) Yet they pretend to have the last word on the pro- blem, and they set up their opinion against that. of four-fifths of the Irish nation. It is not necessary to go to Ireland for the one fact that counts in the' Irish question. It can be found in any record of the General Elections for the last thirty years. There are four-fifths of the population in favour of Home Rule. (Applause.) I know Mr. Eng- land would say that does not matter in the least, because in his leer to "The Examiner," which was not written on Liberal principles, lie said majorities did not matter, and very often, in his opinion, it might be right and proper for the will of' the minority to prevail. (Laughter.) The essence of Liberal Government is that the will of the people shall prevail. (Hear, hear, and applause.)

===== 0002601_19140221_art0085.txt | included=true | included =====
The Parliament Act.

. In conclusion, Mrs. Crosfield said that all the attempts to stir up indignation in Ulster and to force a dissolution of Parliament was not because the Conservatives thought that Home Rule would be bad for Ireland, or that they were:against Home Rule. A was because of their fear for the Parliament Act, which did away with the power of the House of Lords, and so enabled the Liberal Government to pass their measures. They should all organise to- getlier t Liberal Government noftvreurAsntemrtaet nseot thewtohialalttdt ebtmehapei sf fruit rtdkae ns tdor of wy utt ill: dee not fail. (Applause.)

WOMEN AND THE FRANCHISE.

Norway's Successful Move.

Keenly interested in the Woman Suffrage question, Miss TRIIDA CROSF/ELD aroused the interest of the audience in that direction, by referring to the enfranchisement of the Nor- wegian women which took place seven years ago. The proof of the pudding, she said, was in the eating, and after seven years of it in Norway no one had had indigestion. (Hear, hear, ano laughter.) The men had welcomed the idea, and there was never any sex antagonism on the subject. Amongst the reforms which had been

(Ap- plause.) After detailing the benefits of these measures, Miss Crosfield said such results ought to encourage them in England. (Hear, hear.) The other day the Governor of South Australia said: " You ought to have the vote.. Women are horn administrators and organisers." (Ap- plause.)
