THE TORY PREDICAMENT.

Mr. Crosfield and the "Contradictory"

Party.

CROMELD thanked-the ladies heartily for the reception they had given him. A better reception could not be given anywhere in Eng- land than the Warrington ladies knew how to give. (Applause.) lie was sure that the whole of the Liberal party in Warrington was very greatly indebted to them and fully appreciated the work they did. He felt certain that as long not going to have more valuable causes in the defence they lived, and as long as he lived they of which they could fight than the causes they had at the present time., (Applause.) He thought the enthusiasm which the ladiesl put into the work was due to the fact that in these days, more -so than ever before, politics were going right down into the home-life of the n country. Owing to, the Old-Age Pensions Act Ohad been reduced by 20 per utPerisi door pauperism had decreased by 9 (Applause.) And, not less prac- citnisecndaptleo,r indoor pauperism t. Pagre far-reaching than that, was the 'effect of the victory in the last ten years' battle ,in defence of Free Trade. (Hear, hear.)

===== 0002601_19140221_art0088.txt | included=true | included =====
Practical Side of Free Trade.

Nothing touched the homes and of the people more directly, more practically, and in a more simple fashion than Free Trade—(ap- plause)—because there was not a single thing consumed in the household, practically no article of food, clothing, furniture, or anything that went to make for the comfort of the old folk, or the ha.ppiness of the younger folk, which was unaffected by Free Trade. They had been win,ning in the fight, and it had had a good deal to do with the development of keen enthusiasm in their ranks in support of the Liberal cause. Perhaps, added Mr. Crosfield, you* have taken a little, malicious amusement out of the sorry predicament in which the opponents of Free Trade, and with them the Warrington Tories, have found themselves. (hear, hear.) Certainly, in no great contro- veivsythat you or I can call to mind have we witnessed such a mass of contradictions as we lireNf-oermseepnarotny. They the part of the so-called Tariff have boxed the compass; got rid of their leaders, and thrown overboard one-half of their policy. Indeed, they have done every mortal thing they can to wheedle themselves into the good graces of the con- stituents. (Hear, hear.) It has been a mass of contradictions, to which they have treated us. _

===== 0002601_19140221_art0089.txt | included=true | included =====
Tories " Played Out."

After relating a humorous story illustrative of a very contradictory American, Mr. Ciosfield said : That man cannot hold a candle to some of our friends on the Tariff Reform platform. In spite of them all, we have done splendidly in Old England. In spite of all their whirlwind campaigns and the untold amount ,ef money that has been squandered in this effort to re- impose the shackles of Protection upon us, we have done well, and are doing well now. (Ap- plause.) As for the Tories, I see, in them, neither policy nor brains. One feels sorry for the Tory party, because they are pretty well played out. But what is really serious for the Conservative party—if it is to be rehabilitated and occupy the position it once did—is that they are confronted with the fact that they have not a single young man on the front benches of first-class ability coming along, with a claim to lead. I must say that the outlook is pretty poor for them. They are relying, they tell you, on the swing of the pendulum, after three elec- tions, to put them into power now. But if you get them to talk quietly, some of our Conserva- tive friends—and some of them pretty well in heart, quite apart 3, the know—you will find they are not very oep ra effects sanguine of any swing of the pendulum taking them back to power at the present time. I acapntt from p tell you from ol thpebitoitetsom f or ephemeral of that kind, that when one considers the great work we have been doim3, and the equally important work still awaiting for us on the near horizon, which the Liberal party is going to tackle, we have to win the next election, and the next after that. (Cheers.)

STEWARDS ENTERTAINED.

Met Who Do the bet Spade Work

for Liberalism.

LEADERS' EULOGY.

Enthusiastic Anticipations for the Neit Election Result.

At the invitation of Mr. and Mrs. A. 11. Cros- field, the officers and stewards of the Liberal Association, about 80, partook of a substantial tea, and thoroughly enjoyed the subsequent concert and speeches, in Atkinson's Cafe on Tuesday evening.'

Mr. SANDERS explained that following upon a -meeting in the afternoon, Mrs. Crosfield, Miss Crosfield and Mrs. Osborne were so busy that they could not attend. Councillor J. T. Locker (honorary secrettiry of the association) was not well enough to be present.

_ Mr. Crosfield presided and was supported by Mr. C. E. Parker (president of the association), and Councillor Wilkinson. Mr. Sanders (agent) had made excellent arrangements, and the varied vocal iterns by Mrs. Sanders, Miss Carrie Hastings, Mr. J. Willett and Mr. Walker'were splendidly rendered and fully appreciated. Mr. Pendlebury was the accompanist, and Mr. Sanders amused the audience with "Nomina- tion Day," a Lancashire dialect number. Mr. Prescott impressed the audience with his dramatic recital, "The Dover Express," in which he displayed his capable elocutionary powers. Mr. Crosfield delighted the assembly with two well-executed pianoforte solos.

MR. PARKER GOING TO IRELAND.

. Mr. PARKER had to/leave the gathering early in the'evening, as' he said; "to get ready to go to Ireland," a statement which, in view of recent disclosures, caused much laughter. He appreciated the kindly thought of Mr. Crosfield in getting the men together, and he also appre- ciated very much the work of those, who as stewards, were behind the scenes. They made it very easy for those who were in the limelight. and on the front seats, but the success of meet- ings really depended very much on the stewards. Mr. CROSFIELD having stated that their Presi- dent was going to Ulster, Mr. PARKER retorted, "Yes; but I am going to pay my own expenses, anyway. (Laughter and applause.) lam pro- mised a treat to-morrow night. lam going to, see the Ulstermen drilling. (Renewed laughter.) YORK DONE AND WORK TO DO. Mr. Crostield Says Liberals Must Remain Masters In Their Own Household.

Mr. CROSFIELD said it was a sincere pleasure to be allowed to meet so many of those who were amongst the most active workers for the Pro- gressive cause in Warrington. Even the most wonderfully gifted phalanx of men forming the Cabinet, and a Prime Minister, with all Mr. Asquith's extraordinary sagacity and magnifi.. cent intellect, judgment, foresight and deter- mination, could not get through their work if it were not for the honest, unselfish, unostenta- tious work done by vigorous men like those before him. (Hear, hear.) Ile thanked them for the splendid work they were doing for the Progressive cause in Old England, and in War- rington. It had not been without its results. (Hear, hear.) • A Wonderful Contrast.

There was a wonderful contrast between the position of things nowadays and a few years ago, and it was to such men as these that the credit was due.

From 1903, he said, for three years yo tt had the raging, tearing propaganda, three years of the tremendous effort made to throw the country back commercially, to throw it back by vitiating the whole of our public life through introducing the old and discredited system of Protection.l (Hear, hear.) There was also the effort to introduce, into the British Empire, 'a system of forced labour, which I have constantly called, "a vile system of quasi-slavery." (Hear, hear.) In 1906 the Liberals knocked Chinese labour on the .head, and gave Protection a pretty good settler. We also set about clearing off some of the arrears that inevitably accumulate in a country like ours when the Tory party have been in power for a long spell. The Education ' Bill and the Licensing Bill were passed after an enormous amouusnttdeorfivPeadriltiasmauentitlaeryritylafbroeuehr by overwhelming emneaijeosr.i7esT,heasnedmbeyasaurGesovz' ifmnroesomnitentltllleiyiechtlnlrosiadtwi'tnuj out by the Tories—("Shame") —and that made all Liberals feel we were approaching a final crisis. When a couple of years afterwards the Lords had no more sense than to throw out the great Budget of 1909 that crisis was reached; the Parliament Bill was introduced, and, through a couple of elections, the nations supported the democratic party of the people; the Parliament Bill was placed upon the Statute Book—(cheers)—and is nothing less than the modewrnhicMhag:_ea iCiahvaertafoorf years been As for the great Budget of 1909, it is the very foundation of the monumental work of Social The Two Crises. renefgoargd. me ""

Mr. Crosfield passed to the crisis in Ireland,

We'll tax the lot and make 'ern dear and scarce, • and hence our summons To the nation to accept our precious gospel of short commons. From a teaspoon to a toasting fork, a blanket to a bolster, On woollen, fustian, flannel, as on linen fresh from Ulster,

Everything that's good for eating, or which helps keep out the cold, All that's pleasant to the young folk, or a com- fort to the old, We'll heap the taxes on them, and we'll send the prices up, Impoverish the poorest homes, stint every bite and sup."

"It is a glorious enterprise, but," England cried, "Beware, Go softly, Ebenezer, pray, go softly; have a care. No doubt it is a policy for which we'd sell ow souls, But the people who abhor it turn and rend us at the polls.

It is true that to appease them and get rid of their embargo, And make our ship both trim and light we've clucked out half the cargo. But now the farmer and his lads have just begun to kick, And, I grieve to say. good Eben, things are looking pretty sick.

For though we've vowed and worn the cursed foreigner would pay, The taxes all the time till we were ready foi to slay (With an army by conscriptionist new fangled means recruited. Means which, to English ways and needs, feared are most unsuited)

Each miscreant and rascal who has ventured to be born In any land in Europe save the one which we adorn; Yes, though we've given our pill a coat of anti- foreign gilt, The people will NOT take it, but still thunder forth OUR guilt;

but said, amid applause, that there were two crises.

I have never underrated, he continued, the genuine—but unfounded—apprehension that you find in Protestant circles in the North of Ireland. lam certain that, to a large extent, the position you find there to-day is born of old traditions of ascendency. (Hear,' hear.) I have never questioned the sincere apprehension of many Scottish-descended, and, to some extent, British-descended, Protestants in North-East Ulster, and certainly, from the first to last, there has been no disposition in any responsible quarter of the Liberal party to deny our friends in North-East Ulster any conceivable reasonable concessions. (Hear, hear.) These concessions are going to be made to fear and not to real danger: We have had ample proof—l proved it in the Warrington Press 20 years ago—that all through Ireland you find Protestants in tiny minorities in different localities with over- whelming Catholic populations all round them, and not one hair of their heads has been hurt, or any attempt b,een made to molest them or treat them unfairly. (Hear, hear.) It is all the other way—(hear, hear)— Protestants are voted in the biggest positions, hold posts of honour and are looked up to. (Applause.) lam pleased to hear that ap- plause, which shows me that what Mr. Swift MacNeill' said in Warrington has not been thrown away. (Hear, hear.) . •

He coupled with the vote the name of Mr. Sanders, who had so ably organised that meeting, and who had shown his ability to retaliate upon his opponents in most telling fashion. (Applause.) SIIINING IN DARKNESS.

Councillor WILKINSON contrasted the brilliant men at the head of the Liberal party with those at the head of the Conservative party. The Liberals could spare a dozen men and they would still have a large licitly of the most cap- able statesmen to fall back upon. Where were the great men on the Tory side? They had not any, and that was how it came about that some of the smaller luminaries appeared to shine. (Laughter_und applause.) Mr. Smith came to Warrington and talked about men, wealthy and in good positions, sticking to office for their salaries. He thought Mr. Smith should go down on his knees to pray daily for the con- tinuance of the present Parliament, for when it was dissolved he would lose Warrington, and his £,400 a year. (Laughter and cheers.)

And so the time has come to change our key and all our hope Of winning votes is centred in His Holines4 the Pope. The drum ecclesiastic we will beat for all it 6 worth, And stir up religious rancour in the men of Irish birth. With plenty of seditious work, and reckleis Orange rant, Appeals to played-out prejudice, disguised by solemn cant, We may excite the ire of some deluded eons of toil, And in rowdyish disturbance we may yet with luck embroil

Liberals.

• THE LAND BILL.

Mr. and Mrs. Crosfield on tile Good Time Coning.

Brick-street Schoolroom is a very commodious building, but there was such a large number of women Liberals from Fairfield and Howley Wards at the tea, meeting and concert on Thursday evening that there was a marked lack of accommodation. However, Mrs. Crosfield, who presided, explained that very shortly they would have the new hall, where there would be plenty of room. Over 400 attended, and, hav- ing enjoyed the excellent tea, were delighted with the vocal items and keenly interested in the speeches afterwards made. Mrs. Dutton, Mrs. Sanders and Miss Delaney were in good voice, and rendered pleasing numbers, as also did Mr. Dutton, Mr. Hannan, and the Brothers Delaney. Mr. Pendlebury was the accom- panist. A humorous sketch, "Jackets," was ably given by the Working Men's Mission party, whilst equal success attended the efforts of Mr. and Mrs. Hassall and party in "A Patient Hus- band."

Mrs. Crosfield was supported by Mrs. Osborne (association honorary secrntary), Mrs. Pendle- bury (Fairfield Ward captain), Mesdames J. T. Locker, Pemberton, O'Brien, Dutton, Lindley,. and Percival; "alias Short and Miss Booth; Councillor J. T. Locker, Mr. Walker, and others. The helpers were: Mesdames Shaw, Ashton, Langley, Birch, Ashworth, Vose, Wilson, Fox, Gibson, Gatley, and Rushton, with Miss Gibson. and Miss Willett.

Miss Harrison won the prize for the best crochet d'oyley costing threepence; whilst Mrs. Dixon won the prize for the best pair of baby's crochet socks costing sixpence. LAND INIQUITIES TO GO.

Mrs. Croslield's Tribute to Mr. Lloyd

George. At the commencement Mrs. CROSFIELD dealt with the "trip" in witty fashion, and-followed with a few remarks on the absorbing land ques- tion.

Before the Parliament Act was passed, she said, it was not possible to get a measure of land reform through the House of Lords, but the Second Chamber had not now the power to ' throw out and destroy the result of Liberal work. (Applause.) It was the intention of the Liberal party to see that the countrysides were not depopulated and the towns overcrowded; to obtain better housing accommodation; to get a minimum wage for the agricultural labourer; and to give the farmer a security of tenure, which meant that he would not occupy his farm at the whim of the landlord. (Applause.) All. the iniquities of the landlord system would be swept away, and the tenants would no longer be at the mercy of the landlord. The Land Bill was to be prepared by one of the best men in the Cabinet, a genius who was, perhaps, as sensible as few were of the needs and wants of the poor. They knew to whom she was allud- ing; they knew who it was who had the inter- ests of the poor at heart—Mr. Lloyd George. (Loud applause.) The "No Programme" Party. . _

In regard to the Tories, she might say that she did not think they had any programme at all. (near, hear.) If they had, they were ashamed of it and did not show it. The Tory party was in absolute disagreement with itself. (Hear, hear.) There were some in favour of Tariff Reform and some against it.. Some wanted Home Rule and some did not. Some said "Ulster will fight"; others said "Ulster will not fight." They contradicted themselves in every way. (Hear, hear.) They had no belief in their cause, and that was partly why the Liberal prospects became bright, and even brighter than ever. They would become in.. creasingly so, as long as the women of War- rington put enthusiasm in their cause and energy in their work. (Applause.)

THE LAND FIGHT.

Mr. Crosfield Discusses An Old Issue. Mr. CROSFIELD said he had only time to deal with one of the causes for which they were going to fight—the land question—which every- body who dwelt in Liberal circles had heard discussed for many years. (Hear, hear.) He had never hesitated to speak of the interests a the people, whether they lived in the country or in the towns. He had never separated their interests. (Hear, hear.) He was sure that the needs and claims of the farm labourer would find an echo in the hearts of his fellow-country- men not the less because they lived in the towns. That willingness to help mates and fellow-men was one of the finest traits they could find. It was the spirit which should animate all sections of the nation. (Hear, hear.) They were all working for better condi- tions. Take the stipulation that for labourers in the country districts there was going to be a Saturday half-day holiday. That was only one little item, but they should just think what it meant to the households of the labourer, what a difference it made to his wife and his whole

The Greatest Good. It was only part of the vast policy based on the fundamental principle of the great Liberal cause, a principle enunciated in the old maxim, " The greatest good of the greatest number." (Loud applause.) It was a maxim that could be understood by the people, and since the Liberal Government came into power they had, put that maxim into prEctical working effect. (Applause.) They had not given, and they could not promise to give, the millenium. In many respects this was a very imperfect world, and they had to be satisfied if, without making it a perfect paradise beyond the poweu of mortal statesmen, they had helped to make the world better than it was. (Applause.) When they had fewer of the old folk in the paupers' house, and hardly any outdoor pauperism left, improvements in trade and in many ways, let them be thankful, at any rate, and take care that they did not lose the means by which they might continue that good pro- gress. This progress benefited not only all who were living now; it was quite as much for the benefit of the coming generation. (Hear, hear.) That was why they Liberals laid such stress oi. defending the Parliament Act, which they had

won by such a tremendous constitutional and national effort. That was why they said to the whole democracy, "Be on your guard and take care you are not jockeyed out of your rights; take care you are not deluded with the talk of making use of a little bit of prejudice in one small corner of the United Kingdom. Take care you are not robbed of those rights which will alone secure that orderliness in the State which is the best thing for you, the best for the whole of the people, and the best substitute for disorder, discontent and bitter feelings, which may ultimately lead to a revolution in your midst." (Loud applause.)

- , Hearty votes of thanks proposed by Mr. WALKER and Councillor LOCKER closed the meet- ing. Councillor LOCKER said they knew full well that there was no worthier citizen to represent the town of his birth than Mr. Crosfield, whom they would return next time. (Cheers.)

HEL

THE DEMAND OF THE OPPOSITION - FOR AN ELECTION.

One would .have thought that an Opposition honestly desirous of again bringing the Home Rule question to the test of the polls would have taken the first opportunity to concentrate their energies upon that question. But what has happened this week? Three by-elections have been taking place—in South Bucks, owing ' to the elevation of Sir C. A. CHIRPS to the peerage; in Poplar, owing to Sir SYDNEY BUXTON'S appointment as successor to Lord Gladstone in the Governor-Generalship of South Africa; and in Bethnal Green, owing to Mr. NIASTERMAN'S, elevation to the Cabinet as Chan- cellor of the Duchy of Lancaster (retaining his charge of the Insurance administration), which post Mr. HOBHOUSE has vacated for the Post- master-Generalship, succeeding Mr. sAmeEt., who is the. new President of the Local Govern- ment Board. Mr. BURNS takes the place of Sir SYDNEY BUXTON at the Board of Trade. In these cdptests, in representative English con- stituencies, Home Rule has taken a decidedly secondary position. It is not by-elections the Opposition want; it is a General Election. It is not to slay Home Buie that they pine; it is to destroy the Parliament Act, that has Int- considerately taken them out of the motherly lap of the Lords. At the most critical stage in the fortunes of the Flame Rule Bill the electioneering tactics of the Opposition have been and are' directed entirely to working Up grievances against the Insurance Act; and these by-elections have. at least demonstrated more conclusively than ever how factitious and delusive is the cry of the Opposition for General Election on the Home Rule issue.

===== 0002601_19140221_art0105.txt | included=true | included =====
AMERICAN %TES.

When Tariff Reformers are called on to demonstrate the truth of their assertion that Tariff Reform will enable new industries to be started in this country, which cannot be started under the existing competitive conditions, they always say: "Look at. the American tinplate trade. There was,no production of tinplates in the United States till the highly protective McKinley Tariff was passed in 1890; and now they not only produce all the tinplate they want, cheaper than they can buy it from us, but export large quantities in competition with South Wales." The obiious answer is, of course, that mil- lions of dollars have been wasted in the States in establishing and maintaining this "infant industry." The consumer, instead of being enabled to profit by the use of real tinplate freely imported, has been charged unduly high prices for a poor imitation of the .Welsh article; and as the erm tconsumer" includes everyone who purchases "canned" foods, it , includes practically the whole of the public. It is an established fact that this "pauper" industry has never succeeded in turning out more than a colourable imitation of Welsh tin- plate, Besides using less tin to the box of plates, the operatives have never developed the skill in spreading the tin which has given Wales the command of the world's market. Consequently, American tinplate is unable to resist chemical action, and has, therefore, been found unsuitable for "packing" meats, or fruits, or other commodities which contain moisture. That is why the United *States have been obliged to continue to import Welsh tin- plates. If the home production is" more than sufficient," and the price is "lower than ours." as Tariff Reformers maintainothere would be no necessity to import our plates.

PevEau, Fins Gams never fail to please.— Sole agent, J. S. GARLAND. Ironmonger, 51 to 55, Bridge-street, Warrington., _

COMMANDER EVANS'S VISIT.

Commander Evans must have been highly gratified with his visit to Warrington, for Parr Hall was crowded. and his \ audience was appreciative to a special degree. Certainly the occasion was an unusual one, for it was the telling of a story of hardships endured, and of death met in the way of duty. Such stories have always commanded, and we hope they always will do so, the admiration, and the veneration of Englishmen. The Deputy Mayor (Dr. Joseph), who introduced the distinguished traveller, referred to the fact that some people question the good there is said to be in these risks run and discomforts borne. • But li. holds that it is our duty to know the,world we owe to our Creator, and that such efforts as a Polar Expedition not only give us necessary geographical knowledge, but bring out the best qualities that men possess. Commander Evans clearly proved the truth of all that Dr. Joseph had said. That a large body of men could be isolated from the rest of the world , for years, and not have a cross word, and that. the only differences arose upon such points ! as who should be the one to make the most sacrifices for the welfare of the rest, and the good of the expedition, perhaps in itself made the expedition worth while. Commander Evans himself showed how modest a really great arid good man can afford to be. fie spoke feelingly of Oates, who walked out to lift his burden from his companions and to die a lonely- death; he drew a veil over the last moments of the death of his "leader" in the hour of triumph ;,he. effazed himself . almost entirely, and made his lechire' as humorous and. as entertaining as-if tragedy and suffering had been almost unknown.

_ Food was tie greatest of all questions, and he drew a vivid picture of how the party lingered over Sam Weller's invitation to a " swarry.consisting of a boiled leg of mutton."' One Christmas Day, he said, they had a dinner which the cook had been building up in secret for weeks as a surprise treat, which was "a horrible mess," followed by two small plum puddings, which had been kept in a pair of spare socks. Literary men and women will be interested to know 'that the Bible and Dickens formed the small library of these men at the uttermost end of the earth.

Commander Evans told a thrilling story of the fidelity Of two of the i party to himself, which he rounded off in his usual way—by, changing from grave to gay. Lashley, one of his pompanions, was the finest specimen of a hardy man he had ever come across, and Lashley was a life-long teetotaler and non- smoker. Loud applause greeted this testimony to the value of abstinence from evil habits, even in the Polar regions. The only man who could approach Lashley for manly vigour and endurance, in the, Commander's long experi- ence, was his second companion, Crean. And Crean, said the Commander, had never been either a teetotaler or non-smoker, which let in the other side to applaud, though in a subdued key. The out-of-the:way pictures, especially the moving 'pictures,' gave a realistic effect to the lecture, and brought the audience into the closest touch with the expedition.

LIBERAL ENTHUSIASM. A great wave of Liberal enthusiasm has swept over the town this week, as will be seen from our reports of the ward, women's and workers' _gatherings, terminating with the demonstration in Parr Hall last night. To-day the Junior Liberals have a conference and a public meeting in Parr Hall, at which thi Government Land Reform programme will hc fully considered. Most of the other questions are settle& and will soon be dead issues for election purposes, and the Young Liberala are naturally anxious to get to .close quarters with the living programme of the future. Mr. Lloyd Jone and his co-workers have organised to-day's proceedings perfectly, and we may expect progress to be made at the afternoon conference and renewed enthusiasm at the night meeting. On ,Saturday the stalwarts of Whitecross Ward were entertained by Mrs. Henry Roberts,' and the impression given to those versed in Judging such matters was that the workers are literally straining at the leash in expectation of ' the chance they will have—say, in twelve months—of submerging the Opposition locally. Tie same spirit was observable at the stewards' gathering, under the presidency of Mr. Cros- field. Women Liberals, too, have foregathered in unpremdented strength to meet Mrs. Cros- field. The speakers on the Liberal side are very wisely abstaining from personal abuse, of which the Progressive cause, happily, does not stand in need.

THE TRIP. A great deal has been said and written about the famous trip to Ireland, but, in our opinion, it has been reserved for Mrs. Crosfleld, on her visit to Warrington this week, to sum up the situation finally and completely. Mrs. C;ros- field sees in the six supposqd Liberals six peopk whom the Conservative organisers took for six 'innocents," who eventually proved in their innocent way that the Conservative organisers a werd the real innocents in the case. Evidently, according to Mrs. Crosfleld the Tories " don't know Liberal when they see one —a com- prehensive way of stating the case. " What is thought in Liberal circles of The Trip may be inferred from the fact that, on Tuesday night, when Mr. Parker announced to a large body of workers in the party that he had to leave because he was going to Ireland, there was a roar of laughter. It looks as though the innocents made a laughing matter of a very serious subject. We note that our friends the enemy are so elated with the defeat of Mr. Masterman by 24 votes that they have announced it as the "downfall of the Government." They fairly revel in these minority victories for their minority Cause, but as the majority of the Gov- ernment still hovers about the one hundred mark, we shall be able to sleep peaceably for some time to come. In another quarter the Bucks election result, -in which a tremendous Conservative majority suffered a trifle, is put down aska disaster to the Government. It was not an unpleasant kind of disaster.

A BRAVE ENDEAVOUR. Mr. 11. If. &otter, of the National Unionist Association, London, told a Conservative Chi!. audience a startling story last week. According to a contemporary.

The speaker endeavoured to show that the majority of the people of Ireland did not want Home Rule. 'The a,ctual votes east at the last General Election in Ireland for Home Rule showed that only one-tenth, or a little ovm 70,000 people, wanted it. (Hear, hear, and applause.)

It is frankly put as an " endeavour " and not an accomplishment. flow .one-tenth of the people of Ireland could return nearly the whole of the Members of Parliament for Ireland is as mystery. But, we take it, Mr. &otter is a Tariff Reformer as well as a Unionist. Else he would not have been equal to so arranging figures to prove exactly the opposite of whets they mean.

THE GUILD OF HELP. Those who care to know what the Guild of Help is doing for the town should go to the annual meeting on Tuesday evening. The chief speaker, Mr. Hugh Gamon, who comes from Chester, is in close touch with Guild work. An address given by him some months ago at the National Conference of Guilds, created an excellent impression, and the delegates from Warrington felt that he must sooner or later speak at one of the local meetings. The annual report of the Mothers' Welcomes will be pre- sented at the same time. An interestinil development in connection with the child-life of the town will also be mentioned. , From our correspondence' columns it will be keen

A Reminder.

A coloured preacher was vehemently de- nouncing the sins of his congregation. "Bred- dern and sistern, Ah warns you' against de heinous o' shootin' craps. Ah charges yo' against de black rascality o' liftin' pullets! But, above all else, breddern and sistern, Ah de- monisshes yo' at disser season against de crime o' melon stealin' !" A brother in a back seat made an odd sound with his lips, rose and snapped his fingers. Then fie sat don with an abashed look. " Whuffio, mah frien''," said the preacher sternly, "does yo' t'ar up an' snap YO' fingahs when Ah speak o' melon stealin'?" " Yo' jes' reminds me, parson," the man in the back seat answered meekly., " whar All left mah knife."

Made His Trip Heal Cheerful. "When I was in Switzerland," says a Cleve- land business man who has just returned from a vacation in Europe, "I hired a guide to steer me through the Zermatt glacier. "

THE MILITARY YOKE.

Pointed Criticisms of the National Servioe Leane Methods.

SATURDAY,

THAT DEPUTATION.

Trade Unionists Who Are Protestants

and Home Rulers.

A WARRINGTON PEACE MEETING. QUESTIONS FOR MR. RAVERSCROFT.

The conscriptionists recently held their meet- ings in Warrington, and on Thursday it was the turn of the anti-conscriptionists to place their case before the public. For this purpose there was a good audience assembled in the Co-opera- tive Hull, and over the gathering Mr. C. Jack- son Holmes presided.

The Chairman, in opening the meeting, nar- rated a good story of what happened at a meet- ing of the National Service League in War- rington. The peace people distributed peace and anti-military literature outside!' the door, and one lady conscriptionist carried away a quantity which she distributed freely on her way home. Next morning the lady conscription- ist was horrified that she had distributed anti- conscription literature—(laughter)—and the telephone had to be requisitioned so that she might apologise to her friends for her mistake. (Laughter.)

===== 0002601_19140221_art0110.txt | included=true | included =====
"EUPHONISM OF CONSCRIPTION."

• -• Mr. A. 11. Crosfield said his feeling all along had been that in no part of the British Empire would lie allow what was called compulsory military service, which really was a euphon- ism for conscription. (Applause.) We have been concerned with the policy pro- posed to he adopted in old England, and we have been rather specially concerned with it in Warrington, because the efforts of the National Service League have been rather concentrated upon our town. (Laughter.) They have been at it for a very long time. I say deliberately that after all the efforts and the one-sided kind of speeches made in support of compulsory mili- tary service—l am not anxious to say anything over harsh—l say emphatically there is about as much chance of converting the democracy of Great Britain to compulsory service as you or I would have of sweeping away old Hill Cliffe with a can of water. (Loud laughter and applause.)

Heading for Bankruptcy. The Chief Whip of the Liberal party, speaking in Manchester recently to Liberals and Labour, said that the whole of the massed forces of the Progressive party were employed to stop that re-actionary policy. The advocates of compul- sory service asked for a nation in arms, and did not intend .to cut down expenditure on the navy. The exact point to which expenditure upon the navy should be cut down was a very debatable question, into which he would not enter, except to say there was a necessary mini- mum of expenditure upon the navy below which Ihey could not go with safety. If they had a nation in arms the advocates of compulsory service would be able to transport that nation in arms to any desired quarter, and if they were crazy enough to spend the re- sources of the country in maintaining the vast and terrible expenditure upon naval armaments, and to pile up expenditure on the provision of land force, they would, as Lord Haldane had said, be heading towards bankruptcy. (Ap- plause.)

===== 0002601_19140221_art0111.txt | included=true | included =====
A Menace.

They would also Convertthemselves into a menace to other Continental Powers. (Hear, hear.) Nothing would be more•certain to give a greater impetus to the terrible expenditure on the munitions of war in this country and on the Continent.

If you encourage this policy of the National Service League you will first of all heap up the taxation on the citizens heavier than ever it has been, and secondly you will generate a worse spirit between the leading nations of Europe. you owe it to your civilisation to give a lead to the whole world in ideals of a nobler and better character, and finally you would throw back the cause of industrial and social reform. (Loud applause.)

===== 0002601_19140221_art0112.txt | included=true | included =====
AN AUSTRALIAN'S STORY.

Mr. John W. Barry, the organising secretary of the Australian Freedom League, told of the terrible things which were happening under the conscription laws in Australia. There they had set up a military autocracy and caste, under which the working class groaned. If a parent refused to allow his boy to serve he was liable to fines of £lOO, and the boy could be sent to prison, or into even a worse place—a military fortress, where terrible barbarities were in- flicted on youths of 15 or 16 years. The courts where these bo/s were sentenced were filled with officers', and the magistrate had a military ad- viser to tell him what to do. The boys were brow beaten by the military men, and there was no semblance of justice. (" Shame.") In Australia during the last 18 months 18,000. prosecutions had taken place. If that hap- pened in a population of 4 millions, what would happen in Britain, with a population of 45 millions, if they had compulsory service, They would have about 200,000 prosecutions in 18 months. (Hear, hear.) Some 1,500 ladaphad been sent to a fortress in 18 months. ("Shame.")

Workers' Sacrifice. There was no sacrifice on the part of the rich, for their children drilled in the school hours, but those of the poor had to drill at nights and on public holidays. Thus the working class had to make all the sacrifice. They were build- ing up a great military caste in Australia. The scheme was costing three millions a year, and of that one million went in officers' salaries and vested interests. A youth in camp was Paid 3s. a day, whereas at work he could earn Bs. a day, and this was another great sacrifice ex- acted from the workers. There was room for national service, but of the type in which men and women should de- vote their best energies to solve the social .pio- blems of the country, but they would never get that through conscription. (Applause.)

PASTOR AND CAPT. CROSFIELD. The Rev. H. Duunico protested strongly against any form of compulsory service in this country. There were greater heroes on :the railways, in the mines, on the merchant ships, than were found in a land or sea battle. (Ap- plause.) A man driving an express locomotive at 70 miles an hour was more a hero than the best soldier on the battlefield. He saw in the last issue of "The Warrington ,jiminer" that a gentleman for whom he had a high regard, speaking to the Young Liberal League, had said that the citizen army would never be called upon to inter Ore with the just rights of labour. That was a worthless, empty promise—(applause)—for this reason: What were the just rights of labour? No Government had ever before attempted anything so drastic as to interfere with the just rights of labour, and when the military were introduced into Manchester during the railway strike, against the protests of the Lord Mayor, they were not there to interfere with the just rights of labour.

Laughed to Scorn. In such times of crisis and panic as happened in South Africa—(hear, hear)—tho just rights of Labour would be laughed to scorn. These promises were mere spacious proposals to delude the workers and gain their sympathy to con- scription. (Applause.) They would never have the forces of war in the world democratised until they ended the present system of diplomacy. They had suf- fered too long at the hands of ambassadors and secret agents. It was time they made up their minds that they would no longer tolerate secrecy in the foreign department of the Gov- ernment. (Applause.) •

THE QUESTIONS. During questions, Mr. Chamberlain suggested that if five million Socialists in Germany and those in this country struck work against war, there would be no war. •

Mr. Barry thought the only way to stop war was to educate the people to its evils.

Mr. Dunnico said if they got working men intelligent enough to embark upon a national strike against war they would be sufficiently intelligent to bring the Governments to their senses before they declared war. (Applause.) On the motion of the Rev. H. Ogden, seconded by Mr. Jarman, the speakers were thanked for their addresses.

To the Editor of " The Examiner."

Sir,—ln your issue of January 24th you pub- lished the report, statements and opinions of the Wairington deputation to Ireland. Amongst the number of telegrams sent was one from Mr. Ravenscroft (designated a Liberal) to this effect: " All trade unionists here unite in condemning Home Rule." When I read it I thought it rather a sweeping statement. Naturally, I was prepared for diversity of opinion, but was stag- gered by such an emphatic expression of unanimity. I have been actively identified with Liberalism in Warrington for a good many years, but have not had the privilege of know- ing Mr. Ravenscroft. Therefore I would like to ask him through the medium of your columns for a little further proof of the accuracy of is opinion.

-I will admit the deputation acquired a won- derful amount of knowledge in a very short time, and will give Mr. Ravenscroft my reason for asking for further information. I have before me the report in a Belfast newspaper of a public meeting of the Belfast Labour party, under the auspices of the "Independent Labour party of Ireland," on Friday, May 24th, 1912, in Belfast, at which it is estimated nearly 1,500 were present, at which the following portion of a fuller resolution was carried, with one dis- sentient : "That this meeting of working men and women of Belfast welcomes the prospect of the establishment of an Irish Parliament." The speakers were Mr. Thomas Johnston, Mr. James Connolly, Mr. D. R. Campbell, Mr. Richard Braithwaite, and Mr. F. S. Skeffington. I also have before me the agenda of the bi- monthly meeting of the Belfast Trades Council, a body which has consistently supported Mr. Devlin, M.P., since 1906. It was proposed by Mr. John Mercer (Protestant): "That in view of a probable change in the government of Ire- land, steps be taken to secure Labour repre- sentation in the Irish Parliament." Of the Belfast and District Trades and Labour Council, as at present constituted, my assertion is that the president, vice-president, treasurer, secretary, assistant secretary, auditors and trustees are all Protestants and Home Rulers. The exectitive of 15 members are about 12 Protestants and three Roman Catholics. Of these about 13 are Home Rulers and two anti-Home Rulers.

You will quite appreciate my desire to learn the reasons which dictated this particular tele- gram. PATRICK O'BRIEN. 125, Wilderspool-road.

MR. ENGLAND ON "LIBERAL PRINCIPLES." To the Editor of "The Examiner."

Sir,—Forty-two Irish Members are, under the Home Rule Bill, to be allowed to sit at Westminster in the British House of Commons, and to legislate and vote on English, Scotch and Welsh affairs, have a Parliament of their own at Dublin, in -which the English, Scotch and Welsh have no voice or vote, and yet Mr. Parker fails to see that the Irish members will not be a " privileged" body. I suggest to Mr. Parker that if he will not look through party spectacles it will be as clear to him as it is to most people. This policy or privilege has been condemned many times. Even Lord Morley said: "I pre- dict that there is no power on earth that can prevent the Irish members in such circum- stances from being the arbiters and masters of the English policy, of English legislative busi- ness, and of the rise and fall of British admin- istration." The Duke of Devonshire reminded Lord Morley of this speech in the House of Lords on the 27th January, 1913. I suppose that Mr. Parker is aware that the 42 Irish members will draw £16,800 in salaries from the British Exchequer, but, of course, this is not a privi- lege, according to Mr. Parker, though no Eng- lish, Scotch or Welsh M.P. will draw anything, frau the Irish Exchequer. No, the latter will be required and deleted for the services ren- dered by the Irish M.P.'s in Ireland. With reference to his remarks on Free Trade' in Ireland, let me draw his attention to a speech' by Mr. Hewins, M.P., in October, 1912: " By the introduction of this Bill the Government have declared to the world that Free Trade; so far as Ireland is concerned, is at an end. If the Government do not believe in Free Trade for Ireland, and the Irish members do not believe in Free Trade for Ireland, who, then, are going to maintain Free Trade for Ireland? For the Government itself are doing things in regard to this country which are totally inconsistent with the Free Trade system." Again, even the " Litverpool Daily Post" (a Liberal paper) warned its readers on the 13th January, 1912, that "here is no concealment of the object with which many of the 'National- ists are asking that the Irish Customs and Ex- cise should be transferred to an Irish Parlia- ment,did not hesitate to state that this Govern- mentiti. : The bulk of the party are Protection- had scattered Liberal principles to the wind, and named two—may I What about "Retrenchment? aHasdd annoott ht ehri gone to the winds also? What about the crea- tthioeny coarmoeviento over 10,000 and Government Budget officials of nes nr elye- .2200,000,000?

May I add also that this Government, instead of bringing peace into Ireland by this Home Rule Bill have brought a sword, and it will take all the efforts of our statesmen to avoid civil war?

E. ENGLAND.

THE ALTERNATIVE TO TIIE BILL.

To tha Editor of "The Examiner."

- Sir,—The only part of Mr. Charles E. Parker's letter which I desire to deal with is the follow- ing sentence; "The most significant feature in Mr. England's letter is the entire omission of any response to my appeal specially made to the Liberal members of the deputation to state what is their alternative to the present Home Rule Bill." . _

- Now, sir, I fancy that the Liberal members of the deputation; reinforced by practical experi- ence, albeit somewhat short, will agree with the Unionist party on this point. Our alterna- tive to the Home Rule Bill is the maintenance of the Union. Under that Union the land ques- tion, the.souree of three-fourths of past trouble, has been practically settled, the peasants have been re-housed; and the induaries of the coun- try stimulated. Given another three or four years of the work of land purchase and other Unionist reforms, and the agitation for Home Rule, partly sentimental and partly manufac- tured, will die a natural death. Mr. Redmond knows this. With him it is a case of now or never. Hence the Parliament Act, hence the desperate effort to get the Bill .on the Statute Book without risking a general election._ Hale, Cheshire.

w. S. JARRETT.

"Parental Neglect." A batch Of young boys were brought before Messrs. J. Upson (in the chair) and T. Pember- ton, at the Juvenile Court on Wednesday, charged with disorderly conduct in Rylands- street- The boys were dismissed with a caution, the Chairman saying that some of them did not reflect credit on the parents. There appeared to be some parental neglect, and the Bench thought if the boys had been bettei looked after they would not have been in that tronble. A further batch of older boys charged with the same offence were fined 3s. fid. each, and one who made no appearance was fined 4s. fid.

'Will of Mr. W. W. Westwell.

The will of Mr. W. W. Westwell, 103, Lovely- lane, coal merchant at Bank Quay, has been proved at .t 2,338.

A Series of Falls.

As a result of falls three men were admitted into the Infirmary during this week. They are George Wadsworth, 13, St. Peter's-place; Fred Strutton (44), Greystone's-road, Penketh; and James O'Connor (25), 4, Eagle Mount- terrace. None of the men's injuries were serious, and all are progressing satisfactorily.

Mr. Oliver at Preston Brook. Mr. P. M. Oliver will speak at Preston Brook next Thursday evening.

Museum Lecture. The Museum lectures will' be continued to- night in the Co-operative Ball.

Freedom Fellowship. Miss Annie Rose will be the speaker on Mon. day, and Suffragists are invited.

To Aid the Blind.

A concert in aid of the National League of the Blind will be held in Bold-street Wesleyan Schoolroom next Thursday. Mrs. Dodds, pre- sident of the branch, will occupy the chair.

Billiard Shield Presentation.

On Monday night, at the Liberal Club, Crosfield will hand over to the officials of the: Warrington Billiard League a magnificent save' shield that he has presented to them for the use of the second division competition. The presentation will be preceded, at eight o'clock by a game, 250 up, between Mr. Crosfield and Mr. Thomas Horrabin, who is credited with being the best player in the League.

The- Wc:;kCirs at the Whitecross Wire-W-orks are attempting to get the same conditions as obtain at Messrs. Ey-lands' Wire Works, and, through their unions, they are hopeful of secur- ing the concessions, although at the time of writing nothing definite has been arranged. Money in the Mersey. John Heckenbottom, a 13-year-old-boy, of 38, Hume-street, was ordered to receive six strokes with the birch-rod, at the Juvenile Court On Wednesday, for stealing money from a house at ' which he called whilst following his occupation of a van boy. It appeared that the boy took the money from a table in the house, and, becoming frightened, threw it into the river. It was not recovered.

'Wood's College Successes. In connection with a recent shorthand exam- ' nation held under the auspices of Sir Isaac, 11,itman, Liinited, and superintended by Mr. H Ward and Air. J. "Town of Many Industries." When an American town sets cut to get new industries it is said to be on the "boost." War- rington has adopted the Yankee tip, and in a pamphlet entitled "The Town of Many Indus- tries," a strong case is made out whereby manufacturers looking for pastures new should come to anchor in Warrington. A 'prominent point is made of the fact that it is nearly the lowest assessed and rated town in Lancashire, has a special water supply for trade, cheap gas and electricity, admirable rail and water-borne services, and has land for disposal at reasonable prices. The pamphlet, which is profusely illus- trated, should do much to convince manufac- turers that Warrington is the ideal place where- in to concentrate their works.

NeNs'ois Falilonz.Pglrt thoisgraphse establishment in Mr. BirTtl Sankey-street two very fine photographs ever-enlarging works of Messrs. Joseph Crosfield and Sons, which he has recently taken. Each is a splendid example of outdeor photography by the carbon process. The first is a panoramic view of the old section of the works, taken from an elevated position in the "island," or new part, and the second is a section of the new extensions on the "island," a term used to describe the buildings which have in recent years sprung up on the other side of the river. The picture of the old works has the greater part, of the town for a hack ground, and the "atmosphere" has been so perfectly developed that every , part containing even the remotest objects are clearly printed and artistically brought out. The wording on Garnett's ware- house behind Cairo-street and Sankey-street is readable in the picture, and is proof of the re- markable true effects achieved in every detail. Mr. Birtles is to be complimented upon this really fine production.

St. John's Bazaar.

columns, the bazaar in aid of St. John's As will be seen from our advertisement nt Presby- terian Church will be held in Cairo-street School next Thursday,' Friday and Saturday. The present effort is being made in order tc, raise sufficient funds to purchase the freehold of the land on which the church has been built, and to provide further for the erection of a school or class-rooms. The church itself, which , cost nearly .R 2,000 is now well on the way to being clear of debt. The members of the con- gregation and especially- the ladies, have been working hard for over a year in preparation for this bazaar, and the result is that a large assort- ment of useful articles have been got together ready for sale. In addition to the usual fancy goods and drapery stalls, there will be house- hold, refreshment, and fruit and flower stalls, and an excellent series of concerts .entertain- ments, competitions, 8.1 c.. so that all Yisitors to the bazaar may depend upon being able to spend a , pleasant hour or two. The total Sum aimed at is £750, aml the workers confidently apnea] for practical and sympathetic support. Presby- terianism has a. long and honourable history in Warrington, and St. John's Church is doing undoubtedly a good work in the town. •

Esperanto Society. Social. The local Esnerantists held a most successful and enjoyable social at the \society's headquar- ters, 27, Bewsey-street, on Wednesday evening, when about JO members and friends were pie- sent. Among the guests were Msperantists from Manchestet, Liveri)ool, Chester, Earlestown, Golborne, Stockport and Erodsham. The pro- ceedings commenced with a whist drive, the successful competitors being awarded prizes The rousing International choruses Espero" and "Al la Esperantistaro" were 'heartily sung by all Esperantists present. Dur- . ing the evening the Rev. R. B. Primavesi (pre- sident of the Warrington Society) and 'Miss A, West contributed songs, which were, very warmly applauded, and Mr. Dobson, from Chester, and delegate of the Universal Esper- anto Association, gave a humorous recitation, which was much appreciated. Miss J. Corny was the accompanist. The Rev. Eathe Primavesi delivered a short speech, in which he welcomed all the guests, and spoke of the feel- ing of "Esperantism," which binds all together in one common brotherhood. Tie also spoke Of the exceedingly large number of towns all over the world in which there were delegates of the Universal Esperanto Association. Mr. Rhodes Marriot, of Manchester, the president of the Lancashire and Cheshire Federation of Esper- elitists, addressed the ,gathering. The remain. der of the evening was devoted to (lancing and conversation in the "Kara Lingvo." Mr. C Burgess acted as M.C. and Mr. Caldwell the accompanist.

-.„. The births in Warrington last week numbered 36, giving a rate of 25.6; the deaths were 22, giving a rate of 15.2. Scarlet fever and influ- enza accounted each fbr one death.

At the Juvenile Court on Wednesday, Henry Forster, 6, Hardy-street, was proceeded against for the 25th time for failing to send his child to school regularly.—The defendant's wife ap- peared, and said the boy took fits.—The Chair- man (Mr. J. Upson) : What do you mean by it? If he takes fits we think you are responsible; you haven't the decency to clothe him properly. You have pinned that coat; buttons and cotton are cheap enough. With all the money' that has been paid in fines you could clothe him properly.—A fine of ss. was imposed.

The tour was supposed.to start on the cycle from Warrington to Liverpool, and the return via Fleetwood gave opportunity for showing many views of the Fylde and its seuside resorts. Mr. H. TOWN PLANNING.

An Ambitious Rural Scheme for Warrington Area.

For two years the Warrington Rural District Council have considered the Town Planning scheme, and an important step was taken in the matter on Wednesday morning, when owners and persons interested assembled in the Co-operative Hall to discuss a scheme for a por- tion of the Warrington Rural District. Mr. Mather, the chairman of the Rural Dis- trict Council, presided, and there were about 100 persons present. Mr. D. S. Newcombe, who is the town plan- ning officer, stated that the area the Council proposed to lay out was the whole of Sankey and Penketh, and parts of Poulton, Burtoniood and Orford. They had a very ambitious scheme in view, which was far larger than any yet, and would be on totally different lines. In fact, the Council' would be the pioneers of a new type of town planning.

- Councillor Gough opposed the scheme. He thought it would raise the cost of building.—lt was pointed out, however, that it would not do so.

Mr. Percy Bennett wanted to know if some further land of his could be included in the scheme, and he supported the application. con- ditionally. Mr. Newcombe: It is doubtful whether that can be done, but the Council will .!,arefully con- sider it. Alderman Bennett proposed that there should be a scheme, and said it was very badly needed. An immense amount of good could be done by such a scheme. This, as well as another resolution "That the area as explained should be the area of the scheme," was passed. The resolutions will be forwarded to the Local Government Board, who will conduct an inquiry at a later date. Any objections may be sent in writing in the mean- time.

GOVERNMENT 4 AY OFFER PEACE BUT WILL NOT BE BULLIED.

Liberalism in the Parr Hall last night blew an effective cOunter blast to the trumpetings of. the Opposition party in the same hall three weeks ago. The demonstration was of an en- thusiastic character, and was marked by tell- ing, pointed speeches, and a dash of humour was added to the proceedings by comments from the gallery upon the doings of the Opposition. Mr. Arthur H. Crosfield was chairman, and cordial was the welcome he received. He was supported by Mr. Hamar Greenwood,M,P, for Sunderland; Mr. P. M. Oliver, Councillor 11. Roberts, Mr. C. "E. Parker, Alderman Burton, Councillor Wilkinson, Councillor Locker, Mrs. Crosfield, Miss T. Crosfield and others. Apologies were received from Messrs. Z. Armitage, J. A. Ransome, J. Rymer Young, F. W. Monks and others.

difficulties of their country. For three years they sat, and in 1836 they issued their Report. It was a document, the truth of which we were only learning to-day, and the text of it was this: If you want to cure pauperism, deal with the land. What we want, said the Commission, are cottages and not workhouses, land reform and not officials, local government and not red tape. (Applause.) Free the land; and you need not imprison your paupers. (Hear, hear.) Re- claim your bog and your labourer will reclaim himself. There is only waste of fife where there is waste of land. The Government of the day had no use for such Irish madness. In 1337 they sent their apostle, Mr. Nicholls, a ' good Englishman, solid, substantial and rich. He toured the country for six weeks, learnt all there was to be learnt about Ireland, diagnosed the disease and advocated a Poor Law system, founded on the workhouse. With almost in- decent haste his proposals were reduced to an Act of Parliament, and ever since Ireland has been burdened with a system, stupid, wasteful and cruel. (Hear, hear.) Every suggestion of the Commission of 1833 has since been carried into effect, through the Land Acts, the establish- ment of. the Congested Districts Board, the Housing Acts and the Local Government Act. But sixty years of misery intervened, sixty years of misery which could have been saved if Ireland had been governed according to Irish ideas. (Hear, hear.) The House of Lords had been taken from the re-actionaries. Once, like a medifeval hulk, it lay across the seeming harbour. The ship was scuttled. Deprived of the House of Lords, they were rushing to civil war. He asked them to consider their dangerous proposals, before the last step was taken, before the Rubicon was crossed and retreat had become impossible. (Cheers.)

CROSFIELD ON TORY PARTY

CRIES. Mr. Caosrimn, who was received with cheers, said one of the stock cries of the Tory party was that the Government had no mandate for Home Rule, and another, always heard when a really effective piece of work was taken in hand by a Liberal Ministry, was that we were face to face with a national crisis. (Laughter.) As to the mandate. In the autumn of 1910 negotiations took place between the leaders of both parties on the question of Home Rule, and the whole Tory party and Press were busy discussing I devolution for Ireland—which meant Home Rule. Sympathetic articles were written. Lord Dudley urgbd that Ireland should be governed in accordance with Irish ideas—(cheers)—and Mr. George Wyndham was on the same tack; but he (the speaker) did not remember a word being written or said about civil war—(laugh- ter and hear, hear)—or the dire calamities now predicted if Home Rule were granted. Then came the election of 1910, which, so far as the Liberals were concerned, was fought entirely on the Parliament Bill. (Cheers.) Lord Lansdowne made the oft-quoted declaration that the Par- liament Bill was intended to allow the Govern- ment to deal with Home Rule. After the I sweet reasonableness the Tories had been show- ing, after they had recognised the many-sided nature of the Irish question and the opinion of the Colonies and the States on the subject, they could not immediately after the election start a root-and-branch opposition to Home Rule. It was then they invented this theory of no man- date, and talked as if the electors had been tricked and deceived. Was anyone tricked in Warrington? (" No.")

===== 0002601_19140221_art0135.txt | included=true | included =====
What Mr. Smith Said.

Personally, he had been discussing Home Rule for 25 years and every aspect of it. (Cheers.) He would venture to draw Mr. Smith's attention to that little fact because Mr. Smith had been telling a Warrington audience that he (the speaker) knew nothing about Ulster or the Irish problem. (A Voice: "He sold tickets there," and laughter.) Twenty-one years ago, writing to the Warrington Press on the alleged oppression of Protestants in Ulster, he gave many examples showing that Protestants, even when living isolated in enormous Catholic populations, were voted into positions of honour and responsibility, and were time after time sent to Westminster by the votes of Catholics to represent them. (Applause.) He drew Mr. Harold Smith's attention to that. (A voice "And his twelve apostles," and laughter.) My wife, said Mr. Crosfield, dealt with the twelve apostles the other night, and, my friend, if you look at "The Examiner" to-morrow morning you will see that she did so pretty effec- tually. (Laughter and cheers.)

Proceeding, Mr. Crosfield said he was dealing with the Irish question in, the Press in the 'Eighties, the time when, he thought, Mr. Har- old Smith would be about to .get out of swad- dling clothes. (Laughter and applause.) lie had been at these questions a deal longer than ever Mr. Harold Smith had, and had given a deal more time and study, and earnest study —(hear, hear,)—to the problem. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19140221_art0136.txt | included=true | included =====
Real Attack on the Parliament Act.

After the 1910 election Ulster began to come to the front. They had the old jingle from Ulster—" Ulster will fight and Ulster will be right"—and that was worked upon with all the ingenuity of which the stage managers of the Tory partyv‘a , were u capable, and language of in- citements e d , and had been used, ever since. (Hear, hear.) The. Tories egged the Ulstermen on, and then linked the ery of Ulster rebellion with that of no mandate, and with that came a demand for a General Election, which had been the Tories 9e object since the Liberals came into power in 1910. The Tories in London were practically beside themselves— politically speaking, they were in a frantic state of rage—at the idea that not only were the Liberals in power, but that they could give effect to their work—(cheers)—now they were in power. He thought they had given the show away in that blatant and constantly repeated cry of "crisis" and "election." (Hear, hear.) The demand for a General Election from the point of view of the Irish settlement was per- fectly futile; but there was something else in the background. If they man aged to jockey, manceuvre, or wheedle the Liberals into a General Election -- they would have had the election, and that was their object, be- cause following that election, if the Liberals gave way to them, they would, have the Parlia- ment Act in ruins. That was what he meant when he said they were face to face not withl one crisis, but with two. (Cheers.) , He wondered whether those statesmen who nsed language of incitement would really like to see rebellion in Ireland. There were other sections of people in the country besides that little group of misguided Orangemen, and it, was nothing less than a scandal when the lead- ers of the so-called party of law and order got up and talked as they did. (Applause.)

Concession, But Not To Threats. In conclusion, Mr. Crosfield said: Concession? Yes! you say, "Row far?" This Government, in its patience and moderation, but still in its firmness—(hear, hear)—is willing to go in the direction of concession, but surrender to mere , threats and violence?—Never (Applause.) Wwill e maintainthe ou rpolicJr, and, what is more, we defendwill Parliament Act, and we will strengthen the hands of this Government until it can complete the great work of Constitution Reform by dealing—as the King's Speech has foreshadowed—with the reform of the Second Chamber, and so be masters in our own house- hold—(hear, hear)—and under the finest leader- ship that any Progressive party ever had, or could ever wish for—(applause)—we will go on with our great work of social regeneration. (Loud applause.)

IRELAND NEEDED COTTAGES, NOT ‘VORKIIOUSES. Misery That Might Have Been Avoided. Mr. P. M. OLIVER, who had a great reception, said the twelve apostles had earned a rest from criticism, but their week's pilgrimage reminded him of a journey even more significant which was made to Ireland sixty years ago. In 1833 there was sitting in Ireland a Royal Commis- sion to study the reform of the Poor Law. The Commission was composed of Irishmen, men who knew from experience the problems and

===== 0002601_19140221_art0137.txt | included=true | included =====
HOME RULE DODGED. Lesson From Bethnal Green.

Mr. H. GREENWOOD, who was enthusiastically received, said the main question of the moment was Home Rule, but that was persistently dodged by the Conservatives. In Bethnal Green the sole topic of the Conservative party was the administration of the Insurance Act, not Home Rule, and the successful candidate had made use on his poll dards of the sufferings of a poor consumptive patient. (Cries of "Shame.") Yes, we will remind him of that when we get him into the House of Commons. These wild talkers coo like sucking doves when they get into the House of Commons. (Laughter and applause.) The Goverment was going ahead with the Home Rule Bill in all its essentials.

Strikers More Justified.

The next time there was trouble with the railway men—they did fiot ask for political rights, but for a living wage—(applause)—the railwaymen would be more justified than the Orangemen in buying a rifle and a lbandolier and cirtridges, and shoot the first Man who tried to interfere with his strike. (Hear, hear.) He had more sympathl for the poor fellow struggling for a living wage if he rebelled than he had for the ascendancy party in Ulster who had never suffered anything in the way of per- secution, but who had been themselves the per- secutors. (Applause.) There was the talk of exclusion of Ulster Certainly many would be glad to see that ex- clusion, but he was certain that if exclusion was allowed, as years rolled on Ulster would see the unreasonableness of their partition, and appeal to be brought into their native country again. (Cheers.) If Ulster wanted to be ex- cluded, what would the Protestants in the rest of Ireland do? Would Ulster leave them to take their chance? That was a mean policy. (Hear, hear.) The question largely involved the bigotry of the Orangemen, who accused their Roman Catholic fellow-countrymen of being inferior; and, secondly, as holding a desire to tyrannise their Protestant country- men. • That was the crudest form of religious bigotry.

Martyr and Millionaire."

The record of the Orange party was antagonis- tic to the Roman Catholics, hence the attempt at persecution. There was not a case where a Protestant in the three Catholic provinces had been persecuted by Roman Catholics. (Hear, hear.) If the Conservative party could find one single Protestant who had been persecuted in one of the three provinces he would be paraded on every platform throughout the land. He would become a martyr, and a millionaire. (Loud laughter and applause.) Everybody opposed to Home Rule were opposing it simply because the majority of the Irish people are Roman Catho- lics. There is no other reason. (Hear, hear.) As Liberals we cannot have any part or parcel with that attitude of mind, and we won't let the fanatical Orangemen persecute the Roman Catholics. (Loud applause.) To those talking about soldiers and shooting, I say that was not started by the Liberal Gov- ernment or the Nationalist party, and I believe there' will be no shooting. But if Orangemen persecute Roman Catholics because they are Roman Catholics, no Government would be 'Worthy of the name if they did not turn out every policeman and soldier to protect innocent men. (Loud applause.) We are not going to stand by and see people bullied and bludgeoned because of their religious views, whether in Wales or Ireland. (Loud applause.)

" Not Yield to Bullying." At this stage Mr. Greenwood interrupted his speech and asked if there were any questions on Home Rule,, which he would answer at once.

Do you think, asked a questioner, there is any fear of the Liberal Government giving way to Ulster, for if it did the Liberal Party is prac- tically ruined? I cannot speak for the Government, said Mr. Greenwood, but if the exclusion of Ulster is to bring peace and good-will, I think the Govern- ment might go almost that far, but they would not go that far unless it meant a perfect and a final settlement by consent and a shake of the hand all round. The Government will never yield to bullying. (Loud cheers.)

===== 0002601_19140221_art0138.txt | included=true | included =====
Elect Mr. Crosfield.

The Liberal party were never in better heart, and when the time came to go to the electors they would go with a record never equalled before by any Government in this or any other country, and would give a programme dealing with the land that would help to mali-e a better England than the England of to-day. (AP- plan se.) What that time comes I hope that the Pro- gressive forces in this community of Warring- ton will send back to the House of Collllllolls one of the Most useful and most respected members we have ever had there—your own Mr. Crosfield. (Loud and continued applause)

BUILDERS' BURDENS,

Mr. Lloyd George and the Intentions

of the Government.

SAFEGUARDS DESIGNED.

Mr. Furness' Appeal Meets With Encouraging Reply.

t

Mr. William Furness, Peru Lea, Great Sankey, recently wrote to the Chancellor of the Exchequer as follows :-- Dear should be glad to hear from you when it is the intention of the Government to bring in the measure promised to relieve builders under the Finance Act from the unreasonable burdens imposed upon them. I Understood from your public speeches that it was not your inten- tion to penalise the builder.. Unless something is done, and that soon, you will create a scarcity of cottages, the like of which was never contem- plated. _ _

I have bought land just on the borders of Warrington (Belmont Estate, Great Sankey, near station), and am laying it out on ideal lines for cottages from 4s. 9d. to 7s. 6d. per week—eight and twelve to the acre—but I find that I shall, under the Finance Act, be penal- ised for every step rtake. I am afraid that unless something is done (and that soon), this Act will have a terrible rer,il upon the Government when they again appeal to the country, as the electors are begin- ning to realise that it is primarily responsible for the scarcity of houses, and therefore the increase of rents. Surely you never intended that this Act should strike such a blow at the cottage building industry, and therefore hit the householder?—Yours faithfully,

W. FITRNESS.

"A VARIETY OF PROVISIONS." Mr. Furness received the following reply Treasury Chambers, Whitehall, S.W., lath February, 1914.

Dear Sir,—With reference to your letter of the 7th instant; I am desired by the Chancellor of the Exchequer to say that the Revenue Bill, which was introduced in the House of Com- mons last session, ,but did not become law, con- tained a variety of provisions designed to safe- guard the poSition of builders and developers of land. I am to add that it is the intention of the Government to include similar provisions in the Revenue Bill to be introduced in the cur- rent session.—Yours faithfully,

H. P. HAMILTON:

MAY QUEEN ELECT. Workingmen's Mission Band of Hopg Supplies "Her Majesty." A FOPULAR SELECTION.

The Temperance Queen of Warrington has I been chosen.' Her Majesty" at the forth- Homing festival will be Miss Eva Tanner, a popular young lady, the daughter of Mr. and Mrs. R. Tanner, Winwick-stmit, Warrington. The selection was made at meeting of the May Festival Committee of the Band of Hope Union held on Thursday evening, and it has given the greatest satisfaction. Very popular among her compuninns, the Queen-elect is a member of the Working Men's Mission Band of Hope Society, in whose ~ork she takes a very active interest. She has been associated with the Temperance Festival for many years, and last year occupied the position of Fairy Queen. She is a promising young vocalist, and her services are much sought after in the town and district, especially in Lymm, where her ap- pointment to "rule" over her temperance sub- jects in Warrington will give great satisfaction.

WIFE AIID THE RECEIPT. Forgery Allegettjtgainst a Warrington

Woman. COMMITTED TO THE ASSIZES.

At the Borough Court yesterday, Annie Smith, a married woman, of 17, Lloyd-street, was charged with forging and uttering a certain re- ceipt for money purporting to be signed by her husband, Joseph Smith, for the payment of £lO, and further with forging and uttering a receipt purporting to be signed by Dr. Feroze Itormaz Gandhi, as .an attestor of what was purported to be her husband's signature.. Mr. W. 1.1. J. Jenkins prosecuted on behalf of the Warrington Savings Bank, and Mr. A. Browne defended.

Mr. Jenkins stated the prisoner's husband was a depositor in the Warrington Sayings Bank. On the morning of February 11 prisoner went to the bank and asked for a form of with- drawal, which was handed to her. She stated that her husband was ill in bed and unable to attend himself, so she was instructed that her husband should sign the form, his signature being witnessed by a medical man. In the after- noon of the same day she returned with the form filled in.

===== 0002601_19140221_art0144.txt | included=true | included =====
SIGNED AS "DR."

It was noticed that the attestor's signature read "Dr. F. 11. Gandhi," and this aroused sus- picion, with the result that the form was re- tained, without any money being given, and later a warrant for her arrest was granted. Evidence was given by Mr. J. B. liallwood, clerk at the bank; Councillor Osborne, cashier; and Mr. W. A. Proud, actuary. The latter, in reply to a query by Mr. Browne, said he could not find any rule of the bank which said that an attestor was necessary in a case like the one before the Court, neither could he find any clause in the Savings Bank Act to the same effect. Dr. F. 11. Gandhi stated that the signature purported to be his was not in his handwriting, whilst Mr. Fred Smart, handwriting expert, of Manchester, stated that, in his opinion, the handwriting on the form in question was that of the prisoner. • Sergt. Metcalfe gave evidence of arrest. When he read the warrant to prisoner, she replied to the four charges: "I won't say anything. / offered it. I did write it. I did take it:" Mr. Browne asked if the prisoner's husband did not say he had authorised his wife to sign the form, to which the officer replied that they both professed absolute ignorance when he went to the house with the warrant.

===== 0002601_19140221_art0145.txt | included=true | included =====
"THERE WAS NO RULE."

mr. Browne said he thought he wa.s going to hear the police officer say that the husband had said he had authorised his wife to get the money. He submitted that there was authority from the husband, and if there was that authority the charge fell to the ground. There was no rule that there should be an attestor. The Bench committed prisoner to the Liver- pool Assizes, bail being allowed in two sureties of £5O each.

Mr. Browne submitted that the ainount was too high for a working man's wife, but the Bench held to their decision.

Perhaps he would say then that in the interests of peace, happiness and good management, it would be a wise policy to adopt the principles of Home Rule. (Laughter and applause.)

LIST OF MOURNERS AND WREATHS.

No better tribute of respect to the memory of the late Mr. T. 11. Lyon, of Appleton Hall, could have been paid than that of the assembling of such a large number of people for the funeral on Tuesday, for the afternoon was miserably wet and bitterly cold—a condition which none without a'sfncere—regard for the We gentleman would, have Withstood. The coffin. covered by a Union jack, was car- ried from the Hall to St. Matthew's Church, Stretton, on an estate waggon, diwed in purple, and was escorted by' a large number of the ten- antry and estate employes, and, by the house party. The remains had, during the week-end, been reposed on a table in the billiard room, which, for the time being, had been transformed into a chapel. At the four cerners of the table stood huge palm trees, which shot out their foliage over, the coffin, and in addition there was a number of pretty flowers. A small altar, with the deceased's Bible upon it, had been erected, the background being supplied by the coffin lid, with its large brass cross. The whole arrangeng4 with the lighted candles, was an imposing sp...Pole, and numerous friends, neighbours and ill.mployes witnessed the lying-in- state on Sunday. The vicinity of the church was thronged with people long before the time of the funeral. THE SERVICE. At the lych-gate the remains were met by the clergy and choir, who proceeded up the centre aisle to the strains of the Dead March in "Saul." The clergy who took part in the ser-

vice were the Rev. and Hon. C• F. Cross, vicar of Stretton ; the Rev. Canon F. W. Willis, rector of Warrington; the Rev. Percy Stewart, rector of West Derby. and nephew of the late Mr. Lyon, and the Rev. F. E. Evans, vicar of Apple- ton Thorn. Very impressively was the psalm "Lord, Thou hast been our refuge," chanted, and the singing of "On the resurrection morn- ing" as a recessional hymn concluded the ser- vice in the church.

The procession proceeded to the grave near the east end of the church, and following the last rites, the assembly sang the hymn "Now the labourer's task is o'er." THE MOURNERS.
