TOVVNSIIIP ME-TATTLE.

Wild stories are circulating About the wildness of Sunday's gale. - One "Heathen" says he had two Rats blown away, and lost all in The brief space of half-an-hour. Another states that he was three times Blown off his feet, but was unhurt. A third tells of six windows in his house Being blown in with one gust of wind. The windows were on two sides of the house., It must really have been a great whirlwind. We have been challenged by a "Heathen" to Beat these "facts" if we possibly can. Well, here goes. Our umbrella was blown Out of our hands at Victoria-square And came to ground in Wilderspool-causeway. We escaped unhurt—not a rib broken. Doubtless other tall tales have been told, but It is absolutely true that a quantity of heavy Timber was blown into the Ship Canal. Imateur gardeners are now hustling around. They are going in for a lot of spade work. Surely this is a sign that Spring is coming. The curtailed morning tram service begins ta-morrow. • There is already much grumbling about it. It will certainly prove very awkward for People desirous of going to church in War- rington. The parishioners " tea-d" this week-end. It was a most enjoyable function in every way. Found on the street: "If Stockton Heath "Has had no snow it now has the snow- drop." Evidently' one punster is still alone and about.

Lecture to Scholars.—An interesting lecture 1.4?0n Ireland was delivered by Mr. Brookes, the Iteadrati,Lter of the Stockton Heath Couner„ grilool, to his pupils and others, on Wednesday evening. The Lost Sheep.—George Deigton, 2, Elles- mere-road, Stockton Heath, was summoned at the Warrington County Court on Wednesday with being the owner of certain sheep found straying on the highway in Myddletort. Mr. Browne defended and said the defendant was one of the largest im- porters and sheep dealers in England, and he 13t sheep out to be leyed by different farmers at so much per week. Fifty-four sheep were let oat to Mr. Gilbert Marsh, Myddleton,•and they had got out of the premises. It was common knowledge that the farmer was responsible for the time being.—The Clerk ruled against this statement. and said the defendant was the owner within the meaning of the Act.—Mr. Browne said that if a technical offence had been committed he thought justice would be met on payment of costs.—The case was dismissed on payment of costs.

WOMAN IN THE CANAL. Depression Leads to Suicide.

A pathetic story of the untimely end of a Stockton Heath woman in the Ship Canal was narrated at the inquest held by Coroner •T. Ridgway, at the Stockton Heath Police Court on Monday afternoon. The victim was Clara Mather (45), wife of John Mather, 27, Warbur- ton-street. She was found drowned in the Man- chester Ship Canal early on Saturday morning. Arthur Mather, a son, said he last saw his mother alive on Friday night. His father was an invalid, suffering from consumption, and his mother had been depressed about her husband's condition.

Eva Mather said on Saturday morning when she was in bed, her mother came into her room at 2.40 for the candle. Witness asked her what she was going down for, and she said, " I am just going to get father a drink." Witness heard her go downstairs. \ Her mother came upstairs and, re-entering the room, took what witness thought was a black coat out of the wardrobe. She heard her mother close the door downstairs, but thought she had only gone down to shut it, as it sometimes came open.

-Witness got up and looked out of the window, but she could not see her mother. She went downstairs and could not find her. The front door was open. P.C. Richardson described recovering the body from the Ship Canal at 7.15 a.m. He first found a cap on the canal bank. The body was lying at a spot about 12 or 15 yards to the right of the end of Warburton-street.

Mr. Wallhead said he understood Mrs. Mather had been depressed, and that a recent family trouble added to this.

The jury returned a verdict of "Suicide dur- ing.temporary insanity." _

The Foreman described the case as very un- fortunate, and expressed sympathy with the relatives.

PAROCHIAL TEA. _ Children Score in an Attractive Entertainment.

The annual parochial tea party promoted in connection with St. Thomas' Church, Stockton Heath, was held in the Mission Room, Victoria+ square, on Thursday evening, and was enjoyed by a large number of parishioners. After the tea, at which a number of lady members of the congregation helped, an interesting programme was submitted.

In a cantata. "Peeps into Fairy-4nd," the Sunday School scholars acquitted themselyes very well indeed, the setting of the pictures, the dancing and singing of the children calling for special commendation. Mr. H. M. Ashton pleased with his character sketch, "Mrs. Murphy on llonsty," his char- woman make-up—being very clever; whilst Mr. Walter Moores also caused much merriment by the aid of his repertoire of songs. Miss Rhoda Hamman was a neat dancer, especially so in her "umbrella" dance. "Dear little jammy face" was a sweet little song sung by little Miss Teare, who was obliged to respond to an encores A character sketch entitled "Geese" was very pleasingly and amusingly given by the Misses G. Henshall arid B. Forshaw, who received a , warm applause. , The Vicar (the Rev. James Collins) apologised .for the absence of several friends, including Mrs. Willet, who was responsible for arranging part of the evening's programme. At the end of last September they concluded the first year of their splf-offering scheme, and although the response had been encouraging. there was room for improvement. They looked Upon the penny of the poor man with as much pleasure as the sovereign of ,the rich, so they had no need to think, because they had but little money, they could not help. He proposed a comprehensive vote of, thanks, which was heartily carried.

MOORE.

Poultry Lecture.—On Thursday evening Mr F. C. Gill; Penketh, gave an interesting lecture on "Poultry for pleasure and profit" to an enthusiastic gathering of poultry keepers, at the Labour Club. After quoting figures ,of,, foreign imports, which showed we were receiv- ing 2,000 millions of eggs from abroad, he ap- pealed for a better study of utility breeding, and knowledge of foods, stating that a I better result could be obtained at a cheaper cost than by the present careless system which is now so prevalent. The management of poultry to pro- duce the greatest number of eggs was fully dealt with, and the analyses of different foods ,were given, along with advice as to mating, musing, &c. Many questions asked were iswered at the close.

LINES FROM LYMM.

"Good fellows" the traders proved. They spent a jolly good time At their Association's annual dinner. Who told the Blackpool ilea story? Didn't it set the company laughing. • The President made a good suggestion That in the absence of a Ratepayers' Aseocia- don The Tradesmen's Association should help in The District Council elections, not by choos- ing Candidates, but by holding public meetings. But why not a tradesman candidate? The Choral Society scored a great success. Their concert was a musical treat. The Rector is very puzzling with his Continual references to "a little epidemic." What is it—an epidemic of oratory?

On the Bench.—The Petty Sessions were held at the Police Buildings, Lymm, on Wednes- day morning; when Mr. John Coxon was in the chair. Mr. T. J. Ridgway was the only other magistrate present.

The Showman's lied.—" You must go to prison fur 14 days. This is a very serious thing, for this is how farm fires sometimes occur," said Mr. T. J. Ridgway to a young man named Patrick Collin, of no settled abode, who appeared at the Lyram Petty Sessions for sleeping out without visible means of subsistence.—P.C. Atherton stated he saw prisoner enter .an outbuilding of Norcott's Farm.—The Clerk: Had he anything in his possessionP—A few matches, lie was striking some when I saw him, for he was find- ing his way into the building.—Prisoner, who 'stated he had been working for a travelling show, had recently been liberated from Knuts- ford Gaol.

A Nameless Dog.—At the Lyn= Petty Ses- sions on Tuesday, Anthony Gleasner Colt, of Eoxley. Cottage,:Broora Edge, , was, charged, with allowing his dog to be at large, and also with omitting to have the name and address of the owner attached to the collar.—A polio officer said he was on duty in Wavington-road, Broom Edge, when he saw a brown dog, which ap- peared to be lost. There was no name on the collar. The dog was claimed three days after- wards.—Mr. John Grace, solicitor, of Manches- ter, appeared to defend, and said that a metal disc bearing the owner's name and address had by some means been detached from the collar.— A fine of 2s: 6d., including costs, was imposed.

Dispute Over Motor Car.—At the Warrington County Court on Thursday, before Judge Stanger, Messrs. Holt Brothers, Broom Edgev Lymm, sued Sydney Marsh, rubber dealer, Broom Edge, for £l2 ss. Bd., the balance of an account of ..£1.3 ss. Bd., for repairs *and over., hauling of a motor car.—Defendant said the work was not properly done and complained of the time taken by the plaintiffs to do the re- pairs. He had paid .211 of the account in October. He thought he had paid sufficient for the work done. He had to pay another mech- anic to do work to the car.—Plaintiffs said the car was all right when it left their works, and defendant had tried it before he took it away. They reduced the claim to £ll ss. &L—Judgment was entered for that sum accordingly.—Mr. Kay appeared for the plaintiff firm. •

A "Technical Offence."—At the local Petty Sessions on Wednesday , William Jackson, a motor driver, in the employ of Messrs. Leach' and Seed, Manchester, was charged with failing to have the registered, weight painted on the right or off side of his Motor dray—Mr. John Grace, solicitor, Manchester, defended, and pleaded guilty.—Acting-Sergeant Robinson de- tailed stopping defendant for having no rear light, and he also discovered that the unloaded weight of the vehicle was not painted 'upon it.-- For the defence it was stated that a prospective owner was intending to have certain alterations made to the vehicle, and until that was done they could not have definitely given the un- loaded weight. It was a highly technical offence which was not attended by any serious conse- cluences.-7A fine of 10s., including costs, was imposed.—For not having his rear lamp lit the charge was dismissed on payment of costs.

Missionary An ni versary.—The Missionary anniversary sermons at the Primitive Methodist Church were preached on Sunday last by the Rev. R. W. Ferguson, who chose for his morn- ing subject "A Divine Commission," when he showed his congregation how they could carry out Christ's injunction only by the aid of His divine power. He preached in the evening from the text, To what purpose is this waste?" The minister said that whilst the Missionary enterprise opened up the geograph- ical knowledge of a country, its people* customs, languages, natural history, commerce, &c., the sovereign purpose was that of spreading abroad a universal religion of ,which love was the motive. Ile also briefly dealt with three w.ards expressive of the Missionary mandate, "Come, "Abide," Go." In the afternoon a young people's service was held under the presidency of Mr. Ernest Moss, when addresses were given by Miss Welford, the Rev. R. W. Ferguson, Mr.' W. Downward, and Ur. W. A. Southall.—The annual missionary meeting took place on Mon- day evening, Mr. G. L. Welford occupying the chair. The Rev. W. Daw- gave a summary of the Connexional annual report of the Home and African Missions, making special reference to their new venture in the form of a Medical Mission. The deputation was the Rev. T. W. Haticox, of Oron, Africa, who gave a most in- teresting address on his work in connection with the Oron Institute. He gave a graphic illustra- tion of the suspicion and distrust which the natives have towards anything new, and which constitutes one of the strongest barriers the missionaries hav‘ to break down. He said that the best way to win the Africans was through African, and his account of the training of native teachers in the Institute and its wonder- tui growth during the past seven years was most encouraging. On the proposition of the Rev, W. Dew, a vote of thanks was accorded the deputation, Chairman, Mrs. Edwards, Miss Eva Tonge, and othzr collectors.

EXAAMER,

LAYMM.

THE TRADERS DINE. Novel Suggestion by the President of the Association.

INTEREST IN COUNCIL V*ORK.

The tradesmen of Lymm at dinner assembled once more proved themselves to be "good fellows" on Wednesday night. The occasion was the annual dinner of the Tradesmen's Asso- ciation, held at the Spread Eagle Hotel. The new President, Mr. D. Daniels, presided, and supporting him were Councillors R. H. Gotobed, J. W. Dixon, Rev. J. B. Thurston, S. Davies, W. G. Groves, J. Warburton, J. H. Holt and H. Mercer; Messrs. W. E. Hillier, secretary; A. B. Richardson, treasurer; H. S. Saville, J. Price, W. K. Kershaw, F. E. Evans, G. H. Parker and Newton Worthington. The secretary read .apologies for non-attendance from Messrs. F. Lockyer, S. W. Gould, a G. Smith, W. Gould, G. L. Welford, and one from Mr. A. Watkin, which included an invitation to the Association members to be his guests at Chester. " LYM M TRADESMEN'S ASSOCIATION." Mr. W. G. Groves proposed this toast, which he thought was the toast 'of the evening. The members of the Lymm Tradesmen's Association combined to help each other, and he was sure it was a very good thing for every—body. He hoped they would prosper in their endeavours of helping one another. The Association had been in existence probably 14 or 15 years, and they could see that it was growing in strength, although 'their finances did not seem to in- crease. The President had said that he (Mr. Groves) was an old Lymm Grey. He had never , lived anywhere else, and he hoped to remain in Lymm until the time came for him to pass the border. (Applause.) FEARED NO CRITICISM. The President, replying from the chair, took the opportunity of thanking the members for their loyalty towards him during the five years he held the post of secretary, and he asked them to be even more loyal to the new secretary, Mr. Hillier. There was a splendid feeling amongst , the members of their Association. (Hear, hear.) He thought it was acknowledged on all sides that they were in a stronger position now than 'in the days past, and he hoped that good feel- lag would continue, <notwithstanding the keen- ness of competition. As an Association they had critics, but they did not fear criticism. He found that it did not require much self-denial, talent, brains, or any character to be a fault- finder. (Hear, hear.) Those who were in earnest and moved by a desire to do good, very. little time for murmuring or comPln- ing. bad in

A Principle. lie had a principle he wanted them to keep in mind as they were engaged in their daily work. What was that principle? Was it duty or On- thusiasra? These were necessary in an asso- ciation like theirs, and he sincerely trusted that they would never be without energetic workers and robust enthusiasts to carry out the aims and objects of their organisation. But the principle he wanted to bring before them was that no one lives, works and toils for himself alone, for either consciously -or unconsciously, willingly or pnwillingly, with thought or without thought, a man's life must have an influence upon those by whom he was surrounded. Man in his work, iii his play and-,intercourse with other-men, was a power to be reckoned with, for he must influ- ence those with whom he came in contact. He _wanted to impress this uponi them. '

• Work Not Finished. It was admitted that they had done good work in the past, and he did not think their work was finished. When he was appointed the president he said he thought the time had, come when their Association might take up the work of trying to influence those to be selected_ as candi- dates at the local elections. The .political parties fought shy, the candidates saying they were not political, but independent candidates.. That was all right, so far as it went, but he did think those Men, whoever they were, should be selected by some representative body. In the absence of a Ratepayers' Association, he suggested that their Association might very well take up the question. (Hear, hear.) He did not say that they should say who should stand, but that they should convene public meetings of ratepayers, at which men could be selected to fight for the places in their local House of Commons. He was particularly anxious that every member should do his best to further the interests of the Association. He wanted everyone of theni'to use their influence and do good in the place in which they lived. Time was short, and shortly they would have to pass the border line, but they should first at- tempt to do all the good they could. (Hear, hear, and applause.)

"TRADE AND •TOWN OF LYMM." The Rev. G. B. Thurston, in submitting this toast, said that if it meant wishing it well, he was quite sure he could propose that toast with the greatest heartiness. He thought that every- body who had to do with Lymm found before long a great affection for the place. He was a great believer in the spirit that rested in old places, and he thought that was the reason they had such an affection for Lymm. He knew that it was much easier for a man to throw a stone at a house than to build it, and critics were in the position of those who threw the stones. He did not think they all realised what a lot of harm' was done by the criticism which Lymm sometimes received. It was with very great regret that he saw so many houses empty, and no doubt that was in some measure the re- sult of criticism. A "Little Epidemic." They had had in the district during the last few months a little epidemic—nothing serious —but critics had magnified the epidemic. St. Paul was not far from the truth when he said that people who knew everything knew nothing at all. It was a parodox, but there was a great deal of truth in it. Mr. Daniels had spoken of the loyalty amongst the members, but that, generally speaking, had not been his experience in anything he had had to do with, for he had found the wotk in the place left to just a few hard-working people, who were too often left to do the work alone. They wanted more of the "loyal heartsnd true "—those people who mis- take. _ d stand by. them when they made k

Mr. 11. S. Saville responded, and stated he remembered the time when there was only one postal despatch and delivery, and when the tele- phone and telegram were beyond the reach of the average man, but then trade was as good, if not better, than it was to-day. The toast of "Our Guests" was honoured, on the proposition of Mr. F. E. Evans, and was responded to by Mr. Gotobed. An excellent programme Auld been arranged, and was given by the following: M6ssrs. Frank- lyn Taylor„ Levi Thomason, A. C. Saville, G. Parker, and Cheasey. The artistes were thanked for their services. •

SUCCESSFUL CHORAL CONCERT. A Musical Treat Provided.

Local music lovers are indebted to the Lymm Choral Society for the excellent programme pro- vided at the concert given in the Drill Hall on Tuesday night. The society gave evidence of careful training and sang with intelligence and ability; the contraltos probably took highest honours, but the singing all round was exuled; ingly good, and Air. J. H. Brazendale, the con- ductor, is to be congratulated on bringing his forces to such a high degree of efficiency, their spirited interpretation of "The Voyagers" being delightfully contrasted with the sweet and flowing rendering of "Night, lovely night." The difficulties of James' "The soul's longing" were surmounted in a most creditable manner, whilst the singing of "0 happy eyes" (Elgar) was so well received that an encore in the form of ,"The Message" had to be given; but it was in "Moonlight" (Eaton Faning), and which was sung "a cappella," that they reached their [I highest level. sang three part-songs "Fly, singing bird," "The snow," and "Cleansing fires," being rein- forced in the two former pieces by the violins of Miss Hill and Mr. Andrew. All the items merited the unstinted applause given. The only lady soloist was Miss Doris Buckley. Though of tender years, she has already won laurels at -the Blackpool Festival. This is, we think, her first appearance in Lyram, but there can be no doubt about the sincerity with which the audience received her 'selections, "Pleading,", "A birthday," "Flower song," and, as an encore, "When all was young." Her voice yet lacks the power of maturity, but its sweet and sympathetic qualities are already apparent. Mr. Hamilton Harris is a baritone with a fine chest voice, which fortunately he knows how to use to the best advantage; but it is on his highly developed voce mista that Mr. Harris' relies for his finest effects, and his delivering of the softer passages in his songs was extremely fine. The last few notes of his lower register are not good, but as we only heard them in his first song, "Who treads the path of duty," their somewhat harsh effect was soon effaced by ,the unmixed pleasure derived from his subsequent efforts, in fact after the singing of a trio of songs, "The sandman," "Dream in the twi- light," and "Had a horse," an encore was de- manded and given. His contribution of "All that I ask" resulted in "Pals" being given as an additional piece.

It is always a pleasure to find Mr. Andrews' name in the programme, and on Tuesday even- ing he quite played up to his reputation, purity of tone and facility of execution distinguishing each of his selections. We should like to hear this gifted young artiste on a really good instru- ment, the one he used on this occasion, and on his last visit to Lymm, being either mediocre in quality or suffered fromathe sound post being placed too far below the 'ridge. His items comprised "Air vane," "Lurline," and, as an encore, Rubenstein's Melody in F. A.duet by Miss Buckley and Mr. Harris, ”Still as the night," was heartily applauded. The society were fortunate in securing Mr. T. M. Eastham, their former conductor, to perform the duties of accompanist, and his skill in this department contributed in no small degree to the- artistic success of the concert. The singing, of the National Anthem brought to a close One of the most enjoyable concerts held in Lymm for some time, and its success should greatly encourage the members of the society and its indefatigable honorary secretary, Mr. F. Champ.

WOMEN'S SUFFRAGE. Lymm Ladies Deeply Interested in the Topic.

"Women's Suffrage" was the subject of the speeches delivered by Miss Crosfield and Miss Hubback at the Lymm and District Women's Liberal Association meeting, held in the Eagle Brow Schoolroom on Thursday evening. Mrs. Dodds, who presided over a good attend- ance. expressed her keen interest in the question of women's suffrage, which she considered was a religious as well as a political matter. She was confident that women would get the vote, and by doing so would provide great support to., temperance and other good movements.

THE THREE PROPOSALS. Miss Crosfield briefly outlined the three pres- ent proposals relating to women's suffrage, namely: (1) The Conciliation Bill; (2) Mr. Dick- enson's, or the Norwegian Bill; (3) the Adult Suffrage Bill.

_ In reply to the question as to whether the passing of a Suffrage Bill would increase the Liberal majority, the speaker said that if it were a wise and just thing it ought to be made law, apart from the possible way it may influ- ence the vote.

She gave• the history of the Suffrage move- ment as found in Norway, and said that amongst the leading reforms carried ()lit since its introduction seven years ago were the re- duction in infantile mortality, granting of ex- cellent maternity benefits, rendering of sex equality in the law and other courts, compul- sory training for young women in hygiene, &c., and the making for the cause of peace.

===== 0002601_19140221_art0163.txt | included=true | included =====
Women Equal to Men.

In stating the reasons in favour of Womea's Suffrage, Miss Crosfield claimed that women were equal to men in character and intelligence, and as regards the question of physical force being necessary for a vote, she asked " i7hy is it that soldiers and naval men are so handi- capped in exercising the franchise?" She be: peved it was better to cook than to fight, though she recognised that each should have their own sphere of work. Speaking of social legislation, she said: " We want the franchise in order to protect the girls and working women of the country" She quoted Viss Constance Smith's figures as to the number of females and their wages in various industrial and sweated condi- tions, and said that their text was "Equal pay for equal work." The speaker desired that sweating should be stopped, and that women should be considered as capable citizens.

===== 0002601_19140221_art0164.txt | included=true | included =====
POPULAR FALLACY.

Miss Hubback prefaced her address-by saying that she was not a Liberal, and thought it very kind of the committee to allow her to express her views which she regarded as further evi- dence of the non-political nature of the Suffrage question. She confined, her address largely to showing the feasibility of Women's Suffrage be- coming law in this country, and gave examples of the satisfactory working of the system in countries where women have a vote, namely.: In America, nine States and one Territory; in Europe, Norway and Finland; and in the Brit- ish Colonies of New Zealand and Australia. The speaker proceeded to show the fallacy of such objections to Women's Suffrage as (1) "That women would neglect their homes in the interest of their vote"; (2) "'That it would create a divi- sion between husband and wife"; (3) Would women use the vote?"—by giving the state of- affairs as found in the above suffrage countries. Speaking of results as shown by legislation, she said these were directed chiefly to the im- provement of ,social laws and enumerated such Acts that had been passed in relation to improv- ing the condition of the home, children, educa- tion, wages and working hours, the provision of pensions for widows, rental rights, purer food, and the abolishment of sweating.

vote of thanks to the speakers and president was carried with acclamation, on the proposition of Miss Rawlins.

DARESBURY. THE RINGING OF TIIE BELLS.

• A Three Hours' Merry Peal.

To dedicate the new bell at Daresbury Parish Church on Sunday there were special services. In the afternoon members of the Chester Dio- cesan Guild of Bellringers rang a peal consisting of 5,008 changes of bob major, in three hours and 12 minutes, standing as follows :—F. Smith (London), treble; E. H. Lewis (Frodsham), 2; A. Ryder (Daresbury),• 3; F. TJnsworth (Frod- sham), 4; W. Bibby (Frodsham), 5; F. Gleake (Frodsham), 6; T. H. Lightfoot (Frodsham), 7; C. F. White (London), tenor. The peal was composed by Mr. E. M. Atkins, and conducted by Mr. E. H. Lewis. Messrs. Smith and White are members of the staff of ,the founder S of the bells. After evensong 209 grandsire triples were rung, with Mr. T. Houghton on the tenor bell.

===== 0002601_19140221_art0166.txt | included=true | included =====
BELL INSCRIPTIONS.

The inscriptions on the bells are: Tenor, "Given by Mary Milner, 1913, to commemorate the Coronation of George V., June 22nd, 1911." Treble, "In memory of Rev. Thomas Whitley, vicar 1884-1896. Given by his widow, Anne Catherine Whitley and his son, John James Whitley, 413." On six old bells which have been recast: No. 1, "Peace and good neighbour- hood, 1725"; No. 2, "Sir G. Greenall, patron, Rev. T. T. Lee-Jones, vicar. J. J. Whitley and C. H. Tinsley, wardens, 1913"; No. 3, "Prosper- ity to the Church of England, 1725"; No. 4, " Prosperity to the parish, 1808"; No. 5, "Peter Woods, gent., and Saml. Burrows, church- wardens, 1725"; No. 6, "Cast by John Warner and Sons, London, 1874; recast by J. Warner and Sons, 1913."

===== 0002601_19140221_art0167.txt | included=true | included =====
THE DEDICATION.

The dedication service took place in the morn- ing, and the special preacher was Canon BinneY, of Chester. The bells, he said, pro- claimed aloud the importance and need of wor- ship, which was man's privilege. People had ceased to go to church, largely because the idea of worship had been lost, and the bells ring out their message, "Come and worship, come and worship, Christ the Bing," but the response had been small. To hear beautiful music, to listen to a popular preacher, to satisfy the dic- tates of conscience were reasons why certain persons were more or less regular in Attending the House of God, but there was something higher and grander—the adoration which the whole being could give to the Creator, Redeemer, Sanctifier, the thankful remem- brance of the inestimable love of God. The bells were also a call to remembrance. It was not possible for everyone to attend public worship whenever it was Offered, but everyone might be reminded that he lived in a Christian country, and that them was the claim of God upon him when he heard the church bells.

TABLE TALK.

The World's Tribute of Interesting

Items of News.

QUAINT AND AMUSING.

Unique among the oil fields of the world, says the "Engineering and Mining Journal,, is the Summerland group of wells in California, Which extend out into the Pacific Ocean. There are 141 producing wells in this group, which is situ- ated about 18 miles from Santa Barbara. These wells are drilled out in the ocean just off the coast, and 100,000 barrels are taken from the oil sands underlying the ocean bed every year. This interesting spectacle of derricks built out into the sea is visible from the train on the way from Los Angeles to San Francisco.

Facts About Deafness. According to a French statistician, males are more subject to aural diseases than females, and out of every seven middle-aged persons there are two who do not hear so well with one ear as with the other. In every thousand children under 15 years of age, 4 per cent. show symp- toms of ear disease, and 6 per cent, a marked deficiency in hearing power. The liability of disease increases from birth to the age of forty, and then begins gradually to decrease as old age adYances. Out of the total number of casr subjected to surgical treatment in France n one year it is estimated that about 57 per cent. i were cured and 30 per cent. permanently m- proved.

The" Bachelor "Butcher

"If the newspapers publish notices of iny ' marriage my business will be ruined," said a man who applied for a marriage licence at Mil- waukee, and begged the county clerk to sup- press the news. "I own a butcher's shop and am coining money," he said. "There are more than 300 unmarried young women living in my neighbourhood. Because everybody knows that I have money the match-making mothers send their girls round to my shop with a view to get- ting me interested. But if they found out I was married, they would get mad and boycott me. When I get married I am going to intro- duce •my wife as a new hired girl." County Clerk Widule agreed to suppress the man's name.

===== 0002601_19140221_art0170.txt | included=true | included =====
Forty-two Billion Dollars in Debt.

According to figures compiled by the Bureau of Foreign and Domestic Commerce, United States Department of Commerce, the national debts of the world now aggregate 42 billion dol- lars, haring increased 20 per cent. in the last decade and doubled in the last 40 years. The interest and other annual charges amounted in 1912 to 1,732 million dollars. The largest national debt is that of France- -6,284 million dollars; the next largest being Russia, 4,553 million; the United Kingdom, 3,486 million; Italy, 2,707 million; Spain, 1,815 mil- lion; British India, 1,475 million; Japan, 1,242 million, and the United States 1,028 million. The debt of the German Empire is stated at 1,178 million dollars, and of the German Slates, 3,736 million; that of Austria-Hungary, 1,051 million; that of—Austria, 1,434 million, and of Hungary, 1,268 million dollars.

A PEARSO.N AND KNOWLES' COLLIERY FIRE.

Damage to the extent of £l,OOO was done on Tuesday by a fire -at Moss Pits, Inca, near Wigan, belonging to Messrs. Pearson and Knowles Coal and Iron Co., Ltd. The mischief . was first noticed on the surface belts, and when the brigade arrived the fire had secured a good hold of the wooden erections which surround 1 the head gear. There were 60 or 70 men in the two pits, and their safety was threatened br reason of the risk of the head gear and engine house becoming involved in the fire. Mr. It. Owen, the manager, descended the shaft, whicb was rapidly filling with dense smoke, and he succeeded. in getting the men out. The pa e sa- . .. . _

INVENTORS' FORTUNES.

It is not always the greatest invention that brings the largest financial rewards (says "Lea Nouvelles"). Roller skates are said to have brought their inventor £600,000, while nearlY half a million was realised by the man who first devised boot laces. The inventor of the safetY pin, who took the idea from a reproduction of a Pompeian fresco, made £2,000,000. On the other hand, Charles Bourseul, who discovered and described the principle of the telephone in 1855, died poor. Michaux, the inventor of the bicycle, ended his days in the utmost penury, and Frederic Sauvage, who is credited with the in- vention of the screw-propeller, was imprisoned' and died bankrupt and insane.

Vicissitudes

Nonoonformity.

THE RISE AND WORK OF THE WARRINGTON

ACADEMY.

BY R. S. ROBSO.N

1.-ANCESTRY.

Presbyterianism was strong in Warrington during the Civil War and the Commonwealth. It Was the seat of one of the Classis or Presby- teries into which Lancashire was divided on the setting up of the Presbyterian system of government in the National Church in 164 g. Of the nine Classis into which the county was divided, that of Warrington was the fourth. Shaw's "English Church," 1640-60, contains details as to the composition of these Classis, and the names of the ministers and elders who comprised them are given, those of Warrington among the number. Some of the minutes are still extant, dating from 1646 to 1660, and again from 1689 till the beginning of the eighteenth century. The first of these three dates saw the system legalised, the second saw it superseded at the Restoration and the Act of Uniformity of 1662, and the third saw its toleration as a Dissenting interest at the Revolution. The only point of difference between these Presbyterians of the 17th century and their modern descendants and representatives is that the former were Eras- tia.ns—held the principle of the State connec- tion—whereas the latter have ceased to hold it as an essential. In all else they are identical, ! and the praise, confessions of faith, standards ' of doctrine, and directory of government, drawn up for and subscribed to by the former, in the Westminster Assembly, and in use then, are the same to which the present Presbyterian minis- ter in Warrington subscribed at his ordination last year. In addition to continuity in— wor- ship, doctrine and government, we think that Warrington Presbyterianism has a claim to con- tinuity of existence and location in the town. Of course, there are weak links in the chain, Periods when it was driven under by ecclesias- tical persecution in the 17th century, and at the end of the following, when, to preserve the evan- gelical tradit ion, it had to make common cause with those of /Wail e r communion, but the thin blue line always obtained, and once more, and in particular of late, its adherents are once again formed in rank as a regiment in the Chris- tian army, and fronting the common foe. The 'Warrington Presbyterians of the Common- wealth were succeeded by those of the Ejection of 1662, when the vicar of the parish, driven from his pulpit and vicarage, became the founder of the Presbyterians as a Dissenting interest. Ile an his ,followers, who met in secret, are noted in the Archbistiops' Coven- tide Reports of 1665, and the latter in 1669, under other ejected ministers, who made the town their home and maintained Worship and the sacraments (Luring the persecuting period under the Act of Uniformity and its bye-pro- ducts. They came to the surface ten years after that Act, under the "King's indulgence" of 1672-3, when they had a measure of protection in their worship, and were allowed to license their „ meetings" and their "teachers." I believe the original licence for the ejected vicar is still IPresbyterianll. aDissenters. Bate, n in existence. "The Court House licensed as a Yates,meetingddition teacher," placefor a 1 his recent volume, "The King's lndulgence," gives:

" Lancashire.—Warrington licences No. 77, 78 and 79, for the houses of Rebecca Neild, Samuel Leach and Samuel Nicholls, all as Presbyterian meeting houses."

' This is one of a few instances where a licence was granted to a public building. Usually it was the house of the minister or some member of the congregation. Other references to this period are that of the "Essex Hall Year Book," which gives the date of foundation of the con- gregation of Cairo-street as 1662, having refer- ence to its inception at the Ejection, and that in Evans's " Vestiges of Protestant Dissent." which gives the foundation as 1672, the date of the licence mentioned. Driven under once more by the withdrawal of the " Indulgence," in 1673, the different " meetings" maintained the secret ministry of the ejected clergy till the second "Indulgence" of King James in 1688, and the Revolution of 1689 brought the religious liberty, or the measure of it, known as the Toleration, when they were merged in the " meeting " held in a domestic building off the Market-place,, where their history as a stated congregation began, and which was first adapted for wor- ship, then was later rebuilt as a chapel.

11.— SAN KEY-STREET. The local Press described this in 1884 as San- key Chapel, but "meeting" was the term then in general use. This is confirmed by the refer- ence in the " Non-Parochial Register Report" in 1836, where the name of the first location is given as Sankey-street. Its rebuilding was owing to the increase of the congregation under a popular ministry, when the cause in Warring- ton, like others in the " Golden Age" of chapel building, were exulting in their new-found re- ligions liberties. Evans's "Vestiges" gives it e bliis•setrieti 'ic)l(iii,• , church built, 1703," with an added CUPS inscribed i'i'on the communion plate' comprising "Two h and " Two plates inscribed." "Lists of Di We find mention of them tle7rli7e-2119,yieviliiiiI(d•lettleer withrts ise7emr` years later in Evans's tvirisioigineChapels," from 13 hearrington—Presby- u7itcearnsc mheemarbeerrs; aolfaurlceoarCslihseef; eNelloitlysoe:llraililBl(g2l giving a good idea of the status ahroetheocilinnitmy the second entry of the congregation. It is evident that the older registers of bap- tisms and burials•are missing, for on the appeal of the Government for such to be forwarded to Somerset House in 1836, they could only produce "one register of baptisms and of burials from 1771 to 1836. e As to the meetings themselves, they ivere mainly of one pattern, and that at VVarringlon would conform to the type. Externally it wilt' an oblong building with a high pitched roof, a hip-roof, covered with pantiles, from which their frequenters were sometimes called " pantilers." The material was stone, or brick faced with stone. The only external room, where the ac- commodation did not run to a vestry, was the pent house, or porch. Internally there were the . heavy pulpit ,with its sounding board on one of the long side walls, the brass hoop for the bap- ' tismal basin, by the pulpit rail, the prepentoes desk, or more commonly the singing pew, in front of the pulpit, with the communion table in the centre; the heavy, deep galleries round three sides. sometimes round all four. In the corners downstairs were the " family pews," in a double sense, lined with green bilze,,studded with brass nails. Sankey-street Meeting , suffered during the Ifigh Church Riots of 1710 known as the "Sacheverill Riots," though not to the extent that some of the other Presbyterian meeting houses in Lancashire did, that at Cross-street, Manchester, among the number. It is not often given to the same man to have two churches built to accommodate the fruit's of his ministry, but it was so in the case of War- rington, where the Sankey-street Meeting of 1703 was replaced over 40 years later by the Cairo- street Meeting of 1745, which, with enlarge- ments of the original burial ground, aml restoration of the original building, continues in occupation to-day.

lII.—CAIRO-STREET. The use of the old name seems to lave obtaiuccl for 'citrtsi cfereAce to the new bad-

===== 0002601_19140221_art0177.txt | included=true | included =====
1, Newcastle-on-Tyne.

ing, which continued to be called " Sankey " Chapel, according to the " Registers' Report,” which, like the " Vestiges " of Evahs, and the "Petition in the Chapels Bill" in 1844, gives the date of its erection as 1745. It continued for a generation more to be the only meeting place for Protestant Nonconformists in the town.

According to Thompson's "Lists of Dissent- ing Chapels," which did for Nonconformity in 1773 what Evans had done in 1717, and which then reported " Warrington with one chapel and one minister," this was not long to con- tinue. Apart from the advent of Methodism, the Orthodox teaching, which had obtained in the successors of ministry from 1643 to beyond the middle of the following century, gradually underwent a change from that time forward, till it reached, by way, first of Arian and later of Unit-Arian doctrine, that latter stage in which it • still continues. In the beginning of 1779 there was a secession on the part of some, to whom the Liberal Theology did not recom- mend itself. The congregation had been origin- ally composed both of Presbyterian and Inde- pendent sections, and the seceders were of the latter "way," by tradition at least, and Stepney Chapel, which they proceeded to erect, gave visibility to Independency in the town. The record of the formation of the congregation meeting in what • was then Stepney Chapel [on a site now occupied by the shop of Mr. Davies, tinsmith,. in King-street]; as given in their "Covenant," bears date December 17, 1797, and the first entry in the register of baptisms is on january 31, 1798. Their descendants in 1851 erected the first Wycliffe Church, succeeded by the present building in 1872. A secession from Stepney Chapel in 1811 was known by the name of their building, Salem Chapel, later known as Golborne-street Chapel, whom we shall meet again later.

Meanwhile Cairo-street continued to shelter Presbyterians of all shades of opinion down to .the beginning of the 19th century, till the rise Of the New Foundation of St. John's in 1806, which saw the last of Orthodoxy depart. Then Unitarian worship was made legal in 181:1; and in 1844 their petition for the " Chapels Bill," to secure them against any Orthodox claim on chapel or endowment reads: " Foundation of chapel 1745; Anti-Trinitarian in all living memory; burial ground enlarged 1797; schools'built 1844."

===== 0002601_19140221_art0178.txt | included=true | included =====
IV.-THE ACADEMY.

Warrington Academy is unknown to the great majority of Nonconformists, yet as frequently R 8 in their missionary meetings they sing that Imperial hymn, "Jesus shall reign" to the tune " Warrington," they celebrate its name, for it was composed in honour of his Academy by one of its principals, the Rev. John Harrison. Its history is contemporary with that of Cairo- street. It was started as a university for Liberal Nonconformity, and during the genera- tion it existed trained nearly 400 stu- dents, between 1757 and its dissolution in 1786. Its principals and professors were the flower of Nonconformity---intellectually,—including. as they did, Dr. John Taylor, the famous Hebrew scholar and opponent of Wesley; Dr. Priestley, the scientist, whose chair is preserved at Cairo- street ; Dr. A ikin. a great and good man, father of two daughters who made a mark in English literature; Dr. Enfield, Dr. Reinhold Forster, Gilbert Wakefield, John Howard, John Mac- gown'', to mention none of the eminent men who sat under their teaching, nor the literary works, which are the memorials of them and their pro- fessors. The preachers it turned out were largely responsible for the theological position of English Presbyterianism at the end of the 18th century, but even Warrington was not without its Orthodox output. The seeds of decay seem to have been inher- ent in the institution. The students, young on their entrance, were treated as men, religious conduct was no part of their burden, and there was no discipline, they were thrown together in a large sequestered house, the womenkind of the professors being allowed access, as part of the amenities of the place. On a refusal of the Senate to allow private theatricals the students sulked. On another occasion the two daughters of the house steward invited them to supper— hams, potted beef, trifles, &c., being placed before them, but the hams were made of wood, .the potted beef of potted sawdust, and the trifles of soapsuds, other luxuries being equally tempting and tantalising. The students spent their money freely; the father of Benjamin Vaughan, a budding M.P., wrote from Bristol to the head to complain "that his two sons' expenses were nearly suffi- cient for the University, that he had sent them to Warrington to avoid 'the reigning 'contagion of a dissijiated age,' but that on their return they would not appear without having their hair frisseml, and that must be done by a dresser, even on the Sabbath." This state of things, though harmless in the arly days, in- creasing; failed to produce that religious ideal and temperament which the work and day of its alumni called for, and after much contention between trustees and tutors the Academy was suspended, and finally dissolved as above. Manchester Academy, founded the same year, was its successor, and in turn this was followed It cbayntnloi et ii:eressaeindt taini eel ersttaeirntCy otl illeag, Oxford. that oei nas or anything dogmatic, was taught at Warring- ton. The Professors gave liberty amounting to latitude, but certain it was that many of the students took up that position later.

V.—ST. JOHN'S, WINWICK-STREET. One of the little known chapters in English Post Reformation Church History is that deal- ing with the links between Presbyterianism and the Connection known as the Countess of Hunt-

Both in their essence as far as their founders, the Puritans in the one case and the Calvinistic Methodist preachers in the other, were an evangelical protest from the Established Church. Whitfield, the first chaplain and min- ister of the second body, was also the first moderator of the Welsh Presbyterian Church. Again, when the College at Trevecea was left for the newer foundation at Cheshunt, the Welsh Presbyterians took it over, and. it continued, along with Bala, to be their School of the Pr'opliets till quite recent times. When the foundress took it over, one of the things he insisted upon was that the well-known Presby- terian theologian, John Brown, of lladdington, one of a Ministerial race, should transcribe for use among the students there his "System of Divinity." The two were friends and corre- spondents,' the latter to be found in the "Memoirs and Remains" of the Rev. John Brown, 1856. The last chapel in which Whitfield preached

===== 0002601_19140221_art0179.txt | included=true | included =====
SIGErs mu?

in London was included later in the English Tli E \7%TØ RLD OF LETTERS Presbyterian Synod. The present Presbyterian 4 Church in Gloucester was formed by a union between his followers and the Presbyterians, who built the Whitfield Memorial on the spot there where he preached his first open-air ser- mon, and other Presbyterian congregations in England, such as Lewes, Cheltenham, tte., were the outcome of similar unions.

It was not without kecedent then that when the same thing occurred in Warrington a cause was formed that-, like the others mentioned, continues till the present. The "Secession Presbyterian Magazine," with which the Church was later connected, tells the story, the gist of-which we give In a suburb of Warrington stands St. James' Church (Episcopal), of which the Rev. J. Glazebrook, who had been a student of the Countess's College at Trevecca, had been many years minister, and had collected a large con- gregation. On his translation he took care that his successor was a faithful (evangelical) minis- ter. On the death of the latter, 1803, and being unable to secure a man of the same views and spirit, many of the more serious left, attending the Independent Chapel (Stepney), but not join- ing the fellowship. This went on till 1806, when a move was made for a new foundation. A public room was hired, and there a body, who desired an Evan- gelical ministry other than Independent, met. They comprised Episcopalians from St. James', Presbyterians from Cairo-street, and a few from Stepney.

One has seen it stated somewhere that the secession from the latter known as "Salem," under James llansley, united with the new congregation in 1811, but this must be an error. What they did was to unite with them for wor- ship till their own church was built.

-- - Naturally the majority looked to the Lady Huntingdon connection for supply, and a student of that Church was ordained over them the same year—December, 1806. In the follow- ing year they erected St. John's Chapel, opened personal qnalities, was gathered by exemplary for table congregation, who died' in 1827." Then the tJs"iortiacen, aifiirlareiss•ayti-n,,emdc minister, ter, Presbyterian element in St, John's was either still strong enough, or " by accession" had been strengthened, so that for a successor they made application to the body which was later the Home Mission Comrnittee of the United Presby- terian Presbytery of Lancashire for "supply," which they received of such acceptable kind that he received a call to become their second minister after labouring, among them for about two years. Ordained 19th August 1830, the Presby- tery of Lancashire having previously received the formal accession of the congregation to their ranks as a Presbyterian Church, the ministry was abundantly successful, and an asset to the forces for good in the town. But an anomaly existed to which be was never pulpit, the liturgy of the Church of reconciled. Twice every Sunday, before he read by a reader and clerk appointed to the post according to the Trust Deed. A majority were anxious to set aside 'this arrangement and have a new deed ratified, but met with unexpected opposition from some Eenntgelraenddtl was ,as duly of the trustees, and, rather than make trouble, 'after three years of ministry, in addition to the same time as " suPPIY," he resigned, and the congregation reverted to their previous connec- tion, which continued till the resignation of the last Lady Huntingdon minister in 1852. After two years of "supply" the cause had dwindled so much that St. John's was offered for sale, with the sequel that it once more became a Presbyterian cause.

• HMV this came about is told, as was the for- mer instance, in the pages of the official publi- cation of the Church, in the second instance the "English Presbyterian Messenger" for the current year :

"The opening of a preaching station was sanctioned for one year at Warrington, Robert aru 3" undertaking to chasepur- which was offered. for l e , p eglentehreoruesl sale (by the mortgagees), and with the aid of the existing congregation to maintain ordin- ances efficiently for one year, thus freeing the Presbytery and Home Mission from all pecun- iary liabilities for that period." And later, in a report of a Synod's discussion of the organ question; "Mr. Cromar was the first minister of the church who thought of introducing an organ. One indeed stood in the church at War- rington, which Robert Barbour purchased, and which belonged to the Lady Huntingdon Con- nexion. When the Church thus became Pres- byterian, the remains of the congregation sat on in the place and the organ was left undis- turbed, remaining in, use." A year later an English Presbyterian minister was ordained in 1854. After being in charge for some time, and since then he has had five successors at St. John's. During the ministry of James Warnock, one of them, new vestries were added to the church, and among benefac- tions from members was one from Dr. Mackie, an Elder, of .t. 150 worth of books, to the minis- ters of the town, the volumes to be selected by the recipients. The Registers of Baptisms and Burials from 1806 to 1837 were sent up to Somer- set House in the latter year, in response to a • Government request. Making a generous allowance for adherents, the membership of the Presbyterian cause in Warrington, in Cairo-street, would be well over 500 in its best day before secessions. In St. John's during the united Presbyterian and Lady Huntingdom regime, probably about 200; under the present Synod probably a little over 100 would be the maximum; before the present ministry it had dropped to 50.

New Books.

EUGENICS AND POLITICS. The Hibbert Journal. january, 1914. London : Williams and Norgato. 2s. 6d. net.

The January number of " Tho Illibbert Jour- nal " contains some extremely interesting arti- cles, e 4., " Self-sacrifice," by Prof. J. B. 'lane; " The Universities and the Training of the Social Worker," by Miss E. Macadam, of Liver- pool; " The Failure of the Church of England," by the Rev. A. W. P. Blunt. The article with which we wish to deal, however, is that contri- buted by Dr. F. C. S. Schiller, of Oxford, on• "Eugenics and Politics." It is confidently assumed by many biologists, and still more confidently by their political fol- lowers, that biological theory has nowooreached a stage when it may be fruitfully applied to the solution of social and political questions. We know, they say, the course of biological develop- ment. We know the conditions under which that development may be maintained, and to ensure the future progress and even the sur- vival of mankind, it is essential that our biological knowledge should supply the princi- ples on which must be based all subsequent leg- islation. And these writers do not hesitate to tell us that if we neglect their warnings the penalty will be the progressive degeneration of the physique of the nation, and possibly the ex- Unction of the human race. The worst of it is that biological extremists; obsessed by their own pet theories, pay, little or no heed to facts which lie Outside their own field of study, hut, on the contrary, appear to think that considerations other than biological do not count when they apply their theories to social and political pro- blems. Let it not be supposed that we are inclined to dispute the truth of much that Dr. Schiller, for instance, urges, or to deny its relevancy to political discussion. Not, at all. We believe that eugenics certainly has a message of im- portance for mankind, but we doubt if it will receive the attention it deserves, while it is couched in such terms as are generally employed by its more enthusiastic advocates.

===== 0002601_19140221_art0182.txt | included=true | included =====
ARE WE BUtLDING.UP AT THE WRONG

END?

Dr. Schiller does not, in our opinion, contri- bute anything that is very new to this discus- sion. He 'tells the old story of the decline of followingthe ldrt h ;Ist birth-rate, gt eh ewtillela rgtilvie f air R e ability. &c. The what Dr. Schiller thinks is wrong with the country. " The evidence goes to show that throughout the most valuable part of the nation, not only in the upper classes, but also in the middle classes and in the best parts of the working classes, the birth-rate per marriage has in a generation sunk from four and a half to two, and is now only half the size required to keep up the num- bers in those classes. In other words, society IS now soillered that in every generation it sheds one-hl f of the classes it itself values most highly, and supplies their places with the off-spring of the feeble-minded and casual labourer classes, whose families still average more than seven. What seriously aggravates the evil is the whole trend of social legislation. Social reform costs money, and the money is raised by taxation, which bears very hardly on the middle classes, who cannot curtail luxuries like the rich, and will not lower their standard of comfort. They meet the extra expense, there- fore, by further postponing the age of marriage, and further reducing their output Of children. One of the chief effects, therefore. of our present methods of improving social conditions is to deteriorate the race. And this in a two-fold manner: they eliminate the middle class, and they promote the survival of the unfit and defective." '

Thong)) Ire may admit the fact of the decline in the birth-rate, must we' subscribe to the views put forwa ru by Dr. Schiller in explana- tion of that fact? Is there tiny evidence' to show that, as a result of the extra taxation, due to social reform, the middle class has deliber- ately reduced its hirth-rate, either by " further post polling the age of marriage," or by " further reducing its out nit of children " We hardly think so. The total provision for the poorer lasses in the matter of education, poor rate, old-age pensions, &c., is only a fraction, of the total national expenditure, and bears a quite insignificant relation to the actual income of the middle and upper classes.

RATIONAL AND NATURAL SELECTION. There is, we admit, an unmistakable ten- dency observable in the upper and middle classes both to postpone the marriage age and to limit the output of children, but we are not disposed to view it as altogether an unmitigated evil. It is due to various causes, of which the feminist movement is by no means the least important. Women are no longer content to regard marriage and motherhood as their only 'Send and aim," and, consequently, they seek careers which, while permitting their individual development, do not necessarily preclude the fulfilment of the parental function. The eugenist may think that it is not for the good of the race that women should be conscious of other aims than that of motherhood, in which ease we must beg to differ from him. And we do not think that because women claim the recognition of their rights as human beings, it necessarily follows that there will be such a diminution of the birth-rate as to imperil the future of the race. We have sufficient confi- denceto behme that when the humanity tendency in question has gone beyond what is safe, it will be adjusted. We see absolutely no reason whatever to conclude that because there is a diminishing birth-rate it is imperative we should throw overboard every scheme of social amelioration and let the struggle for existence have uninterrupted sway. That the eugenist has much to say which is of the utmost import- ance to mankind we admit. But we think his message is to the individual, rather than to the State, at any rate, in the present state of biological knowledge. "So far as the eugenic principle says Prof. L. T. Holdionse, in his ," "Social Evohlton and Political Theory," " ad- vocates the substitution of rational for natural I selection, it is, in the abstract, upon firm ground. Where it can be clearly established that a stock is tainted with it hereditary blem- ish so great as to outweigh its merits, it is de- sirable that that stock should not be perpetu- ated. That is already recognised ethically as a duty, and is acted on by many individuals, in cases where there is such a taint as that of insanity. There is every reason why our know- ledge on these matters should be carried further and systematised, and it is possible that in cer- tain cases it may be found desirable to crystal- lise ethical- sentiment in positive law: for ex- ample, in the ease of such a class as the feeble- minded, whose permanent care is desirable for the benefit of the individual, it may be right that, as a condition of such care, restriction from marriage should be insisted on by society in the future interest of the race. On the other hand, the use of eugenic arguments against legislation designed to replace the struggle for existence' by ordered social co-operation is at bottom a misapplication of the principle, Tt rests on the survival of the older Meng of natural selection tinder it new form, in new ter- minology. The method of social legislation should not be to accommodate institutions to the survival of the stronger;• it should be to

NEW THINGS IN THE LAST 50 YEARS.

Mr. Norbury Williams, writing to celebrate the jubilee of the " Manchester City News," has prepared a list of the new things that have come during the past 50 years.

- _ " here is a list (striking, and, I thfnk, upon the whole, -fairly accurate) of a few things we did not possess fifty or sixty years ago :—Steel pens, envelopes, notepaper, lawn tennis, motor cars, bicycles, ironclad ships, screw steamers, electric telegraph, sleeping and dining cars, elec- tric light, telephones, lifts, mammoth hotels, fountain pens, garden parties, afternoon tea, tramways (horse), ditto (electric), photographs, postcards, perambulators, spring mattresses, plate-glass, bitter beer, torpedoes, breech- loaders, revolvers, wooden tobacco pipes, compe- titive examinations and cramming, art colours, society papers, illustrated magazines, hypno- tism, Christian Science, millionaires, massage, volunteers, typhoid, diphtheria, airships, suffra- gettes, Salvation Army, tinned goods, fish knives, goloshes, waterproofs, gas beating and cooking, sewing machines, threepenny bits, !florins, venetian blinds, Spiritualism, weather forecasts, wall posters, moustaches, wood pave- ments, hospital nurses, lady helps, limited lia- bility, victories, C,ooks tourists, dyspepsia, par- cel post, appendicitis; hot-water bottles, bacilli, vote by ballot, halfpenny stamps, halfpenny circular cars„ ,!taxicabs„ daily illustrated papers, process blocks, three-colour printing, Rontgen rays, motor cycles, golf, gum, incandescent man- tles, radium, and antiseptic surgery. We had hardly any of these things in 1864; we have them all in 1914.

DISEASES NAMED AFTER THE SAINTS. About forty diseases have the names of saints attached to them; a most :varied group of holy men and women from Saint Agatha to Saint Zachary. Judging by this, one would think the medical profession to be a very religious lot. Whoever wished to immortalise the names of the saints by giving them a place in the medical dictionary certainly chose a disagreeable selec- tion of disease for the saints. There is Saint nacre's disease, or piles; Saint Avertin's disease, or epilepsy; Saint Gete's disease, or leprosy; Saint Dymphna's disease, or insanitk ; Saint Giles' disease, or cancer; Saint Job's disease, or syphilis; Saint Main's evil, or itch; Saint lloch's disease, or bubo; Saint Mar- tin's evil, or drunkenness; Saint Lazarus disease, or hog measles (also leprosy); Saint Sement's disease, or lues. It would have shown much better taste, to say nothing of respect; to use the names of the saints only in connection with diseases of polite society, such as gall- stones, neurasthenia, amenorrhea and corns. Chorea appears to be a favourite among the saints; not only is it referred to as Saint Vitus' dance, but also as Saint Guy's dance, Saint An- thony's dance, and Saint Alodestus' disease. Many of these dance diseases which spread in epidemic form over Europe at certain periods in its history, and which some thought were due to the bite of a tarantula, were merely mild forms of the Saint Ragtime tic, which now has England and America in its bear-like clutches. —" The Family Doctor."

ALBERT SQUARE SIXTY YEARS AGO.

As nearly all our readers are familiar with Alhert-square, Manchester, they will be inter- ested to read a description of it as it appeared in the youth of a gentleman still hying, and who writes to the "City News":—

I was born in Ancoats: but removed at a very early age to a house in Tasle-alley. In front of our house were open spaces, and 1 remember playing amongst the sand whilst John Dalton- street was being laid out. On one side of the now Albert-square were blocks of houses with cellar dwellings, and on part of the site now occupied by the Town Hall was the Town's Yard, in which were accommodated the scav- enging department, lighting department, and the fire brigade. The fire brigade consisted of five regular men, with auxiliaries composed of various tradesmen who were paid •sixpence an hour when on duty at a fire, with a small retaining fee, paid quarterly, oil condition that they at- tended fire drills. The five regular men were paid eighteen shillings per week, and sixpence an hour when on active duty. A small branch of the brigade was stationed at Pollard-street, Ancoats. • When a fire broke out a bell was tolled in the Town's Yard. Three youths were engaged to Call upon the auxiliaries if a fire broke out dur- ing the night. Each youth had his own round, which extended as far as Ancoats, and he was provided with a large rick, similar to those used by the policemen of that time. If the fire was of .large dimensions, any civilians on the spot were engaged to work the manual engine. They ' were supplied with a badge, worked in gangs, and received sixpence per hour. No high pres- sure was available, but the water from the mains ran into large canvas tubs, from wliTa it was taken by the hand pumps. I have in my possession a priotograph of an i engine called the Deluge, taken in the Town's Yard. I remember a large fire at Rylands' warehouse in High-street, when the whole block was destroyed, and firemen came from the slur- rounding districts attracted by the glare in the sky. The police force of that time being in- • sufficient, the horse soldiers from Ilulme bar- racks were called out to maintain order. An- other large fire broke out in a waste warehouse in Miller's-lane during severe wintry weather. I saw the firemen with icicles hanging from their helmets and clothes. •

THE FUNCTION OF EUGENICS. Eugenics has, then, a function to perform, but we protest strongly against the claim made apparently by some of its supporters that it is to supersede all other forms of political thought. Its true function, .we should say, is to apply biological theory to the problems of society, but it is not in itself a complete social philosophy. It needs to be incorporated in a comprehensive social philosophy, which takes not only the biological, but the sociological factors into account, and of which the ethical developtuent of mankind is the central feature. Dr. Schiller's article is interesting, but we do not believe that it will deter any social re- formers, whose zeal is .inspired by a pidi„ophy such as .we have unheated, from proseeutin.g their schemes,

THE WRONG DOCTOR.

An old man who looked like a foreigner was walking along a Loudon street playing a con- certina that had seen better days. Noticing a brass plate with the words "Doctor of Music" written under the name on it, he went up to the house and rang the bell. The door was answered by a servant, who asked his business. " Please," he said, "is the doctor o' music in?''

" Yes; what is it you want?" the servant asked.

" Why, just pop in an' ax him bow much he'll charge to mend my concertina!" was the reply.

IT DIDN'T MATTER. " What are you doing, dear?" asked the little 'girl's mother, as she paused to look at some very strange marks the child was making on a piece of paper.

'l'm writing a letter to Lillie Smith," was the answer.

"But, my dear," laughed the mother, "you don't know how to write."

" Oh, that doesn't matter, mother. Litho doesn't know how to read."

RAIN ON THE ROOF. [By MIRIAM CLARE I'OTTRR.I lain upon the roof in the garret; little fingers knocking on the pane; A fairy voice is calling in the splashing and the falling, . " I am the rain—the rain!" Shadows, shadows, shadows, in the corners by the eaves; Wet against the windows, lie the little faded leaves.

Pain upon the roof in the garret; play we are a pirate crew at sea; Play the old oak chest, in the vale of cobwebs dressed, Is a leaking, creaking ship, the Stinging Bee; play the broken cradle, where our pile of play- things lie, Is an island full of treasure, where we'll anchor by and by.

Rain upon the roof in the garret; shadows, dust, and cob Webs all around; We know the game to play on a dark and blowy day,

And we launch the Stinging Bee without a sound; With a pilot at the spinning-wheel, we'll land at break of day On lonely Cradle Island, and steal all the things away.

THE PIN AND THE NEEDLE. A pin and a needle, being idle, began to quar- rel, as idle folks are apt to do. "I should like to know," said the pin to the needle, "what you are good for, and how you can expect to get through the world without a head?" What is the use of your bead," said the needle, rather sharplY, "if you have no eye?" What is the use of an eye," said the pin, if there is always something in it?" "I can go through more work than you can," said the needle.

" Yes, but you will not live long." "Why note" said the needle. "Because you always have a stitch in your side," said the pin. "You're a crooked creature,' said the needle. "And you're so proud that you cannot bend Without breaking your back." "I will pull your head off if you insult zno again," said the needle, "I will pull your ej[e out if you touch ray. head," said the pin. While they were thus quarrelling a little girl came in and began to sew with the needle. In a short time she broke it at the eye. Then she tied the thread around the neck of the pin, and in trying to pull it through the cloth she soon pulled off the head. Then she threw it into the dirt by the side of the needle. Well, here we are," said the needle. "We have nothing to fight about now," said the pin.

. -- " Bad luck seems to have brought us to our senses," said the needle. "We are very much like men. TheY quarrel about the good things they, have till they lose them, and find out that they are brothers only when they are in the dust together."

A Disclosure in a Warrington

Divorce Case.

Mk. Thomas Taylor petitioned in the Divorce court on Tuesday, for divorce from his wife, Mary Taylor, on the ground of misconduct. Mr. Taylor, 12, Arthur-street, Warrington, stated that lie was married at the Parish Church, Warrington, on February 21st, 1909. He and his wife lived in Warrington and at Eastwood-road, Walton Inferior. There were no children. Before the marriage his wife was a barmaid. In January, 1910, she left him, and previous to this she had been consistently brutal and cruel to him. In February. 1910, he decided to go abroad, and went to America, whence he returned on October 14th last year. Upon returning he found a summons issued for his arrest. for arrears under a maintenance order, which his wife obtained. He surrendered to the police, and the case was heard before the Stockton Heath magistrates. His wife admitted in cross-examination that she had committed misconduct, and the summons was dismissed. Thomas O'Toole, of the Lancashire Constabu- lary, stated that respondent and a man named Kershaw had been living together at Elverstone- street, Leigh. for two years. She was acting bar- maid at the Lilford Hotel, Leigh. They were known in the neighbourhood as Mr.. and Mrs. Kershaw.

His Lordship granted _a decree nisi with costs.

Mr. Thotnat; ilucknill, instructed by Mr. S Steel, Warrington, appeared for the peti- tioner.

WOMEN'S QUESTIONS.

Why the Shoemakers' Bills are Heavier than Formerly.

LIGHTLY CLAD.

Durham University opened its doors to women some timo ago. --Now it receives them on the same terms as men, thus setting Oxford and Cambridge an excellent example. Women with English diplomas are allowed in Italy to qualify as engineers. The Norwegian Government has appointed Miss Ella Gleditsch to a fellowship at one of the Universities in that country. • This entitles the holder ,to work in an American College, and Miss Gleditsch, who has been studying under Madame Cuiie, has taken up research work in connection with radium at Yale.

The Doyenne of Australasian Women Writers.

A very remarkable woman is Miss Catherine Helen Spence, who is deservedly called "the doyenne of Australasian women writers." In 1839, when fourteen years a age, her father emigrated to South Australia and became the first Town Clerk of Adelaide. One of her brothers held office as a Minister of the Crown. Miss Spence, when 83, published a •book, and occupied the pulpit of the Unitarian Church to which she belonged. She has written all her life, is the author of five novels, and in- numerable political pamphlets and works on social and political questions of the day. She is an enthusiastic reformer .and has used her talents chiefly on behalf of 'ciiildren On her 80th birthday a public reception was given in her honour, which was attended 'by the fore- most men and women of theT'States that com- bine the Commonwealth of Australia.

Tight Skirts and Shoemakers' Bills. Since the advent of the short tight skirt, footwear has certainly cost more. It has given a greater importance to the appearance of one's boots or shoes, which must be smart and neat, and one of the textile trade journals has dis- covered that this is not the only reason why it has been necessary to pay more frequent visits to the bootmaker's. It is a grievance with the woollen manufacturers that the tight skirt makes for economy in the use of cloth, but, says this journal, what you save in this way is more than counterbalanced by extra shoe-leather bills. Wearing a very tight skirt you have to take twice as many steps when walking out of doors, and therefore wear out your footgear so-much more quickly. This, it is suggested, is one more reason why women would he well advised to give up the inconvenient tight skirts. The Vogue for Light Clothing, _

_ The dresses•and other garments of to-day are much lighter, consequently more hygienic and comfortable than those of the Victorian era. But although diaphanous materials are now worn almost as much in winter as in summer, and quite as much for evening and theatre wear, the vogue for them has not yet been car- ried As far as in Prance during the eighteenth century. Then, a great lady's dress was always tranAparent. A Frenchwoman who lived some ' little time after the Revolution made a bet that all the clothes she had on, jewellery included, would not weigh as much as two silver coins. -She won the bet.

===== 0002601_19140221_art0194.txt | included=true | included =====
Chinese Influence.

Last year the Russian and Bulgarian influ- ence predominated in the world of fashion; this year we have gone further East, and China has supplied us with the loose sacque, the pagoder basque, and more especially the colours of the spring have been suggested by that wonderful country. Chinese embroidery in red, and faded yellow, on a black ground, is used on many of the newest coats, that in shape closely resemble that of a Manchu dignitary, being cut all in one, except that the bascule is joined on at the hips, where the *seam is hidden •by a cord. One thing we have learned from the Chinese, and that is the suitability of black as a background. The result is particularly good in a number of effective combinations, such, for instance, as canary, plum colour,. and black; old Nankin blue, black, white, and jade green; and dull pinks, and canary, on navy blue;, all of which are seen on the newest spring gowns. On afternoon and evening dresses a bit of

===== 0002601_19140221_art0195.txt | included=true | included =====
THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, FEBRUARY 21, 1914.

Chinese embroidery gives a touch of distinction and colour, where sombre tints are chosen.

Inspirations from Mexico. -War, or political unrest in a country, how- ever far distant, always influences fashions directly or indirectly. Thus the trouble in Mexico having brought that unhappy republic into prominence, Parisian designers have sought inspiration from the native dress and colours. The latter are very bright, and will be seen side by side with the soft, faded shades that will be much worn this spring and during the summer. The embroideries in beads of the brightest tints, that decorate the national Mexican costume, now embellish Parisian dresses, coats and other garments, and bid fair to rival the Oriental work which has been, and still is, so lavishly used. The Mexican in- fluence is also seen in some of the new coatees, prepared for spring wear. Mourning Wear To-day.

As a material for the fashioning of mourning garments, .taffetas is at tho moment very much in evidence, and, trimmed with crepe, it is at its best. Perhaps no fabric has of late years received more attention from manufacturers than the 'utter, with the result that it is now a beautiful material, soft and pliable, and as different as it is possible for anything to be from that worn in the sixties. Then dresses were laden with crepe that cockled, showed every drop of rain, and turned a rusty black in a month or two. The crepe of to-day is as light as Chiffon, has a beautiful finish, is waterproof, and gives an air of quite the reverse of heavi- ness and oppressiveness to mourning garb. No wonder, then, that it has returned to favour, with milliners as well as with dressmakers. The former combine it with rich, dull Ottoman silk for smart mourning headgear. Caracul is a favourite black fur for wearing with mourning attire.

New Millinery. Crocodile skins have, for a number of years, been in use for footwear, bags, and other articles, but until this spring milliners have made little or no use of it. Now, however, hats of a sporting type are built almost entirely of this skin. They have shaped crowns, and rolled brims, and are said to wear well, and to resist the ravages of had weather. Suede is also used in many shapes, and with various trimmings. Panama straws are back again. They have wide brims and dome-shaped crowns, which are encircled with a hand of black corduroy silk ribbon. Sailor hats have rolled brims, and are trimmed with striped ribbon bands or bows. Three-cornered shapes, in fine and other straws, are also shown. Some of the new straws are made to imitate ribbons, and are used for crowns. Tulle hats for after- noon wear are charming and fairy-like in their lightness. They stand high above the face, and in some instances are tied with ribbons under the chin; this gives them the semblance of the bonnets of years ago.

SUTTON GUARDIAN'S DEATH.

Mr. Charles Walsh, a draper, of Sutton, for- merly a Liberal member of the St. Helens Town Council, who had been a member of the l'rescot Board of Guardians for a long period, died on Friday at the age of TO. His favourite pastime was the study of Shakespeare and other literary matters, and in his younger days he was an ardent politician.
