This was the beginning of a very unhappy 4aonth for Priscilla. She did not know what to do with the last straw, as she and her husband invariably termed their visitor when they we.re alone. He proved a most embarrassing guest, paying no attention household rules. He despised the best be- room, preferring to sleep out of doors, or per- haps in one of the farm buildings. Ile tzamped all over the place, and would some- times disappear for days together witnout giving any notice. He pretended to be anx- ious about correspondence from Canada, and when day after day they told him no letter had arrived:" he did not conceal a suspicion that they might have intercepted it. Pris- cilla's dislike of him grew as time went on, but this dislike her husband did not share. The man interested him, and he related to his wife a conversation they had had, each reproaching the other for his methcd of life. "Why," the tramp. had said, "you're the most inane. helpless Individual I ever saw. Pain, Wind, Headache, Faintnel

WARRINGTON "A "

The Warrington "A"; men brought home a nice little victory from Swinton, where they won a good game by eight points to three, and this win ought to assist them to once more again climb towards the top of the Combina- tion ladder. In the first half the Warrington chances were none too bright, although they held their op. ponents well in check. Swinton were not long before becoming aggressive, and for a time play hovered on the "25" line at the Warrington end. Lever nearly got across, when he was promptly tackled by Shaw. It was a fine stop that Shaw made. A mark was made on behalf of Swinton, but the kick at goal failed. Then Baker got the ball, and, running finely, got over the line. The, try was disallow i ed, as t was stated he acci- dentally put his foot into the touchline prioi to scoring. The game was of a lively character, and had Warrington not been particularly keen on tack- ling their lino would have been ,crossed. Just on half-time moss got possession and ran over a try for Swinton. ili7orsley failed at the goal- kick, and the teams turned round with War- rington three points to the bad,

WARRINGTON WAKE UP. Warrington in the second half got going in their proper stride, and they made matters un- comfortable for the home team. The half had bot gone long ere Harmer got away and ended a fine sport with a well-deserved try. Critchle4 was entrusted with the kick and secured the additional points. This put fresh life into the Warrington "A" men, and for a time they pl,ayed at the top of form. The Swinton men, however, were found to be skilled tacklers, and but for their ability in this work the Warrington score would hays been considerably augmented. Later Baker got possession, and it was not long before he had, made another try assured. This was, without doubt, the best try of the match. Critchley was called upon to take the goal-kick from a difficult angle, and failed to get the goal. The game resulted: Warrington, one goal two tries (eight points); Swinton, one try (three points).

THE VISIT OF ST. HELENS. A Good Game at Wilder:pool. There should be an interesting match at Wil- derspool this afternoon between Warrington "A" and St. Helens "A." The sides are well ,balanced and they should.give a good display. The Warrington Committee are turning out their strongest available side, which will be: Bramhall; Baker, Steele, harrett, Shake- shaft ; Brookes, John Brown; Harmer, Naylor, Critchley, Green, Bevan, Pouncey. Cup tie, and the Highfield in winning by 11 points to 8 have now to meet St. Helens in the first round of the Cup Competi- tion proper. They are not likely to go further than that stage. Eylands', although beaten, were not disgraced, for they put up a genuine fight, and had not quite the luck which came the way of their opponents. The Highfield, through the effective work of their forwards, became dangerous, but the de- fence of Iforrocks overcame them. Later the homeSters took up a strong attitude, and a fine bout of passing ended in Laws scoring a try. Warren failed at goal. . .

The visitors retaliated, and the equalising try was secured.

The home team experienced hard luck, and a magnificent bout of passing saw Whittaker Pulled up op the line. HOCKEY.

LATCHFORD'S BAD PATCH. Another Defeat Sustained by the "Lads." ' There is no doubt that Latchford first eleven have struck a bad patch at present. They are encountering one of those periods which all teams occasionally come across, when every- thing seems to be against them. They certainly did not deserve to lose by 4-1 at Knutsford to Mid-Cheshire. All the luck that was about seemed to go to the homesters, who, of course, accepted every chance. Latchford played Furness at inside right. Newbold right half, and Dale inside left. The opening exchanges were all in favour of Latch- ford, who attacked vigorously, but without ulti- mate success. Time after time the visitors' half-backs broke up the attacks of the Mid- Cheshire forwards, and gave the ball to their own forwards, who were very unfortunate with well directed efforts, Knight, Wilkinson -and Newbold are to be congratulated on their good work in this portion of the game. After about 15 minutes Mid-Cheshire scored from a scramble in goal. Soon after Hunt, the home centre- forward, ran the length of the field, and through a mix-up between full-backs and goalkeeper, scored a rather lucky goal. Still he deserved it for his pertinacity. Bruhl had a hot time after this, but came through his ordeal well, saving what seemed certain goals. Latchford again attacked, and Pearson scored from a right wing pass. After half-time the home team's goalkeeper had a lot of work, and was rather fortunate in getting his legs in the way of shots from Pearson and Knight, while a shot from Dale hit the ' goalpost and rebounded into play. From two breakaways, Mid-Cheshire scored. Bruhl filled his position better than any man on the field, his skill in the first half in stopping shots being great. Billington also gave a good display at left back. 1 Second Side Make a Draw.

The second eleven met Brooklands at Brook- lands and the game resulted in a draw with two goals each. For the first ten minutes Latch- ford were continuously attacking, and only sound defending play, coupled with a good know- ledge of the three-back game, enabled Brook- lands to keep out the Latchford forwards. As Brooklands got going they forced the pace, and made several attacks on the Latchford goal. The defence, however, was equal to the occasion, and after conceding three corners relieved the pressure for a time. From a breakaway, Roe scored for Brooklands, and this lead they re- tained until after half-time. In the second half Latchford opened out the game, and this policy was justified by results, Starkey, jun., twice picking up long passes and scoring. Roe equalised for Brooklands. The game ended with a score of 2-2, and this was a Artie reflex of the play. Both sides were fast and made many Sudden excursions into each other's circle, and altogether the game was a thoroughly enjoyable one, which was contri- buted to by the first-class condition of the ground. Brooklands right-half and right-back were the pick of a good team, the half-back especially breaking up many attacking move- ments. For Latchford, Minards at centre-for- ward, and Albinson behind him were in good form.

WARRINGTON LEAGUE NOTES.

As I said last week, there -seemed to be an epidemic at the present time amongst the War- rington League clubs of breaking their engage- ments. It appears to have developed more last week-end, as Crosfield's Reserve, Weaver Refin- ing Co., and Daresbury cancelled their fixtures with Stockton Heath, Great Sankey and Adult School. The League ought to find some method of dealing with these defaulters. In the Second Division the Orford St. John's cancelled the fixture on their own ground with the Emmanuel Old Boys, which caused much disappointment to the Old Boys, as they have the intention of repeating the verdict of their first meeting, when they defeated the John's in Victoria Park.

The meeting of St. Barnabas' and Bank Quay Recs. on the Lovely-lane glound proved a very interesting encounter, when the latter gained the verdict by the odd goal in three, and as these teams have to meet again, a very inter- esting game should be witnessed.

===== 0002601_19140221_art0217.txt | included=true | included =====
NORTH CHESHIRE LEAGUE. Latchford it: Bowdon Vale,

•At Thelwall-lane. After the preliminary ex- changes the .home team asserted their superior- ity and had the best of the game. The visitors' goal was continually under bombardment. The Vale could not make much impression on the home defenders', and they were easily overcome. FRIENDLY GAMES.

Emmanuel Old Boys' Reserve v. Elmwood Congregational. . .

At Victoria Park.- The Cons. were the first to become aggressive, but lost chances by hesitat- ing in front of goal. They were driven back to their own lines and the Boys' forward line were easily held in check and soon had to retire. .T. Plicenix, with a good drive, scored for the visit- ors. The Reserve then woke up and made a raid on the Cons.' goal. X mistake by one of the Cons.' backs nearly proved fatal, and directly after the homesters were penalised for off-side, which brought relief to the visitors' defence. Following some end-to-end play, Rigby scored for the Old Boys, and at half-time the scores were one goal each.

On restarting the game was for a while in midfield. From a long pass out to the wing the Boys began to press, and Green gained the lead. The, Cons. now had the best of the game, and were sent back by off-side. However, from a centre by J. Phenix, Hughes equalised. The game resulted in a draw of two goals each.

Gossages' Recs. v. Great Sankey. Owing to the Great Sankey Club having their League fixture cancelled, they arranged to play the former club at Widnes. The visitors attacked, Gardiner sending in a fine shot, which Farmer failed to hold, and Great Sankey were the first to score. Encouraged by this success, the visitors were now all over the home team, and Illidge added a second point, and Great Sankey were two up at half-time.,

On resuming, the game was very fast and keenly contested, and by superior combination the visitors took the ball towards the home goal, when Darlington increased their lead with a hard drive at close range. Payne scored the visitors' fourth goal. Result: Great Sankey, four goals; Gossages', nil.

LATCHFORD WORKSHOP MEDAL COMPETITION.

_ The committee of the above club decided to continue their Workshop Competition during the last two months of the present season. The competition will be played under the same con- ditions as before, with the exception that only five Cheshire League club players shall be allowed to take part 111 ally one team in the competition. All applications should be made to the Secretary, Mr. T. Powell, Latchford- street, Thelwall-lane, before the 2nd of March, and the competition will be started on or about •the 9th of March. Already there have been several inquiries from workshop teams anxious for the commencement of the competition, and the committee are expecting a record entry.

CROSS &MN RY NORTHERN CHAMPIONSHIPS.

WILL WARRINGTON WIN TO-DAY? ' On Saturday last the North-Eastern Asso- ciation were holding their qualifying races, which resulted in Heaton Harriers placing the first two individuals and winning team, in the Juniors, and Blaydon Harriers, by placing their six counters in the first nine, with J. McCluskey as the individual winner, won a double first in the Senior race,-but J. W. Breed, Durham City, the only danger, was badly supported by his clubmates, the holders of the Senior champion- ship.

Warrington Athletic Club had a training spin over about 81 miles of ;nixed country. Pose, picking up splendidly, came home 300 yards ahead of Bolton and Holbrook, both of whom moved in improving style; 40 yards away Bur- gess was producing his best performance, and had a similar advantage over Gregson, who is coming along rapidly; 80 yards away Antrobus was bringing Scott, Turton and Jepson along in good order, with the captain, J. Parsonage, a close whipper-in. Hampson came home first, but having stopped to remove a thorn, did not cover the full course. Well! if there is a better team than Warrington on this showing, then to-day's Senior race will be made memorable as the hottest held over Haydock Park Race- course.

===== 0002601_19140221_art0223.txt | included=true | included =====
INDIVIDUAL RUNNERS.

The holder and National champion, E. Glover, ,will establish a record if he wins ther-7enior race. C. Vose,•his runner-up for both honours, has yet to be beaten by Glover over this course. G. Wallach followed the other two home last season, and will be doing his best perform- ance in his 36th year if he realises the expecta- tions of his friends. S. Francom is a similar subject to Wallach, probably less erratic, but lacking in 'speed. L. Proctor may again beat his clubmate, W. Scott, whose performance should be closely noted, and T. Fennah, who had a slight advantage over H. Holbrook, when the consistent Warringtonian achieved his objec- tive as a team unit, will experience difficulty in repeating the performance. Bolton should turn the tables on Francom. If McCluskey's time and stated distance are correct, he will have a tremendous following ,from the North-Eastern counties. The Junior race will provide great sport, and of the many fancied, F. Bakewell and E. Pye have the best credentials. Albert, Farrel, Runcorn, is just ripe for his best per- formance, and his prospects are equal to the best.

Special excursion train leaves Central Station at 1.43, and a good outing, with the best of spirt, is anticipated by many, who intend to cheer the locals to victory. .

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, FEBRUARY 21, 1914.

Miscellaneous.

===== 0002601_19140221_art0242.txt | included=true | included =====
PARLIAMENTARY POINTS.

Lord Murray Faces the House of

Lords Over the Marconis.

A LAND AMENDMENT DEFEATED.

In the House of Commons on Tuesday Mr. Lloyd George informed Mr. Cassel that it was the intention of the Government to reintroduce the Revenue Bill, which was withdrawn last session.

~------ The debate on the motion for an Address in reply to the King's Speech was continued. Mr. E. Royds moved an amendment which expressed regret that no legislation had been foreshadowed to restore the credit and security of land and house property, which had been undermined by the Finance (1909-10) Act, and by recent public utterances of Ministers suggesting fur- ther land legislation. Mr. Lloyd George re- plied, and after a somewhat disorderly scene the amendment was defeated by 301 votes to 213. LORD MURRAY'S EXPLANATION. _ - _

In the House of Lords Lord Murray made a personal statement regarding his purchase of shares in the American Marconi Company. With respect both to his private purchase and his investment of Liberal party funds he ad- mitted an error of judgment and expressed regret, but he said that there was nothing which a fair judgment would hold to reflect upon the honour and integrity of our public life. The Marquis of Lansdowne advised the House not to declare any conclusions that night, and asked Lord Ampthill to defer the motion had on the paper for a Select Committee to inquire into certain allegations in the public Press against Lord Murray. The Marquis of Crewe concurred in this advice.

LABOUR PARTY ANGRY. In the House of Commons on Wednesday Col. Seely (Secretary for War), answering Sir J. Jardine, said no applications had been received from officers of the King's military forces for permission to help in drilling or in organising civilians in Ulster. Major M. Archer-Shee asked whether Mr. PolltBMa, who has been deported from Smith Africa, was a British subject, and whether, if he was not, he would be refused admission to this country as an undesirable alien. Mr. McKenna replied that Mr. Poutsma claimed to be a naturalised British subject, and therefore did not come within the scope of the Aliens Act. Major Archer-Shee asked if legislation woulii be introduced to amend the Aliens Act, "so that desperadoes of this character—"

The end of the sentence was drowned by the protests of the Labour members. The debate on the motion for an Address in reply to the King's Speech was resumed by Mr. G. N. Barnes, who moved an amendment ex- pressing regret that no mention was made of the recent deplorable events in Dublin, and no promise made of an impartial and representa- tive Commission of Inquiry into the conduct of the police. Mr. Birrell replied, and after some debate the amendment was rejected by 233 votes to 45. An amendment moved by Sir J. Bethell, regretting that no reference was made in the Ziieech to the unfair administration by the Road Board of the funds under its control, was defeated by 268 votes to 55.

===== 0002601_19140221_art0243.txt | included=true | included =====
A GREAT FRENCH RAILWAY

and the ROUTE TO THE WINTER SUN.

The P.L.M. holds a world-wide reputation for the speed of its trains. The Cate d'Azur Rapide covers the distance between Paris and Mar- seilles in 101 hours, and reaches Nice 31 hours after. It is the fastest long distance train in Europe.

The permanent way is considered one of the best in the world, and the construction of the engines is such as to give them the maximum of reliability. Signals are adequate to the im- portant requirements of the traffic. With re- gard to all conditions, if the P.L.M. is some- times equalled, it endeavours never to be sur- passed. Words cannot convey the deep charm of this country; it must be seen with the eye. Once visited, it is always re-visited.

Pleasant and rapid, the P.L.M. is the natural highway to the South. By the Mont-Cenis, Ventimiglia, and the Simplon, it commands the whole of Italy, from Rome to Naples and from Venice to Florence. By the Marseilles it gives access to the East; to Greece, Egypt, Tunis, Algeria, Corsica, and all the delightful islands of the Mediterranean.

Switzerland, too, is most easily and delight- fully reached by the P.L.M., travelling via Pontarlier, Geneva, Evian-Saint-Gingolph, or Chamonix-Martigny, the passenger can get to any part; to Neuchatel, Berne, Interlaken, the foot of the jungfrau, Lausanne, all the famous places around Lake Leman (Vevey, Clarens, Montreux, &T.), Martigny (Grand Saint-Ber- nard), Zermatt, at the foot of the Matterhorn, and the great glaciers of Monte Rosa, etc. PROVENCE—THE RIVIERA.

On the shore of a sea so blue that even the deep-blue sky looks pale, and snugly shel- tered under the mazy bush of the Maures the red porphyry rocks of the Esterel, and the great snowy Alps, the warm land of Provence lies, balmy and luminous, like an earthly paradise. It is, as it has been new-named, the Azure Coast.

Whoever would shun the gloom of winter will find on the happy shore unfailing sunshine and eternal spring. Here, in the joy of life and the sweetness of repose, he will renew his strength amidst scenery that mountain and sea combine to beautify beyond compare, where every hill- crest reached and every headland turned create a new surprise. Among the districts mentioned one surpasses all others in winter; the sunny, warm and glorious Riviera, where golden fruits and rare flowers grow, which has the enchantment of carnival fetes, battles of flowers, regattas, theat- rical evenings, races and all kinds of sports. Close to the most lively places are found the most delightful corners for rest. The season starts in October, and until May the coast between Hyeres and Mentone is the social centre of the world. The P.L.M. is also the direct means of com- munioation between the North of Europe and the Lyons International and Urban Exhibition, 1914, that will be held from May Ist to Novem- ber Ist, in the town of Lyons itself, one of the most important and richest industrial centres in France. This exhibition promises to be one of the most interesting and instructive ever held, embracing as it does all branches of iin- dustry; the chief merchant nations of the world taking part in it. Exhibitors will have the unusual concession of their goods being sent ' back to the country of origin free of transport, an advantage that will be appreciated Special through bookings will be available by the P.L.M.

CHAS. KNOTT.

COMPANY-FORMING RECORD. A new record was established by the number of new companies registered at Somerset House last year, the "Investors' Guardian" reporting the number to be 6,871, and the registered capi- tal J 2146,766,000, this amount being £18,000,000 below that of 1912. Money market conditions were not conducive to the active formation of new companies, particularly those of a more speculative character. The capital of mining and oil companies was much less than that registered in 1912, but there was an increase in nearly all the manufacturing and trading groups—engineering, coal, motor, carriage, tex- tiles, drapery, eze.

WYCLIFFE P.S.A.

The speaker at the Wycliffe P.S.A. on Sunday was Mr. Sidney Rowland, and his theme was based upon "Render to Cmsar the things that are Cmsar's, and unto God the things that are God's."

In that sentence he said they found' a lesson showing how to do their duties as citizens, and to the powers that be, whilst they were also told to render the higher duties of life to God for God had the right to expect the best service from man.

Mr. David Owen, who presided, read the lessons. Mr. Payton rendered an organ solo in his customary masterly style. Two new members received a hearty welcome.

BRICK-STREET MISSION.

Presentation to Choirmaster.

The members of the above Mission and Choir held a social evening on Tuesday, to consider the best ways and means to raise funds to reduce the debt with which the church is burdened. The chair was occupied by the President of the Church, Mr. William Barton, sen. He said the meeting would be thrown open for any suggestions that might bring about a successful issue.

It was decided that a Redemption Fund be formed, and this idea was greatly appreciated. It was also decided to hold sectional efforts, and several other special means were adopted. A concert was held and the following pro- gramme was presented: Special anthem by the Church Choir; song by Miss H. Howell; violin solo, Mr. R. Syers; and bass solo, Mr. James Tomkinson. The accompanist was Miss Eva Shaw.

Councillor James Shaw made a presentation on behalf of the members of the congregation and choir, to Mr. W. Barton, sen., for the past services he had rendered as choirmaster and organist. Tltis, post he had filled in a remark- able and mOst pleasing manner for the last 24 years. Mr. Shaw pointed out a few of the splendid and most important advantages the church and school had received by the sacri- fices in which Mr. Barton had made on their behalf. He handed to Mr. Barton a handsome baro- meter, which bore the following inscription "Presented to Mr. William Barton, son., for services rendered as choirmaster and organist for Brick-street Mission."

Mr. Barton responded with a few pleasing re- marks.

Light refreshments were served, and later the choir rendered "The radiant morn" in a very pleasing manner.

"CONFLICTING WARRING SECTS.''

Remarkable Discourse by Catholic Priest.

"This country rejoices in about 268 conflict- ing, warring sects, all of which shelter under the name of Protestant."

So said the Rev. Dr. Downey, who, along with the Rev. Hicks Gower, of the Catholic Mission- ary Society, has been holding a week's mission at St. Mary's Catholic Church, Buttermarket- street, during the past week. The comment was made at the opening service on Sunday evening during a Catholic evidence sermon._

These, sects, he said, are all flatly contradict- ing each other on many points, and therefore can their dogmas be all true? I do not think they can, and that brings one to the question— How many different forms of Christianity has Christ established? We know that He established one. Did He not pray that all His followers might he one, as Christ and the Father were one? In these sects, conflicting continually, there is not much resemblance to a one-ness.

Christ as a Teacher. Those outside the Catholic Church seem to have forgotten that Christ came to us moie as teacher. These people .think of Him only as a saviour, and sing' hymns about His shedded blood, but the idea that He was a teacher with a definite slibject to teach, which all the fol- lowers of Christ are bound to accept, has never entered into their heads. The dominant note of authority, which brooks no opposition, rang through every word spoken by Christ. To-day can we find the voice of authority in Protest- antism?

If we take the Anglican Church and seek to find out what they teach upon the vital point of whether or not Christ was present in the Sacrament, what happens? How are we to find out their views? Some say, "Go and ask the Anglican Church ministers." Very good, and if I ask a High Church minister he says, "Yes, certainly He was present." I ask a Broad Churchman and he tells me not to worry, as it , does not matter, and a Low Churchman says, "No, certainly not." Nov, which are we to , accept? The three ministers of the same reli- gious body disagreed. Where was the voice of authority in that? No Church in the world dare stand up and settle any question of importance with the voice of authority except the Catholic Church There are 298,000,000 Catholics in the world to- day, but, large as is the Catholic Church, there tis no room in it for anyone who will not obey the voice of authority. THE CATHOLIC NATIONS. "Why are Catholic nations such as Spain and Italy less prosperous than Protestant nations like England and Germany?" was the subject of the Rev. Hicks Gower on Monday. His idea, he said, of prosperity in a country was not the presence of millionaires and wealthy capitalists, but the greatest happiness and the greatest number of the inhabitants. Judged by that, England was not the most pros- perous country in the world, neither was Ger- many.religionThe In Protestant England the poor numbered one in every 31, in Catholic Belgium there was only one 0)7 ole2t Lint' poorprosperity criterion 1 was a two-edged sword. If they took as the criterion of religious ru t tl prosperity they would have to admit that not onl ), Y 1 rotestantism, but Paganism and Catholicism were true. The fact of the matter was that prosperity ing to do necessarily With religion, but WEIS brought about by other causes outside its scope.

UVtIL LIMITED. Year's Sales Eclipse All Pre\ loos Records.

, SHACKLETON'S OPINION. The annual general meeting of Bovril, Limited, was held lust week at River Plate House E C , . .

I 'rite Earl of Erroll presided, and in moving the adoption of the report, said that the saki Of the year. were far ahead of all previous re- cords—an increase of more than .1270,000 in the last three years. The sales for 1914 were more than double those for the same period last year. 'The increase alone represented an additional sale of considerably more than , 1,250,000 bottles of novril in the first five weeks of the year._

So great _ had been the inrease in the con- sumption in recent years that even the resources of the vast Bovril cattle farms had been heavily taxed to keep pace with the demand. Thus, quite apart from any annual surplus of cattle on, for instance, the Argentine estates of Bovril, it was necessary to select and buy cattle from the farmers for hundreds of miles round the factory, either for immediate use or as store cattle to fatten for the following season. The buying of additional cattle had had to be clomp in the face of a growing world demand for beef, which had so raised prices that the local ranchers wanted nearly twice as much for their, stock as they did two or three years ago, anj, it was this all-important factor that had in- , creased the cost of Bovril and rendered ,neces- sary an advance in their selling prices, which' came into force on January Ist last.

_ - Sir Ernest Shackleton, who sails in August next to make his journey across the.Antarctie, had written to his agent as follows: "I con- sider the question of the concentrated beef supply is most important—it must be. Bovril." In view of the unmatched experience of Sir Ernest Shackleton, who had been with, or asso- ciated in equipment of, no fewer than five im- portant Polar expeditions, this was a high testi- monial. Sir Ernest would take with him large supplies of Virol, .the warmth-giving qualities of which were well known to him.

PUBLIC AMUSEMENTS.

FINE VARIETIES AT HIPPOEIROME. Large houses have been attracted to the Hip- podrome during the week by the excellence of the variety turns appearing. The popul a Hebrew ragtime comedian, Dave Samuels, leads the way in that line and scores a decided success. In a novel shooting and equilibristic act some daring feats are performed by the Yukitos, which are loudly applauded. Brierle3r and Fenton make a strong appeal in "The Flower Girl and the Newsboy," and sing re- markably well. "The Five Joy Belles" cause great amusement.

Next Week's Programme. A good bill tor next week is headed by the Five Clittons in a gymnastic and equilibristie act. Sydney Lynn and Hastings Lynn will pre- sent "The Awakening." Other turns will be: Bertram Banks, child. mimic; Bert Earle, jumper and dancer; The Four Bachelors, vocal- ists and entertainers; Zakaree Ermakoo, in firearms' display; and Billy Hobbs, coon im- personator and dancer.

"THE QUEEN-MOTHER" AT ROYAL COURT. " The Queen-Mother," a stirring.' military play, with. a Spice of romance, has proved a successful attraction at the Royal Court Theatre during the week. The numerous exciting situ- ations are well worked, the plot is deep, and the various characters in this gripping produc- tion are realistically portrayed. 3fis3 Mary Fulton appears with success in the title-role.

STYLE IN WALKING. Dwellers in cities and residents of the coun- try have a different gait in walking, according ito Dr. Felix Regnault, who publishes n "Arch- ives de Physiologie" an exhaustive study of walking. The city man uses short steps, holds his body erect, keeps his legs straight, .and strikes the pavement sharply with his heels before putting down the rest of the foot. The country man takes a longer stride, leans for- ward, keeps his knees bent, slides his foot above the ground, and leans his weight upon the whole sole at once. Each of these gaits has its merits. That of the country man enables him to cover longer distances without fatigue if the road be fairly even; but when this is rough or broken he inAtinctively adopts the mode of the city man. Pace has much to do with gait. The average man can cover about three miles and a half an hour without. fatigue. Natural walking is one of the finest exercises not for the legs only, but also for the whole body, especially for the in- ternal organs of the abdomen.
